Convergence of Normal Form Power Series for Infinite-Dimensional Lie Pseudo-Group Actions


Abstract
We prove the convergence of normal form power series for suitably nonsingular analytic submanifolds under a broad class of infinite-dimensional Lie pseudo-group actions. Our theorem is illustrated by a number of examples, and includes, as a particular case, Chern and Moser’s celebrated convergence theorem for normal forms of real hypersurfaces. The construction of normal forms relies on the equivariant moving frame method, while the convergence proof is based on the realization that the normal form can be recovered as part of the solution to an initial value problem for an involutive system of differential equations, whose analyticity is guaranteed by the Cartan–Kähler Theorem.



Peter J. Olver
\dagger

Masoud Sabzevari
\ddagger

Francis Valiquette
\star

Keywords
: Involutive system of differential equations, Lie pseudo-group, moving frame, normal form power series, differential invariant, chain.

2020 Mathematics subject classification
: 22F05, 53A55, 58K50

Contents

1
Introduction.

In general, a
normal form
, also known as a
canonical form
, is defined as a simple representative element chosen from an equivalence class of objects. The identification of a normal form serves to simplify the treatment of such objects, and also solves the equivalence problem; namely, two objects are equivalent if and only if they have the same normal form. A simple algebraic example is the Jordan canonical form, which represents the similarity class of a square matrix,
[
61
]
. In dynamical systems,
[
48
,
49
]
, normal forms are extensively used to study dynamics, including bifurcations, classification of singular points, and determining the behavior of solutions.

In the present paper, we focus on the problem of constructing normal forms of analytic
p
-dimensional submanifolds under the action of a Lie pseudo-group, which includes the case of Lie group actions. Such problems arise in a wide range of applications, including classical invariant theory,
[
52
]
, ordinary differential equations,
[
18
,
31
,
65
]
, partial differential equations,
[
8
,
47
]
, differential operators,
[
33
]
, the calculus of variations,
[
32
,
34
,
36
]
, control theory,
[
23
]
, nonholonomic geometry,
[
16
]
, image processing,
[
5
,
9
,
28
]
, and many more. Normal forms can be algorithmically constructed using the method of equivariant moving frames,
[
19
,
44
,
55
,
57
]
, which produces formal power series whose non-constant Taylor coefficients provide a complete set of independent differential invariants of the pseudo-group action on submanifolds. Our main result is a theorem guaranteeing the convergence of such formal power series under rather general conditions on the Lie pseudo-group action in the infinite-dimensional case — since convergence in the case of finite-dimensional Lie group actions is immediate — and on the cross-section used for the moving frame construction.

Our results were inspired by the seminal paper of Chern and Moser,
[
13
]
, that constructed normal form power series for nonsingular analytic real hypersurfaces in complex manifolds, and then proved their convergence. This particular equivalence problem was first studied by Poincaré in
[
62
]
, who gave two heuristic counting arguments that indicated that two real hypersurfaces in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
are, in general, biholomorphically inequivalent, and raised the question of finding the invariants that distinguish them. This question was then solved in the two-dimensional case by Cartan,
[
10
]
, and, subsequently, in higher dimensions by Chern and Moser,
[
13
]
. Their analysis was based on an ingenious combination of Cartan’s equivalence method and an innovative convergence proof, based on the method of chains that they introduced, which relies on the observation that the successive transformations mapping a regular hypersurface to its normal form can be characterized as solutions to ordinary differential equations, and are therefore analytic. On the other hand, Kolář,
[
37
]
, produced examples of singular hypersurfaces whose normal form power series are divergent, thus indicating the subtlety of the convergence issue. The normal form analysis promoted by Chern and Moser has inspired many developments in CR geometry,
[
4
,
41
,
38
,
69
]
, and has also been applied to differential equations,
[
21
,
40
,
46
,
64
]
, control systems,
[
63
]
, and the geometry of submanifolds,
[
6
,
14
,
26
,
27
,
55
]
. In the authors’ previous paper
[
60
]
, Chern and Moser’s analysis was extended to construct normal form power series for singular hypersurfaces by applying the equivariant method of moving frames for Lie pseudo-groups,
[
57
]
. However, the convergence of the resulting power series continued to rely on the Chern–Moser chain-based arguments that only apply to a limited range of problems; see, for instance,
[
17
,
21
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
45
]
. The present paper grew out of our inability at the time to provide an independent proof of convergence.

The aim of this paper is to establish a new theorem establishing convergence of normal form power series, that applies in great generality, and includes Chern and Moser’s convergence theorem. Our result is based on characterizing the normal form as part of the solution to an initial value problem for a certain involutive system of partial differential equations, whose solutions are analytic as a consequence of the Cartan–Kähler existence theorem. Since the theory of involutive differential equations is at the heart of our proof, Sections
2
and
3
summarize the general theory, as developed in
[
20
,
66
]
.
Note
: We will use a purely partial differential equation version of the Cartan–Kähler theorem, which circumvents all the differential form constructions that appear in most other treatments, e.g.,
[
7
,
51
]
.

There are four key steps in our argument. The first is to recall in Section
4
that every analytic Lie pseudo-group is characterized by an involutive system of partial differential equations known as its determining equations,
[
56
,
67
]
. This means that every local diffeomorphism belonging to the pseudo-group is a solution to the determining equations and conversely. A new contribution of the paper is the formulation in Section
5.1
of a system of partial differential equations that are satisfied by the restriction of the pseudo-group transformations to a prescribed
p
-dimensional submanifold, which we call the
reduced determining equations
. A pseudo-group is called
reducible
if there is a one-to-one correspondence between the solutions of the pseudo-group determining equations and its reduced determining system; see Definition
5.5
for the precise statement. In Theorem
6.5
we show that every Lie pseudo-group that eventually acts freely on an open subset of the submanifold jets space is reducible; such pseudo-groups are exactly those for which the method of equivariant moving frames can be applied,
[
57
,
59
]
. Using the Cartan–Kuranishi Prolongation Theorem,
[
66
]
, we show in Theorem
5.12
that the reduced determining equations of a reducible pseudo-group remain involutive and that furthermore, the Cartan characters of the reduced determining equations are equal to the first
p
Cartan characters of the pseudo-group determining system.

The next step, carried out in Section
7.1
, is to rewrite the reduced determining equations in an equivalent form which have the property that part of their solution, to a specified initial value problem, is the normal form of the submanifold upon which we based the reduction. The resulting equations are therefore called the
normal form determining equations
. Since the rewriting amounts to a simple algebraic change of variables, involutivity of the normal form determining equations is assured. The final step is to apply the method of equivariant moving frames described in Section
6.2
to prescribe formally well-posed initial conditions, cf.
[
66
, Section 9]
, for the normal form determining equations. These initial conditions are given by what we call a
well-posed cross-section
that serves to define the moving frame. The well-posedness condition on the cross-section is a refinement of the notion of algebraic cross-section that was introduced in
[
58
]
, the key difference being that a well-posed cross-section is determined with respect to a Pommaret basis, while an algebraic cross-section relies on a Gröbner basis construction. To accomplish this, we show in Section
8
that once the reduced pseudo-group action becomes free at order
n_{f}
, the moving frame construction, and thus the prescribed initial conditions, is compatible with the involutivity of the normal form determining equations starting at order
n_{f}+1
. In Theorem
8.17
, a simple algebraic test, based on the existence of a Rees decomposition for the cross-section at order
\geq n_{f}+1
is established, which guarantees that a cross-section of minimal order will be well-posed. Given well-posed initial conditions, an application of the Cartan–Kähler theorem immediately implies that the solution to the initial value problem for the normal form determining equations is analytic, which, in particular, yields the analyticity and hence convergence of the so-constructed normal form. This leads to the main result of the paper, which is stated in Theorem
8.21
, that a well-posed cross-section to the prolonged pseudo-group action whose cross-section-based normalization constants define analytic functions determines a convergent normal form power series for any reducible analytic submanifold.

To the best of our knowledge, our convergence theorem provides the most general result available in the literature, which can be applied to an extremely broad range of Lie pseudo-group actions. All related works on the subject, e.g.,
[
17
,
21
,
38
,
39
,
40
]
, prove the convergence of normal form power series within a specific context. In CR geometry, one of the most general results recently appeared in the work of Lamel and Stolovitch,
[
41
]
, who proved convergence of normal form power series for a class of nondegenerate CR submanifolds subject to certain constraints on the normal form.
Moreover, as we argue in Section
9
, our main theorem sheds new light on, and generalizations of Chern and Moser’s notion of chains that they used to prove the convergence of the normal forms constructed within their paper,
[
13
]
; see also
[
17
,
45
]
.

The equivariant approach to moving frames developed in
[
19
,
44
,
57
]
, which underlies the final stage of our construction, is a generalization of the classical method,
[
11
,
15
,
24
]
, that can be systematically and algorithmically applied to general Lie group actions as well as a wide range of Lie pseudo-groups. In
[
55
]
, the equivariant moving frame construction was reinterpreted as the specification of a normal form for a submanifold under a pseudo-group action, and
[
57
]
ended with two explicit examples for relatively simple infinite-dimensional Lie pseudo-group actions. Being concerned with the algebraic formulation of the method, the resulting power series were only formal, and the question of convergence was not considered. We remark that the implementation employed here differs from the original version introduced in
[
19
]
for general Lie group actions and
[
57
,
58
]
for infinite-dimensional Lie pseudo-groups, in that it is based on the action of the reduced pseudo-group instead of the original pseudo-group. That said, both implementations yield exactly the same differential invariants, invariant differential operators, etc. Finally, we note that Arnaldsson
[
2
,
3
]
has recently combined the equivariant moving frames with Cartan’s equivalence method for solving equivalence problems, basing his method on involutive bases for polynomial ideals.

Remark 1.1
.

Our results are illustrated by a running example that was considered in the original moving frame papers
[
56
,
57
,
58
]
. In Section
10
we present a number of further examples illustrating our methods and results, including revisiting the Chern–Moser example of nonsingular real hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Applications to additional and more substantial Lie pseudo-group actions will be the subject of subsequent papers.

2
Jet Bundles and Differential Equations.

In this section we review the standard geometric language of jet spaces for studying systems of differential equations, and present the basic operations of prolongation and projection. While many of our initial considerations hold in more general contexts, we work in the analytic category throughout since we will ultimately rely on the Cartan–Kähler Theorem to prove the convergence of normal form power series.

2.1
Jet Bundles.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an analytic
p
-dimensional manifold, and
\pi\colon M\to\mathcal{X}
an analytic fiber bundle with
q
-dimensional fibers. Locally, the total space
M
is isomorphic to the Cartesian product
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\mkern 1.0mu^{p}\times{\mathbb{R}}\mkern 1.0mu^{q}
; since all considerations are local, we do not lose any generality by working in the latter context. Accordingly, we introduce the local coordinates
z=(x,u)\in M
with
x=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p})\in\mathcal{X}
parametrizing the base space, and so will play the role of independent variables, while
u=(u^{1},\ldots,u^{q})\in\mathcal{U}
parametrize the fibers, and will play the role of dependent variables in our system of differential equations. In the following, we let
m=p+q
denote the dimension of the total space
M
.

In general, given two analytic manifolds, say
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{U}
, and an integer
0\leq n<\infty
, we let
{\rm J}{}^{n}={\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U})
denote the
n
-th order
jet space
, whose points (jets) represent equivalence classes of locally defined functions
u\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{U}
up to
n
-th order contact, or, equivalently, possessing the same order
n
Taylor series at the base point
x
,
[
51
]
. In particular
{\rm J}{}^{0}={\rm J}{}^{0}M=M
. In the above framework, we can identify such functions with local sections of
M\to\mathcal{X}
, and
{\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U})\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}M
is an open subset (coordinate chart) of the jet bundle
{\rm J}{}^{n}M
of sections of the fiber bundle. Even more generally, the graphs of sections form
p
-dimensional submanifolds of
M
that are transverse to the fibers, and thus
{\rm J}{}^{n}M\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}(M,p)
is an open dense submanifold of the (extended) submanifold jet bundle,
[
50
]
. However, since all our considerations are local, we can concentrate on
{\rm J}{}^{n}={\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U})
throughout. For any
0\leq k<n
, we have the jet projection

\pi^{n}_{k}\colon{\rm J}{}^{n}\to{\rm J}{}^{k},

(2.1)

together with the base projection

\pi^{n}\colon{\rm J}{}^{n}\to\mathcal{X}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\pi^{n}=\pi\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\,\scriptstyle\circ\,$}\pi^{n}_{0}.

The induced coordinates on the
n
-th order jet space
{\rm J}{}^{n}\simeq\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
are written as
z^{(n)}=(x,u^{(n)})
where
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
u^{(n)}\in\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
. Separating the jet coordinates by order,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{(n)}=\mathcal{U}^{0}\times\mathcal{U}^{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{U}^{n},

\displaystyle 0\leq n<\infty,

(2.2)

where

\mathcal{U}^{k}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(\ \ldots\ u^{\alpha}_{J}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;|J|=k,\ \alpha=1,\ldots,q\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(\ \ldots\ u^{\alpha}_{J}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|J|=k,\ \alpha=1,\ldots,q\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(\ \ldots\ u^{\alpha}_{J}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|J|=k,\ \alpha=1,\ldots,q\hbox{}\}}{\{(\ \ldots\ u^{\alpha}_{J}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|J|=k,\ \alpha=1,\ldots,q\hbox{}\}},\hskip 20.00003pt0\leq k\leq n,

(2.3)

denotes the space coordinatized by all
k
-th order derivatives of the dependent variables, which has dimension

\mathsf{t}_{k}=\dim\,\mathcal{U}^{k}=q\,\binom{p+k-1}{k}.

(2.4)

Throughout the paper we use symmetric multi-index notation for derivatives. Thus, a symmetric multi-index
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k})
, with
1\leq j_{\nu}\leq p
, corresponds to the
k
-th order derivative
\partial_{J}=\partial^{k}/\partial x^{j_{1}}\cdots\partial x^{j_{k}}
, and the jet coordinate
u^{\alpha}_{J}
represents the
J
-th derivative of
u^{\alpha}(x)
at the base point
x
.
Thus, to each jet coordinate
u^{\alpha}_{J}
, we assign the multi-index
(\alpha;J)
. We introduce the space of all multi-indices

\mathcal{I}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq 0\hbox{}\}},

(2.5)

along with the subset of indices

\mathcal{I}^{\geq n}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq n\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq n\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq n\hbox{}\}}{\{(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq\alpha\leq q\text{ and }|J|\geq n\hbox{}\}}

(2.6)

of order
\geq n
. We also use the concatenation notation
J,i=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k},i)
to denote the symmetric multi-index obtained by appending
i
to
J
. Inversely, we use
J\setminus k
to denote the multi-index obtained by removing
k\in J
from
J
.

As noted above, we can identify finite order jets of sections with Taylor polynomials. Explicitly, for
0\leq n<\infty
, we identify a jet
z^{(n)}=(x,u^{(n)})\in{\rm J}{}^{n}
with the
q
-tuple of polynomials of degrees
\leq n
whose entries are

\displaystyle P^{\alpha}_{n}(y)=\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\>\frac{u^{\alpha}_{J}}{J\mskip 2.0mu!}\,(y-x)^{J},

\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q.

(2.7)

If
(x,u^{(n)})
is the
n
-jet of a section
u(x)
, so
u^{\alpha}_{J}
represents the
J
-th partial derivative of its component
u^{\alpha}(x)
at
x
, then
P^{\alpha}_{n}(y)
is the corresponding Taylor polynomial of degree
n
based at the point
x
.

There are two inequivalent ways to define the infinite order jet bundle. The usual method is to define
{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
as the projective (or inverse) limit of the finite order jet bundles
{\rm J}{}^{n}
under the projection maps (
2.1
). Thus, an infinite jet has local coordinates
x^{i},u^{\alpha}_{J}
for all
i=1,\ldots,p
,
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
, and all multi-indices
|J|\geq 0
. We identify such an infinite jet with a
q
-tuple of formal power series:

\displaystyle P^{\alpha}(y)=\sum_{|J|\geq 0}\>\frac{u^{\alpha}_{J}}{J\mskip 2.0mu!}\,(y-x)^{J},

\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q.

(2.8)

Since the coefficients
u^{\alpha}_{J}
are arbitrary, there is no guarantee that (
2.8
) converges.

An alternative approach is, in analogy with the finite order case, to define infinite jets as equivalence classes of sections up to infinite order contact, which is equivalent to the condition that their Taylor series (
2.8
) agree at the base point. Since we restrict to analytic sections, the corresponding Taylor series converge and, indeed, uniquely determine the section. Since the coefficients
u^{\alpha}_{J}
must now define a convergent series, with a non-zero radius of convergence, they are no longer allowed to be arbitrary. Thus, the result of the latter construction is a subbundle
\rm A^{\infty}\subset{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
of the preceding infinite jet bundle, which consists of infinite jets that produce convergent Taylor series, as in (
2.8
). We will call
\rm A^{\infty}
the
analytic infinite jet bundle
.

Traditionally, the equivariant moving frame calculus takes place in the ordinary infinite jet bundle
{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
, without regard to convergence. The goal of this paper is to provide conditions, on both the pseudo-group action and the cross-section defining the normalizations, that guarantee that the normal form determined by the moving frame normalizations belongs to the analytic infinite jet bundle
\rm A^{\infty}
.

2.2
Differential Equations.

A system of
n
-th order
differential equations
is given by a system of equations

\Delta(x,u^{(n)})=\bigl(\,{\Delta_{1}(x,u^{(n)}),\ldots,\Delta_{l}(x,u^{(n)})}\,\bigr)=0

(2.9)

involving the
n
-th order jet space coordinates. To avoid singularities, the defining functions
\Delta\colon{\rm J}{}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
are assumed to be analytic, the corresponding subvariety

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(x,u^{(n)})\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;\Delta(x,u^{(n)})=0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(x,u^{(n)})\hbox{}\,|\,\Delta(x,u^{(n)})=0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(x,u^{(n)})\hbox{}\,|\,\Delta(x,u^{(n)})=0\hbox{}\}}{\{(x,u^{(n)})\hbox{}\,|\,\Delta(x,u^{(n)})=0\hbox{}\}}\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}

(2.10)

is assumed to form an analytic fibered submanifold of the fiber bundle
\pi^{n}\colon{\rm J}{}^{n}\to\mathcal{X}
, and the Jacobian matrix of the defining functions is of maximal rank on
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
; see
[
50
]
.

Prolongation
and
projection
are two natural operations on differential equations. The former lifts the system of differential equations to higher orders by differentiation, while the latter lowers the order by keeping only the equations (if any) of a specified lower order. The prolongation of (
2.9
) to order
n+k
is the fibered submanifold
\mathcal{R}^{(n+k)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{n+k}
locally described by the system of equations

\mathcal{R}^{(n+k)}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta_{\nu}(x,u^{(n)})&=0,&&1\leq\nu\leq l\\
\mathrm{D}_{x}^{J}\Delta_{\nu}(x,u^{(n)})&=0,&&1\leq|J|\leq k\end{aligned}\right\},

where

\mathrm{D}_{x^{i}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\sum_{|J|\geq 0}u^{\alpha}_{J,i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}},\hskip 20.00003pti=1,\ldots,p,

(2.11)

are the usual total derivative operators, which mutually commute, and

\mathrm{D}_{x}^{J}=\mathrm{D}_{x^{j_{1}}}\cdots\mathrm{D}_{x^{j_{k}}}
, for
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k})
a symmetric multi-index, are their higher order iterations. On the other hand, the projection of the
n
-th order differential equation
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
to a differential equation of order
n-k
, with
0<k\leq n
, which encodes the relations (if any) among derivatives of order
\leq n-k
, is given by

\pi^{n}_{n-k}(\mathcal{R}^{(n)})\subseteq{\rm J}{}^{n-k}.

To construct a local representation of
\pi^{n}_{n-k}(\mathcal{R}^{(n)})
one starts with (
2.9
) and eliminates, using only algebraic operations, all derivatives of order greater than
n-k
in as many equations as possible. If there are no equations of order
\leq n-k
, then, at least locally,
\pi^{n}_{n-k}(\mathcal{R}^{(n)})={\rm J}{}^{n-k}
. As in
[
66
]
, we assume that the systems of differential equations are
regular
so that, to avoid dealing with singular points and subsets, all projections and prolongations are assumed to be fibered submanifolds.

The
k
-th prolongation and projection of a system of differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
is given by

\pi_{n}^{n+k}(\mathcal{R}^{(n+k)})\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{(n)}.

This process may not return the original system
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
due to the existence of integrability conditions. A system of differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
is said to be
formally integrable
if for all
k\geq 0
, the equality

\pi^{n+k+1}_{n+k}(\mathcal{R}^{(n+k+1)})=\mathcal{R}^{(n+k)}

(2.12)

holds. In other words, a system of differential equations is formally integrable if, no matter the order at which the system is prolonged, no additional integrability conditions arise.

3
Involutivity.

Formal integrability does not in itself suffice to guarantee the existence of solutions to a system of differential equations, and, for this purpose, we need to introduce the notion of involutivity. To this end, we summarize the theory of involutive systems of partial differential equations, in the form presented by Seiler in his book
[
66
]
; see also
[
20
]
. In particular, we will
not
use the exterior differential systems formulation of involutivity,
[
7
,
51
]
.

We begin with the linearization of a system of partial differential equations (
2.10
).
Consider the tangent bundle
T{\rm J}{}^{n}\to{\rm J}{}^{n}
parametrized by
(x,u^{(n)},\xi,\psi^{(n)})
, where
\xi^{i},\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
are the fiber coordinates. Any vector field, i.e., section of
T{\rm J}{}^{n}
,
is locally represented by

\mathbf{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>\xi^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\>\psi^{\alpha}_{J}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}},

whose coefficients
\xi^{i},\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
depend on
1
1

1

We will often suppress the dependence on
z^{(n)}
to avoid cluttering formulas.

z^{(n)}=(x,u^{(n)})
.
In view of (
2.2
), we introduce the vertical (fiber) projection

\pi_{V}\colon T{\rm J}{}^{n}|_{z^{(n)}}\to T\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{U}^{(n)}|_{z^{(n)}}

given by removing the horizontal component:

\pi_{V}(\mathbf{v})=\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\>\psi^{\alpha}_{J}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}.

The (
vertical
)
linearization

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}|_{z^{(n)}}\subset T\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{U}^{(n)}|_{z^{(n)}}
of the system of differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
given by (
2.9
) at a point
z^{(n)}\in\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
consists of the system of linear equations

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}=\pi_{V}(\mathbf{v})\,\Delta=\bigg\{\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\>\frac{\partial\Delta_{\nu}}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}\psi^{\alpha}_{J}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nu=1,\ldots,l\ \bigg\}.

(3.1)

We further introduce the
highest order term map

\textbf{H}\colon T\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{U}^{(n)}|_{z^{(n)}}\to T\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{U}^{n}|_{z^{(n)}}
which only retains the terms
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|=n
in (
3.1
). The resulting system of linear equations

\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}=\textbf{H}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)})=\bigg\{\displaystyle\sum_{|J|=n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\>\frac{\partial\Delta_{\nu}}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}\,\psi^{\alpha}_{J}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nu=1,\ldots,l\ \bigg\}

is called the
symbol
of the differential equation
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
. Its
\displaystyle l\times q\,\binom{p+n-1}{p-1}
coefficient matrix

M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Delta_{\nu}}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}\end{pmatrix}

is called the
n
-th order symbol matrix
. In line with the standard regularity assumption, we suppose in the following that all intrinsic algebraic properties of the symbol, e.g., its rank, are independent of the point
z^{(n)}\in\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
under consideration.

The columns of the symbol matrix
M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
correspond to the unknowns
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|=n
. In order to formulate the involutivity and solvability of the system of partial differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
, we need to order the columns in an intelligent manner; our preferred ordering will be prescribed by the notion of the class of a multi-index, which relies on a choice of ordering of the independent variables. For general arguments, we use the natural ordering
x^{1}\prec x^{2}\prec\cdots\prec x^{p}
throughout. With this choice, the definition of class is as follows.

Definition 3.1
.

The
class
of a multi-index
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k})
is the smallest index that appears in
J
, so

\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J=\min\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\}.

Note that, in the set
\mathcal{U}^{k}
of jet coordinates
u^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|=k
, there are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{t}_{k}^{(i)}}=q\,\binom{p+k-i-1}{k-1}

(3.2)

jet coordinates whose multi-index
J
is of class
i
. Thus,

\mathsf{t}_{k}={\displaystyle\mathsf{t}_{k}^{(1)}}+\ \cdots\ +{\displaystyle\mathsf{t}_{k}^{(p)}}

(3.3)

is the total number of jet coordinates of order exactly
k
, as in (
2.3
).

We sort the columns of the symbol matrix
M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
using a class-respecting term ordering so that if
\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J>\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits K
, then the column corresponding to the unknown
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
must be to the
left
of the column corresponding to the unknown
\psi^{\beta}_{K}
. Within a fixed class, one is free to choose any convenient ordering of the columns. For example, if
p=3
and we order
x\prec y\prec z
, then one possible ordering of the order
n=2
columns of a symbol matrix is
\psi_{zz},\psi_{yz},\psi_{yy},\psi_{xz},\psi_{xy},\psi_{xx}
, so the first column has class 3, the next two, which can be switched, have class 2, and the final three, again in any order, are of class 1.

With this column ordering, let
M_{\mathcal{R},\text{REF}}^{n}
be the row reduction of
M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
to its row-echelon form, cf.
[
61
]
. Abn unknown
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
that corresponds to the first non-vanishing entry of a row in
M_{\mathcal{R},\text{REF}}^{n}
, i.e., the row’s pivot entry, is called the
leader
of the row. We will use
\mathsf{r}_{n}
to denote the rank of the symbol matrix
M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
, i.e., the number of leaders/pivots.

The jet coordinates
u^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|=n
that correspond to the leader columns of the symbol matrix
M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
are known as
principal derivatives
. It follows that the number of principal derivatives of order
n
is

\mathsf{r}_{n}=\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n},

which also equals the number of independent differential equations of order
n
in the system.
The other jet coordinates of order
n
corresponding to the non-pivot columns are known as
parametric derivatives
. The number of parametric derivatives of order
n
is given by

\mathsf{d}_{n}=\mathsf{t}_{n}-\mathsf{r}_{n}.

(3.4)

We let

\mathsf{r}^{(n)}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\>\mathsf{r}_{k}

(3.5)

denote the total number of principal derivatives of order
\leq n
, and

\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=q\,\binom{p+n}{n}-\mathsf{r}^{(n)}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\>\mathsf{d}_{k}

(3.6)

the total number of parametric derivatives of order
\leq n
. By the Implicit Function Theorem and our regularity assumptions,
\mathsf{d}^{(n)}
equals the fiber dimension of the
n
-th order system (
2.10
).

An
n
-th order system of partial differential equations is said to be in
Cartan normal form
if all its symbol matrices of order
0\leq k\leq n
are either empty or in reduced row-echelon form with respect to the above class-respecting ordering of the columns. We further say that it is in
reduced Cartan normal form
if, in addition, the entire symbol matrix

M_{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}=\begin{pmatrix}M_{\mathcal{R}}^{0}&M_{\mathcal{R}}^{1}&\cdots&M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}\end{pmatrix}^{T}

is in reduced row-echelon form,
[
20
]
, meaning that the entries in the column above the pivots are all
0
. Thus, the differential equations are in reduced Cartan normal form when they take the form

u^{\alpha}_{J}=\Delta^{\alpha}_{J}(x,\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots),

(3.7)

where
u^{\alpha}_{J}
are the principal derivatives, and all the jet coordinates
u^{\beta}_{K}
appearing on the right hand side are parametric and are indexed by the columns that have
nonzero
entries in the corresponding row of the reduced row echelon form of the entire symbol matrix. At order
|K|=|J|
, these are all parametric derivatives that appear after
u^{\alpha}_{J}
in the class-respecting term ordering, that is
\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits K\leq\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J
. Thanks to the Implicit Function Theorem, any regular system of differential equations of order
n
can be placed in reduced Cartan normal form.

Definition 3.2
.

The number of leaders of class
1\leq k\leq p
in the row-echelon symbol matrix
M_{\mathcal{R},\text{REF}}^{n}
is denoted by
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(k)}}
. The resulting nonnegative integers
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(1)}},\ldots,{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(p)}}
are called the
indices
of the
n
-th order symbol
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
.

We are now able to state the key definition of an involutive symbol.

Definition 3.3
.

The symbol
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
with indices
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(k)}}
is said to be
involutive
if the symbol matrix
M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+1}
of the prolonged symbol
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+1}
satisfies
2
2

2

The first equation is automatically satisfied as a consequence of the definition of the indices
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(k)}}
, and is included for later referencing.

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{p}\,{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(k)}}=\mathsf{r}_{n},

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{p}\,k\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(k)}}=\mathsf{r}_{n+1}=\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits M_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+1}.

(3.8)

Remark 3.4
.

We observe that the class of a derivative is not necessarily preserved under coordinate transformations. The notion of a
\delta
-regular coordinate chart
is characterized by the fact that the sum on the right hand side of (
3.8
) takes its maximal value under all possible (linear) changes of coordinates. In particular, a necessary condition for
\delta
-regularity is that the highest index
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(p)}}
takes its maximal value. For a first order system of differential equations, this means that a maximal number of equations must be solvable for an
x^{p}
-derivative, and hence the surface
x^{p}=0
cannot be characteristic.

Clearly, the involutivity condition (
3.8
) requires that we work in a
\delta
-regular coordinate system. Indeed, we will assume throughout that we are always working in
\delta
-regular coordinates, noting that generic coordinate systems are
\delta
-regular,
[
25
,
66
]
. However, in Examples
10.1
and
10.5
, the most natural coordinate system is not
\delta
-regular, and so the involutivity criterion (
3.8
) is not satisfied unless we impose a suitable change of variables before conducting the analysis.

Definition 3.5
.

A system of differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
is
involutive
if it is formally integrable and its symbol
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
is involutive.

Recall that formal integrability requires verifying (
2.12
) for
all

k\geq 0
. But when the symbol is involutive, it suffices to check integrability only for
k=0
. A proof of this result, as stated below, can be found in
[
66
]
.

Theorem 3.6
.

A system of differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
is involutive if and only if its symbol
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
is involutive and
\pi^{n+1}_{n}(\mathcal{R}^{(n+1)})=\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
.

Thus, to check involutivity at order
n
, one needs to make sure that the coordinate chart is
\delta
-regular, then verify the algebraic involutivity condition (
3.8
) for the indices of order
n
, and finally check that there are no integrability conditions at order
n+1
.

The indices
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(k)}}
determine the number of principal derivatives of order
n
and of class
k
in the system of differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
. On the other hand, the number of parametric derivatives of order
n
and class
k
is given by the
Cartan character

{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(k)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{t}_{n}^{(k)}}-{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(k)}},\hskip 20.00003pt1\leq k\leq p.

(3.9)

The involutivity condition (
3.8
) can be restated in terms of the Cartan characters as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{p}\>{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(k)}}=\mathsf{d}_{n},

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{p}\>k\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(k)}}=\mathsf{d}_{n+1}.

(3.10)

We also note that, according to
[
66
, Proposition 8.2.2]
, involutivity implies that the Cartan characters are non-increasing:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(1)}}\geq{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(2)}}\geq\ \cdots\ \geq{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(p)}}\geq 0.

(3.11)

Remark 3.7
.

Owing to their direct relationship (
3.9
), when formulating results or illustrative examples, one can work either just with the indices or just with the Cartan characters, depending upon one’s preference. We have chosen to display both in order to suit readers of either persuasion.

Remark 3.8
.

If
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(k)}}>{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(k+1)}}=0

is the last nonzero Cartan character of an involutive system of differential equations, then the general solution to the system depends on
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(k)}}
arbitrary functions of
k
variables, which can be identified with the initial conditions of order
k
. On the other hand, the number of arbitrary functions of less than
k
variables required to express a general solution is not well-defined; see also
[
7
,
12
,
51
,
66
]
.

Any
n
-th order system of differential equations (
2.9
) can be written as a first-order system of differential equations by setting the jet coordinates
u^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|\leq n-1
to be new dependent variables. To write down this new system, we introduce the differentiation notation

\partial_{i}u^{\alpha}_{J}=\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}{\partial x^{i}}.

Then a first order representation
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}
of the
n
-th order system
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
is given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\widetilde{\Delta}_{\nu}(x,(u^{(n-1)})^{(1)})=0,\qquad&&1\leq\nu\leq l\\
&\partial_{i}u^{\alpha}_{J}=u^{\alpha}_{J,i},&&|J|<n-1,\quad 1\leq i\leq p\\
\ &\partial_{i}u^{\alpha}_{J}=\partial_{k}u^{\alpha}_{J,i\setminus k},&&|J|=n-1,\quad k=\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J<i\leq p\ \end{aligned}\right\}.

(3.12)

The function
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\nu}
is not uniquely defined, as there are in general several possibilities to express a higher-order derivative
u^{\alpha}_{J}
in terms of the new coordinates. To easily compute the indices of the symbol
\Sigma_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{1}
of the first order system (
3.12
), we use the mapping

u^{\alpha}_{J}=\begin{cases}\ u^{\alpha}_{J},&|J|\leq n-1,\\
\ \partial_{k}u^{\alpha}_{J\setminus k},\quad&|J|=n,\quad\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J=k.\end{cases}

(3.13)

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
{\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{c}}_{1}^{(1)}},\ldots,{\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{c}}_{1}^{(p)}}
be the Cartan characters of the first order representation
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}
and
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(1)}},\ldots,{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(p)}}
those of the original system of differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
. Then

{\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{c}}_{1}^{(k)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(k)}},\hskip 20.00003pt1\leq k\leq p.

Moreover, the
n
-th order system
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
is involutive if and only if its first order representation
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}
is involutive.

The proof of Proposition
3.9
may be found in
[
66
, Appendix A.3]
. For a first-order system of involutive differential equations
\mathcal{R}^{(1)}
, the reduced Cartan normal form is

\displaystyle u^{\alpha}_{p}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\alpha}_{p}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{k},\ldots),

\displaystyle 1\leq\alpha\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(p)}},

(3.14)

\displaystyle u^{\alpha}_{p-1}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\alpha}_{p-1}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{k},\ldots),

\displaystyle 1\leq\alpha\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(p-1)}},

\displaystyle\vdots

\displaystyle u^{\alpha}_{1}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\alpha}_{1}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{k},\ldots),

\displaystyle 1\leq\alpha\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}},

\displaystyle u^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\alpha}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p},u^{\delta}),

\displaystyle 1\leq\alpha\leq\mathsf{b}_{0},

with, by virtue of involutivity
[
66
, Corollary 7.1.28]
, the indices satisfy

0\leq\mathsf{b}_{0}\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}\leq\cdots\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(p-1)}}\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(p)}}\leq q.

Moreover, all the derivatives appearing on the right hand side of each equation are parametric of class smaller than or equal to the class of the principal derivative occurring on the left hand side of the equation. If
\mathsf{b}_{0}=0
, the system does not contain algebraic equations relating the dependent variables. On the other hand, if
\mathsf{b}_{0}>0
, since the equations are in reduced Cartan normal form, no derivatives of order
0
or
1
of the principal zero-th order derivatives
u^{\alpha}
can appear on the right hand side of any of the equations.

Formally well-posed initial value conditions for the first-order system in reduced Cartan normal form (
3.14
)
are prescribed by

\displaystyle\hfil u^{\beta}(0,\ldots,0)\;

\displaystyle=f^{\beta},

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{0}<\beta\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}},

\displaystyle\hfil u^{\beta}(x^{1},0,\ldots,0)\;

\displaystyle=f^{\beta}(x^{1}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}<\beta\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}},

\displaystyle\ \vdots

\displaystyle\hfil u^{\beta}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p-1},0)\;

\displaystyle=f^{\beta}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p-1}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(p-1)}}<\beta\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(p)}},

\displaystyle\hfil u^{\beta}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p})\;

\displaystyle=f^{\beta}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(p)}}<\beta\leq q.

(3.15)

Remark 3.10
.

In (
3.15
), we use the convention that if, for example,
\mathsf{b}_{0}={\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}
,
then the first set of equations in the initial conditions (
3.15
) are vacuous, and similarly for the other sets.

As they should, the initial conditions (
3.15
) specify the parametric derivatives occurring on the right hand side of the system of differential equations (
3.14
). For example, the parametric derivatives of class 1 are determined by differentiating the equations
u^{\beta}(x^{1},0,\ldots,0)=f^{\beta}(x^{1})
for
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}<\beta\leq{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}
on the line
\{(x^{1},0,0,\ldots,0)\}
. The parametric derivatives of class 2 are obtained from the initial conditions on the plane
\{(x^{1},x^{2},0,\ldots,0)\}
, and so on.

Recalling our notation (
2.6
), the reduced Cartan normal form equations (
3.14
) and their infinite prolongation split the set of multi-indices

\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}=\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{prin}}\,\biguplus\,\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{par}}

(3.16)

into the disjoint subsets containing, respectively, the principal and the parametric multi-indices:

\displaystyle\hfil\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{prin}}\;

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;u^{\alpha}_{J}\text{ is a principal derivative}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,u^{\alpha}_{J}\text{ is a principal derivative}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,u^{\alpha}_{J}\text{ is a principal derivative}\hbox{}\}}{\{(\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,u^{\alpha}_{J}\text{ is a principal derivative}\hbox{}\}}

\displaystyle\hfil\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{par}}\;

\displaystyle=\{\,(\beta;K)\;|\;u^{\beta}_{K}\text{ is a parametric derivative}\,\}.

(3.17)

The
Pommaret division
assigns to the multi-index
(\alpha;J)
of class
\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J=k
the
multiplicative indices

\{1,\ldots,k\}
, which serve to define the
involutive
(
Pommaret
) cone

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(J)=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(\alpha;J,k^{1},\ldots,k^{n})\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;1\leq k^{j}\leq\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(J)\;\text{and}\;n\geq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(\alpha;J,k^{1},\ldots,k^{n})\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq k^{j}\leq\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(J)\;\text{and}\;n\geq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(\alpha;J,k^{1},\ldots,k^{n})\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq k^{j}\leq\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(J)\;\text{and}\;n\geq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{(\alpha;J,k^{1},\ldots,k^{n})\hbox{}\,|\,1\leq k^{j}\leq\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(J)\;\text{and}\;n\geq 0\hbox{}\}}.

(3.18)

The set of principal indices
\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{prin}}
forms an ideal in
\mathcal{I}
. Involutivity of the Cartan normal form equations (
3.14
) implies that this ideal can be decomposed into a union of non-intersecting involutive cones

\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{prin}}=\biguplus_{i=1}^{p}\;\biguplus_{\alpha=1}^{\mathsf{\scriptstyle b}_{1}^{(i)}}\;\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(i).

(3.19)

The decomposition (
3.19
) reflects the fact that the prolongation of the system of equations (
3.14
) can be obtained by differentiating each equation solely with respect to the multiplicative indices of the principal derivative. The indices
(\alpha;i)
associated to the principal derivatives in (
3.14
) are said to form a
Pommaret basis
of
\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{prin}}
.

On the other hand, the set of parametric indices
\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{par}}
does not form an ideal. That said it still admits a disjoint decomposition into involutive cones

\mathcal{I}^{\geq 1}_{\mathcal{R},\text{par}}=\biguplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\\[1.9919pt]
i=1\end{subarray}}^{p}\;\biguplus_{\beta=\mathsf{\scriptstyle b}_{1}^{(i)}+1}^{q}\;\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(i)

(3.20)

known as its
Rees decomposition
. Paraphrasing
[
66
, Proposition 5.1.6]
, we have the following result, which will come into play in Section
8
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
be a formally integrable
n
-th order system of differential equations. The prolongation of
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
combined with our chosen multi-index ordering induces the splitting of multi-indices
(
3.16
)
. The ideal
\mathcal{I}^{\geq n}_{\text{prin}}
has a Pommaret basis, and therefore
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
is involutive, if and only if
\mathcal{I}^{\geq n}_{\mathcal{R},\text{par}}
admits a Rees decomposition.

We end the section with the Cartan–Kähler existence theorem, stated, for simplicity, for first order involutive systems of differential equations in reduced Cartan normal form (
3.14
). This fundamental theorem is a generalization and consequence of the basic Cauchy–Kovalevskaya existence theorem for analytic systems of partial differential equations,
[
51
,
66
]
. In essence, the Cartan–Kähler theorem is established by successive application of the Cauchy–Kovalevskaya theorem to the initial value problems corresponding to each line in the initial conditions (
3.15
).

Theorem 3.12
.

Let the functions
\Delta^{\alpha}_{k}
and
f^{\beta}
in (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) be real-analytic at the origin. If the system (
3.14
) is involutive, then it possesses one and only one solution that is analytic at the origin and satisfies the initial conditions (
3.15
).

4
Lie Pseudo-Groups.

In this section we apply the preceding constructions to the differential equations defining Lie pseudo-group actions, referring to
[
30
,
56
]
for details. Let
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}(M)
denote the Lie pseudo-group of all local analytic diffeomorphisms
3
3

3

In general, the notation allows
\varphi
to only be defined on an open subset of
M
.

\varphi\colon M\to M
. We will employ Cartan’s convenient notational convention and use lower case letters to denote source coordinates and the corresponding capital letters to denote target coordinates. Thus, given a local diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}
, its local coordinate formula will be written
Z=\varphi(z)
, so that the target coordinates
Z=(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{m})
are functions of the source coordinates
z=(z^{1},\ldots,z^{m})
.

Given
0\leq n<\infty
, let
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}(M,M)
be the subbundle consisting of all
n
-th order jets of local diffeomorphisms of
M
. We remark that
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
forms a groupoid, cf.
[
42
]
, under composition. We also let
\mathcal{D}^{(\infty)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{\infty}(M,M)
denote the corresponding space of infinite order jets of diffeomorphisms, and
\mathcal{A}^{(\infty)}\subset\mathcal{D}^{(\infty)}
the subspace of analytic diffeomorphism jets, i.e., those that define convergent Taylor series.

Given a regular analytic Lie pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{D}
, let
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\subset\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
denote the subbundle (subgroupoid) consisting of
n
-th order jets of pseudo-group diffeomorphisms, which we can identify with the
n
-th order determining equations of
\mathcal{G}
, whose solutions are the pseudo-group transformations. Note that, by analyticity,
\mathcal{G}^{(\infty)}\subset\mathcal{A}^{(\infty)}
. According to Theorem 1 of
[
30
]
, there exists an order
n_{\star}\in\mathbb{N}
, called the
order of involutivity
, such that, for all finite
n\geq n_{\star}
, the determining equations

\mathcal{G}^{(n)}=\big\{\Delta_{\nu}(z,Z^{(n)})=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nu=1,\ldots,l_{n}\big\}

(4.1)

are involutive. Separating the pseudo-group jet coordinates by order, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\simeq M\times D^{(n)}=M\times D^{0}\times D^{1}\times\cdots\times D^{n},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\simeq M\times G^{(n)}=M\times G^{0}\times G^{1}\times\cdots\times G^{n},

where

D^{k}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(\ \ldots\ Z^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(\ \ldots\ Z^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(\ \ldots\ Z^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\}}{\{(\ \ldots\ Z^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\}}

denotes the space of
k
-th order derivatives of the local diffeomorphism
Z=\varphi(z)\in\mathcal{D}
, and similarly for
G^{k}
. In view of (
2.4
), (
3.2
), with
p=q=m
, we then have

\mathsf{t}_{k}=\dim\,D^{k}=m\,\binom{m+k-1}{k},

(4.2)

while the number of derivatives of order
k\geq 1
and of class
a
is

{\displaystyle\mathsf{t}_{k}^{(a)}}=m\,\binom{m+k-a-1}{k-1},\hskip 20.00003pt1\leq a\leq m.

(4.3)

For
n\geq 1
, we have the relations

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}\>\mathsf{t}_{k}=\mathsf{t}^{(n)}=\dim D^{(n)},

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{m}\>{\displaystyle\mathsf{t}_{n}^{(a)}}=\mathsf{t}_{n},

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{m}\>a\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{t}_{n}^{(a)}}=\mathsf{t}_{n+1}.

(4.4)

For the Lie pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
, and each
0\leq n<\infty
, let
\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=\dim G^{(n)}
denote the fiber dimension of the projection
\pi^{n}\colon\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\to M
. For
0\leq k\leq n
, let
\mathsf{d}_{k}=\dim G^{k}
denote the number of parametric pseudo-group parameters of order
k
, so that

\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=\mathsf{d}_{0}+\mathsf{d}_{1}+\cdots+\mathsf{d}_{n}.

The number of principal pseudo-group parameters of order
k
is then given by
\mathsf{r}_{k}=\mathsf{t}_{k}-\mathsf{d}_{k}
.

Let
\zeta^{(n)}=(\ \ldots\ \zeta^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )
, for
a=1,\ldots,m
and
|B|\leq n
, be fiber coordinates on the tangent bundle
T\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
, and let

{\rm j}_{n}\mathbf{V}=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\sum_{0\leq|B|\leq n}\zeta^{a}_{B}\frac{\partial}{\partial Z^{a}_{B}}

denote a vertical vector field on
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
, whose coefficients are functions of
z^{(n)}
. The linearization of the pseudo-group determining equations (
4.1
) at the identity jet
\displaystyle\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}
are the
linearized determining equations

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}^{(n)}=\bigg\{L_{\nu}(z,\zeta^{(n)})=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\sum_{0\leq|B|\leq n}\>\left.\frac{\partial\Delta_{\nu}}{\partial Z^{a}_{B}}\;\right|_{\displaystyle\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}}\>\zeta^{a}_{B}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nu=1,\ldots,l_{n}\bigg\},

(4.5)

which serve to define the Lie algebroid associated with the Lie pseudo-group groupoid
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
,
[
56
]
. As before, we introduce the highest order term map
\textbf{H}\colon T\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{D}^{(n)}|_{\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}}\to TD^{n}|_{\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}}
, which only keeps the linear terms of order
n
in (
4.5
), to obtain the
n
-th order pseudo-group
symbol

\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}^{n}=\textbf{H}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}^{(n)})=\bigg\{\sum_{a=1}^{m}\sum_{|B|=n}\>\left.\frac{\partial\Delta_{\nu}}{\partial Z^{a}_{B}}\;\right|_{\displaystyle\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}}\>\zeta^{a}_{B}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\nu=1,\ldots,l_{n}\bigg\}.

Our regularity assumption on
\mathcal{G}
requires that the intrinsic algebraic properties of the symbol are independent of the point
(z,Z^{(n)})\in\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
.

Referring to (
3.8
), (
3.10
), for
n\geq n_{\star}
, the order of involutivity, the indices and Cartan characters of the determining equations
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
satisfy

\sum_{a=1}^{m}\>{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(a)}}=\mathsf{r}_{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\sum_{a=1}^{m}\>{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(a)}}=\mathsf{d}_{n},

(4.6)

and, since the equations are involutive,

\sum_{a=1}^{m}\>a\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{n}^{(a)}}=\mathsf{r}_{n+1}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\sum_{a=1}^{m}\>a\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(a)}}=\mathsf{d}_{n+1}.

(4.7)

Example 4.1
.

The following well-studied Lie pseudo-group, cf.
[
56
,
57
]
,

X=f(x),\hskip 20.00003ptY=f_{x}(x)\,y+g(x),\hskip 20.00003ptU=u+\frac{f_{xx}(x)\,y+g_{x}(x)}{f_{x}(x)},

(4.8)

where
f,g
are analytic scalar functions with
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
, so
f_{x}(x)\neq 0
, will serve as our running example illustrating the constructions. The determining equations
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
of order two (in reduced Cartan normal form) are

\displaystyle X_{y}=X_{u}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\ Y_{x}=(U-u)X_{x},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{y}=X_{x},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{u}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptU_{u}=1,

\displaystyle X_{xx}=U_{y}X_{x},\hskip 10.00002ptX_{xy}=X_{xu}=X_{yy}=X_{yu}=X_{uu}=0,\hskip 10.00002ptY_{xx}=\bigl({U_{x}+(U-u)U_{y}}\bigr)X_{x},

\displaystyle Y_{xy}=U_{y}X_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\ Y_{xu}=Y_{yy}=Y_{yu}=Y_{uu}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptU_{xu}=U_{yy}=U_{yu}=U_{uu}=0.

(4.9)

Thus, the parametric jet variables that serve to parametrize the fibers of
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
are

\displaystyle X,

\displaystyle Y,

\displaystyle U,

\displaystyle X_{x},

\displaystyle U_{x},

\displaystyle U_{y},

\displaystyle U_{xx},

\displaystyle U_{xy};

(4.10)

all the other second order jet coordinates, i.e., those appearing on the left hand side of the determining equations (
4.9
), are principal. We observe that

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{0}=\mathsf{d}_{1}=3,

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{2}=2,\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm and so}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathsf{d}^{(0)}=3,

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{(1)}=6,

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{(2)}=8.

It is not hard to see that, in general, the order
n\geq 2
parametric variables are
U_{x^{n}},U_{x^{n-1}\mskip 2.0muy}
, hence
\mathsf{d}_{n}=2
and
\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=2\mskip 2.0mun+4
. Using the notation

{\rm j}_{\infty}\mathbf{V}=\sum_{|B|\geq 0}\xi_{B}\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{B}}+\eta_{B}\frac{\partial}{\partial Y_{B}}+\phi_{B}\frac{\partial}{\partial U_{B}}

(4.11)

to denote a vertical vector field, the corresponding linearized determining equations
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}^{(2)}
of order two are obtained by applying (
4.11
) to the determining equations (
4.9
) and then evaluating the result at the identity jet, by setting

\displaystyle X=x,

\displaystyle Y=y,

\displaystyle U=u,

\displaystyle X_{x}=Y_{y}=U_{u}=1,

and all other jet coordinates to
0
. The result is the linearized system

\displaystyle\xi_{y}=\xi_{u}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\eta_{x}=\phi,\hskip 20.00003pt\eta_{y}=\xi_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\eta_{u}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\phi_{u}=0,

\displaystyle\xi_{xx}=\phi_{y},\hskip 20.00003pt\xi_{xy}=\xi_{xu}=\xi_{yy}=\xi_{yu}=\xi_{uu}=0,

\displaystyle\eta_{xx}=\phi_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\eta_{xy}=\phi_{y},\hskip 20.00003pt\eta_{xu}=\eta_{yy}=\eta_{yu}=\eta_{uu}=\phi_{xu}=\phi_{yy}=\phi_{yu}=\phi_{uu}=0.

The order two symbol
\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}
is thus given by the equations

\begin{gathered}\xi_{xx}=\xi_{xy}=\xi_{yy}=\xi_{xu}=\xi_{yu}=\xi_{uu}=0,\qquad\eta_{xx}=\eta_{xy}=\eta_{yy}=\eta_{xu}=\eta_{yu}=\eta_{uu}=0,\\
\phi_{xu}=\phi_{yy}=\phi_{yu}=\phi_{uu}=0.\end{gathered}

(4.12)

Using the term ordering
x\prec y\prec u
, the indices of the symbol (
4.12
) are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}=7,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}=6,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(3)}}=3,

(4.13)

while the Cartan characters are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}=2,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(3)}}=0.

(4.14)

On the other hand, the determining equations of order three are obtained by differentiating those of order two in (
4.9
) and then replacing any principal derivatives using the preceding equations, thereby producing

\displaystyle\hfil X_{xxx}\;

\displaystyle=(U_{xy}+U_{y}^{2})X_{x},\hskip 20.00003ptX_{xxy}=X_{xxu}=X_{xyy}=X_{xyu}=X_{xuu}=X_{yyy}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil X_{yyu}\;

\displaystyle=X_{yuu}=X_{uuu}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptY_{xxx}=\bigl({U_{xx}+(U-u)(U_{xy}+U_{y}^{2})+2\mskip 2.0muU_{x}U_{y}}\bigr)X_{x},

\displaystyle\hfil Y_{xxy}\;

\displaystyle=(U_{xy}+U_{y}^{2})X_{x},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{xxu}=Y_{xyy}=Y_{xyu}=Y_{xuu}=Y_{yyy}=Y_{yyu}=Y_{yuu}=Y_{uuu}=0,\hskip-72.26999pt

\displaystyle\hfil U_{xxu}\;

\displaystyle=U_{xyy}=U_{xyu}=U_{xuu}=U_{yyy}=U_{yyu}=U_{yuu}=U_{uuu}=0,

(4.15)

where
U_{xxx},U_{xxy}
are the only parametric third order derivatives and the other
28
third order derivatives are all principal. We thus see that the algebraic involutivity constraint

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(3)}}=\mathsf{r}_{3}=28

is satisfied. Alternatively, in terms of the Cartan characters,

{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(3)}}=\mathsf{d}_{3}=2.

Since
\pi^{3}_{2}(\mathcal{G}^{(3)})=\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
, there are no integrability conditions at order three, and the determining equations (
4.9
) are involutive. Based on the Cartan characters (
4.14
), the solution depends on two functions of one variable, as was already clear from the original formula (
4.8
) for the pseudo-group transformations.

5
Reduction of Lie Pseudo-Group Actions.

We are now interested in the action of a Lie pseudo-group on
p
-dimensional submanifolds of the total space
M
. To work in local coordinates, we assume that the submanifolds are transverse to the fibers, and thus form local sections of
M\to\mathcal{X}
. In this section, we formulate the reduced determining equations for the action of pseudo-group elements on sections, and prove that they form an involutive system of differential equations. This construction is a key intermediate step towards our formulation of the system of differential equations satisfied by the normal forms of submanifolds.

We introduce the local coordinates
z=(x,u)=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p},u^{1},\ldots,u^{q})
on the total space
M
, where
p+q=m=\dim M
, so that submanifolds are locally given as the graphs of functions
u=u(x)
. In accordance with Cartan’s notation introduced in Section
4
, the corresponding target coordinates are given by
Z=(X,U)=(X^{1},\ldots,X^{p},U^{1},\ldots,U^{q})
. Let
{\rm J}{}^{n}
denote the corresponding submanifold jet space, with coordinates
z^{(n)}=(x,u^{(n)})=(\ \ldots\ x^{i}\ \ldots\ u^{\alpha}_{J}\ \ldots\ )
for
i=1,\ldots,p
,
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
, and
|J|\leq n
.

As in
[
57
,
58
]
, let
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\to{\rm J}{}^{n}
denote the
lifted bundle
obtained by pulling back the diffeomorphism jet bundle
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}\to M
to the submanifold jet space via the standard projection
\pi^{n}_{0}\colon{\rm J}{}^{n}\to M
. Local coordinates on
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
are given by
(z^{(n)},Z^{(n)})=(x,u^{(n)},X^{(n)},U^{(n)})
, where
(x,u^{(n)})
are the preceding submanifold jet coordinates, while
Z^{(n)}=(X^{(n)},U^{(n)})=(\ \ldots\ X^{i}_{A}\ \ldots\ U^{\alpha}_{B}\ \ldots\ )
for
i=1,\ldots,p
,
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
, and
|A|,|B|\leq n
are the fiber coordinates of the diffeomorphism jet bundle
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
. The lifted bundle has the structure of a groupoid using the double fibration with source map
\bm{\sigma}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},Z^{(n)})=z^{(n)}
and target map
\bm{\tau}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},Z^{(n)})=Z^{(n)}\cdot z^{(n)}
prescribed by the prolonged action of the diffeomorphisms on submanifold jets.

When writing out the action of a pseudo-group transformation on a submanifold, we will continue to use, in accordance with Cartan’s convention, lower case letters for the source submanifold
u=u(x)
and its jet coordinates
u^{\alpha}_{J}
. However, to avoid notational confusion, especially when distinguishing submanifold jets from diffeomorphism jets, we will use hats on the dependent variable and its derivatives to denote the target submanifold, which we thus write as
\widehat{U}=\widehat{U}(X)
with the order zero jet being simply
\widehat{U}=U
, while the higher order jet coordinates are denoted
\widehat{U}^{\alpha}_{J}
. Later, once the reader becomes used to which symbol denotes which type of jet coordinate, the hats can be dropped to clean up the formulas, and, indeed, we shall do so in the examples treated in Section
10
.

Example 5.1
.

Let
M={\mathbb{R}}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}
and
\mathcal{X}={\mathbb{R}}
, which is the setting for plane curves
s=\{(x,u(x))\}
. Given the action of a diffeomorphism of
{\mathbb{R}}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}
on curves, the source curve is the graph of a scalar function
u=u(x)
for
x,u\in{\mathbb{R}}
, with jet coordinates
u,u_{x},u_{xx},\ldots
, while the target is also the graph of a scalar function, which, in accordance with the above-stated convention, is written as
\widehat{U}=\widehat{U}(X)
for
X,\widehat{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Its jet coordinates are then given by
\widehat{U},\widehat{U}_{X},\widehat{U}_{XX},\ldots
.
The coordinates on the lifted bundle
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
are thus given by

\displaystyle(z^{(n)},Z^{(n)})

\displaystyle=(x,u^{(n)},X^{(n)},U^{(n)})

\displaystyle=(x,u,u_{x},u_{xx},\ldots,X,U,X_{x},X_{u},U_{x},U_{u},X_{xx},X_{xu},X_{uu},U_{xx},U_{xu},U_{uu},\ldots),

where
u
,
u_{x}
,
u_{xx}
,
\ldots
are the source curve jet coordinates, while
X,U,X_{x},X_{u},U_{x},U_{u},\ldots
(which do not have hats) are the diffeomorphism jet coordinates. The source and target maps on the lifted bundle
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\to{\rm J}{}^{n}
are

\displaystyle\hfil\bm{\sigma}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},Z^{(n)})\;

\displaystyle=(x,u,u_{x},u_{xx},\ldots),

\displaystyle\hfil\bm{\tau}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},Z^{(n)})\;

\displaystyle=(X,\widehat{U},\widehat{U}_{X},\widehat{U}_{XX},\ldots){}

\displaystyle\hskip-60.0pt{}=\left(\!X,U,\frac{U_{x}+u_{x}U_{u}}{X_{x}+u_{x}X_{u}},\frac{{\hskip-54.2025pt\displaystyle\big[(X_{x}+u_{x}X_{u})(U_{xx}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{x}U_{xu}+u_{x}^{2}U_{uu}+u_{xx}U_{u})\atop\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt{}-(U_{x}+u_{x}U_{u})(X_{xx}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{x}X_{xu}+u_{x}^{2}X_{uu}+u_{xx}X_{u})\big]}}{(X_{x}+u_{x}X_{u})^{3}},\ldots\right),

where the higher order target jets are obtained by repeatedly applying the operator of
implicit differentiation

\mathrm{D}_{X}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{D}_{x}X}\,\mathrm{D}_{x}=\frac{1}{X_{x}+u_{x}X_{u}}\,\mathrm{D}_{x}

to
\widehat{U}
; see also (
6.1
) below.

The
horizontal total derivative operators
on the infinite order lifted bundles
\mathcal{E}^{(\infty)}
are

\mathrm{D}_{x^{i}}=\mathbb{D}_{x^{i}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\bigg(u^{\alpha}_{i}\,\mathbb{D}_{u^{\alpha}}+\sum_{|J|\geq 1}u^{\alpha}_{J,i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}\bigg),\hskip 20.00003pti=1,\ldots,p,

(5.1)

where
4
4

4

Here
z^{a}
can be either
x^{i}
or
u^{\alpha}
.

\mathbb{D}_{z^{a}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{a}}+\sum_{b=1}^{m}\sum_{|A|\geq 0}Z_{A,a}^{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial Z^{b}_{A}},\hskip 20.00003pta=1,\ldots,m,

are the total derivative operators on the diffeomorphism jet bundle
\mathcal{D}^{(\infty)}
. We use the same notation (
2.11
) and (
5.1
) for the total derivative operators on
{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
and
\mathcal{E}^{(\infty)}
, respectively, since they coincide when applied to a function
F(z^{(n)})=F(x,u^{(n)})
that does not depend on the diffeomorphism jet coordinates.

Given a local section
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to M
, whose graph defines a
p
-dimensional submanifold
s=f(\mathcal{X})
, and a local diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(M)
, with
s\subset\mathop{\rm dom}\nolimits\varphi
, we call the composition
\overline{\varphi}{}=\varphi\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\,\scriptstyle\circ\,$}f
the
reduction
of
\varphi
to the submanifold
s
. The reduced map
\overline{\varphi}{}\colon\mathcal{X}\to M
is in general not a section of
M
since
\varphi\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\,\scriptstyle\circ\,$}f(x)
does not necessarily belong to the fiber of
M
over
x\in\mathcal{X}
. On the other hand, its image, namely
S=\varphi[\mskip 2.0mu{f(\mathcal{X})}\mskip 2.0mu]=\overline{\varphi}{}(s)
is an equivalent submanifold. If we assume that the image submanifold
S
is transversal to the fibers of
M
, we can locally identify it with the graph of a local section
F\colon\mathcal{X}\to M
, so
S=F(\mathcal{X})
.

Remark 5.2
.

We will use overbars to denote reduced maps and jet coordinates. As with the hats, these can also be dropped once the reader becomes used to which symbol denotes which jet coordinate, and, indeed, we shall do so in Section
10
.

For
0\leq n<\infty
, the reduced action of local diffeomorphisms on submanifolds is encoded by the
reduction map

\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}\colon\mathcal{E}^{(n)}\to{\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U}\times M)
given by

\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)},X^{(n)},U^{(n)})=\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},Z^{(n)})=(z^{(n)},\overline{Z}{}^{(n)})=(x,u^{(n)},\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{U}{}^{(n)}),

(5.2)

where
\overline{Z}{}^{(n)}=(\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{U}{}^{(n)})
has components

\overline{Z}{}^{a}_{J}=\mathrm{D}_{x}^{J}Z^{a}\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm for}\hskip 20.00003pta=1,\ldots,m,\hskip 10.00002pt0\leq|J|\leq n,

which are obtained by successively applying the total derivative operators (
5.1
) to the diffeomorphism target coordinates
Z=(X,U)
. We call
\overline{Z}{}^{a}_{J}
the
reduced jet coordinates
. The reduction map is compatible with the reduction of diffeomorphisms to submanifolds. Namely, given a diffeomorphism
\varphi
and a section
s=f(x)=(x,u(x))
contained in its domain, let
(x,u^{(n)},X^{(n)},U^{(n)})\in\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
be given by their combined jets, so that
(x,u^{(n)})={\rm j}_{n}f|_{x}
and
(x,u,X^{(n)},U^{(n)})={\rm j}_{n}\varphi|_{(x,u)}
. Then
{\rm j}_{n}(\varphi\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\,\scriptstyle\circ\,$}f)=\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)},X^{(n)},U^{(n)})
.

We will regard
{\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U}\times M)\to{\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U})={\rm J}{}^{n}
as a fiber bundle over the submanifold jet bundle, so that the reduced jet coordinates
\overline{Z}{}^{(n)}=(\ \ldots\ \overline{Z}{}^{a}_{J}\ \ldots\ )
are its fiber coordinates.

Example 5.3
.

Let
M={\mathbb{R}}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}
and
\mathcal{X}={\mathbb{R}}
, as in Example
5.1
. The reduction map (
5.2
) is computed by successively applying the total derivative operator

\mathrm{D}_{x}=\mathbb{D}_{x}+u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\mathbb{D}_{u}+u_{xx}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{x}}}{\partial/\partial u_{x}}{\partial/\partial u_{x}}{\partial/\partial u_{x}}+u_{xxx}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{xx}}}{\partial/\partial u_{xx}}{\partial/\partial u_{xx}}{\partial/\partial u_{xx}}+\ \cdots\ ,

(5.3)

with

\displaystyle\hfil\mathbb{D}_{x}\;

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}{\partial/\partial x}{\partial/\partial x}{\partial/\partial x}+X_{x}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial X}}{\partial/\partial X}{\partial/\partial X}{\partial/\partial X}+U_{x}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial U}}{\partial/\partial U}{\partial/\partial U}{\partial/\partial U}+X_{xx}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{x}}}{\partial/\partial X_{x}}{\partial/\partial X_{x}}{\partial/\partial X_{x}}+X_{xu}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{u}}}{\partial/\partial X_{u}}{\partial/\partial X_{u}}{\partial/\partial X_{u}}+U_{xx}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial U_{x}}}{\partial/\partial U_{x}}{\partial/\partial U_{x}}{\partial/\partial U_{x}}+U_{xu}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial U_{u}}}{\partial/\partial U_{u}}{\partial/\partial U_{u}}{\partial/\partial U_{u}}+\ \cdots\ ,

\displaystyle\hfil\mathbb{D}_{u}\;

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial u}}{\partial/\partial u}{\partial/\partial u}{\partial/\partial u}+X_{u}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial X}}{\partial/\partial X}{\partial/\partial X}{\partial/\partial X}+U_{u}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial U}}{\partial/\partial U}{\partial/\partial U}{\partial/\partial U}+X_{xu}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{x}}}{\partial/\partial X_{x}}{\partial/\partial X_{x}}{\partial/\partial X_{x}}+X_{uu}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{u}}}{\partial/\partial X_{u}}{\partial/\partial X_{u}}{\partial/\partial X_{u}}+U_{xu}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial U_{x}}}{\partial/\partial U_{x}}{\partial/\partial U_{x}}{\partial/\partial U_{x}}+U_{uu}\mathchoice{\frac{\partial}{\partial U_{u}}}{\partial/\partial U_{u}}{\partial/\partial U_{u}}{\partial/\partial U_{u}}+\ \cdots\ ,

(5.4)

to
X,U
. This produces, at order
n=2
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}^{(2)}(x,u,u_{x},u_{xx},X,U,X_{x},X_{u},U_{x},U_{u},X_{xx},X_{xu},X_{uu},U_{xx},U_{xu},U_{uu})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt=(x,u,u_{x},u_{xx},\overline{X}{},\overline{U}{},\overline{X}{}_{x},\overline{U}{}_{x},\overline{X}{}_{xx},\overline{U}{}_{xx})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt=(x,u,u_{x},u_{xx},X,U,\mathrm{D}_{x}X,\mathrm{D}_{x}U,\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}X,\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}U)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt=(x,u,u_{x},u_{xx},X,U,X_{x}+u_{x}X_{u},U_{x}+u_{x}U_{u},

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt\hskip 68.28644ptX_{xx}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{x}X_{xu}+u_{x}^{2}X_{uu}+u_{xx}X_{u},U_{xx}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{x}X_{xu}+u_{x}^{2}U_{uu}+u_{xx}U_{u}).

Observe that the expressions for the reduced jet coordinates are obtained by total differentiation of
\displaystyle X=X(x,u),

\displaystyle U=U(x,u)
, treating
u
as a function of
x
.

5.1
The Reduced Determining Equations.

Just as the original pseudo-group jets satisfy a system of differential equations,
so do the reduced pseudo-group jets.
To construct this “reduced” system, first define the
lifted subgroupoid

\mathcal{H}^{(n)}\subset\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
to be the pullback of
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
to the submanifold jet bundle
{\rm J}{}^{n}\to M
. We then define the
n
-th order
reduced pseudo-group jet bundle
by applying the reduction map (
5.2
):

\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}=\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}(\mathcal{H}^{(n)})\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U}\times M).

(5.5)

This can be written as a system of equations of the form

\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}=\big\{\,\overline{\Delta}{}_{\nu}(z^{(n)},\overline{Z}{}^{(n)})=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nu=1,\ldots,\overline{l}{}_{n}\big\}.

(5.6)

If we fix a section
s=\{(x,u(x))\}
with jet
z^{(n)}={\rm j}_{n}s|_{x}=(x,u^{(n)}(x))
, then (
5.6
) can be viewed as an
n
-th order system of differential equations
for the reduced diffeomorphism
\overline{Z}{}=\overline{\varphi}{}(x)
, that we call the
reduced determining equations
, whose properties will be investigated shortly.

In local coordinates, the reduced determining equations encode all the algebraic relations that exist among the reduced jets
\overline{Z}{}{}^{(n)}
. They are obtained by writing out the formulas for the reduced jet coordinates in terms of the parametric pseudo-group derivatives, and then eliminating the latter from the resulting algebraic expressions, i.e., implicitizing the resulting parametric formulae, thereby producing the identities involving only the submanifold jet coordinates and the reduced jet coordinates.

Example 5.4
.

Recalling the determining equations (
4.9
) of the Lie pseudo-group (
4.8
), we now compute the reduced determining equations, assuming that
u=u(x,y)
. The pseudo-group jet coordinates parametrizing
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
are given in (
4.10
). At order zero, we trivially have

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}=X,

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}=Y,

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}=U.

Next, at order one, in view of the first order determining equations in (
4.9
), we find

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{x}=X_{x}+X_{u}u_{x}=X_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{y}=X_{y}+X_{u}u_{y}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{x}=Y_{x}+Y_{u}u_{x}=Y_{x}=(U-u)X_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{y}=Y_{y}+Y_{u}u_{y}=X_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{x}=U_{x}+U_{u}u_{x}=U_{x}+u_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{y}=U_{y}+U_{u}u_{y}=U_{y}+u_{y}.

Differentiating again, and skipping computational details, at order two we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xx}=U_{y}X_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{xx}=\bigl({U_{x}+(U-u)U_{y}}\bigr)X_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{xy}=U_{y}X_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{xx}=U_{xx}+u_{xx},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{xy}=U_{xy}+u_{xy},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{yy}=u_{yy}.

Implicitization, i.e., eliminating the parametric variables
X,Y,U,X_{x},U_{x},U_{y},U_{xx},U_{xy}
, we find that, up to order two, the relations among the reduced pseudo-group jet coordinates are

\begin{gathered}\overline{X}{}_{y}=0,\qquad\overline{Y}{}_{x}=(\overline{U}{}-u)\overline{X}{}_{x},\qquad\overline{Y}{}_{y}=\overline{X}{}_{x},\qquad\overline{X}{}_{xx}=(\overline{U}{}_{y}-u_{y})\overline{X}{}_{x},\\
\overline{X}{}_{xy}=\overline{X}{}_{yy}=0,\qquad\overline{Y}{}_{xx}=\bigl(\,{\overline{U}{}_{x}-u_{x}+(\overline{U}{}-u)(\overline{U}{}_{y}-u_{y})}\,\bigr)\overline{X}{}_{x},\\
\overline{Y}{}_{xy}=(\overline{U}{}_{y}-u_{y})\overline{X}{}_{x},\qquad\overline{Y}{}_{yy}=0,\qquad\overline{U}{}_{yy}=u_{yy},\end{gathered}

(5.7)

which thus form the second order reduced determining equations. We note that the parametric variables are
\overline{X}{},\overline{Y}{},\overline{U}{},\overline{X}{}_{x},\overline{U}{}_{x},\overline{U}{}_{y},\overline{U}{}_{xx},\overline{U}{}_{xy}
.

A key observation that we will need in Section
7.1
is that the reduced determining equations must become identities when the pseudo-group element is the identity map, and hence the two sections coincide. Algebraically, this specialization amounts to equating

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}^{i}=x^{i},

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}^{i}_{i}=1,

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}=0,

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,p,

\displaystyle J\neq i,

\displaystyle|J|\geq 1,

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}^{\alpha}_{K}=u^{\alpha}_{K},

\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q,

\displaystyle|K|\geq 0.

(5.8)

The equations defining
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}
must vanish identically on the affine subvariety defined by (
5.8
). For example, in the case of the pseudo-group in Example
5.4
, every reduced determining equation in (
5.7
) vanishes identically when

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{x}=\overline{Y}{}_{y}=1,

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{y}=\overline{Y}{}_{x}=\overline{X}{}_{xx}=\overline{X}{}_{xy}=\overline{X}{}_{yy}=\overline{Y}{}_{xx}=\overline{Y}{}_{xy}=\overline{Y}{}_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}=u,

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{x}=u_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{y}=u_{y},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{yy}=u_{yy}.

(5.9)

According to
[
50
, Proposition 2.10]
, this implies that the equations (
5.6
) can be expressed as a linear combination

\overline{\Delta}{}_{\nu}=\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\left[\,{A_{\nu}^{i}(\overline{X}{}^{i}-x^{i})+A_{\nu}^{i,i}(\overline{X}{}^{i}_{i}-1)+\sum_{\scriptstyle J\neq i\atop\scriptstyle 1\leq|J|\leq n}\>A_{\nu}^{i,J}\,\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}}\,\right]+\sum_{\alpha\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{q}\>\sum_{0\leq|K|\leq n}\>B_{\nu}^{\alpha,K}(\overline{U}{}^{\alpha}_{K}-u^{\alpha}_{K}),

(5.10)

where the coefficient functions
A_{\nu}^{i},A_{\nu}^{i,i},A_{\nu}^{i,J},B_{\nu}^{\alpha,K}
are analytic.

We now state the key condition to be imposed on the pseudo-group actions to be considered in this paper.

Definition 5.5
.

The pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
is order
n

reducible
on the local section
s\colon\mathcal{X}\to M
if, for all
x\in\mathop{\rm dom}\nolimits s
with
z^{(n)}={\rm j}_{n}s|_{x}
, the reduction map
\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}\colon\mathcal{H}^{(n)}|_{z^{(n)}}\to\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}|_{z^{(n)}}
is one-to-one on the indicated fibers. The pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
is
reducible
on
s
if it is reducible for all sufficiently large
n\geq n_{\natural}
. The integer
n_{\natural}
is called the
order of reducibility
.

As we will see in Theorem
6.5
below, all pseudo-groups for which the moving frame calculus is applicable automatically satisfy this condition on generic sections. In particular, this implies that any finite-dimensional Lie group action is reducible.

Definition 5.6
.

A section
s\colon\mathcal{X}\to M
is called
regular
if
\mathcal{G}
is reducible on it.

In what follows, we will only deal with regular sections. In particular, the reduced determining equations are to be evaluated only on regular sections. Assuming analyticity, if the pseudo-group is regular on one section, regularity holds on generic sections.

Let
\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}
denote the fiber dimension of the reduced determining equations (
5.6
), which can be identified as the number of parametric reduced pseudo-group parameters of order
\leq n
.
A basic property of reducible Lie pseudo-groups is given in the following result.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a reducible Lie pseudo-group with order of reducibility
n_{\natural}
. Then for all
n\geq n_{\natural}
, the number of independent reduced pseudo-group parameters equals the number of pseudo-group parameters. That is,

\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}.

(5.11)

In other words, reducibility requires that the reduction map does not change the fiber dimensions at sufficiently high orders. Since

0\leq\mathsf{d}^{(n)}\leq(p+q)\,\binom{p+q+n}{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt0\leq\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}\leq(p+q)\,\binom{p+n}{n},

(5.12)

we see that reducibility imposes constraints on the size of the pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
, in that it cannot be too large; see Lemma
5.11
below. For example,
\mathcal{G}
cannot be the full diffeomorphism pseudo-group
\mathcal{D}
, which maximizes the inequality (
5.12
) for
\mathsf{d}^{(n)}
.

Example 5.8
.

Returning to Example
5.4
, in view of (
5.7
) and its prolongations, it follows that the parametric reduced pseudo-group jet coordinates are

\displaystyle\overline{X}{},

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{},

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{x^{k}},

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{x^{k-1}y},

\displaystyle k\geq 1.

(5.13)

Thus, the reduced dimensions satisfy

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(1)}=6=\mathsf{d}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(2)}=8=\mathsf{d}^{(2)},\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm and, in general,}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}=2\mskip 2.0mun+4=\mathsf{d}^{(n)},

thus proving that this pseudo-group is reducible.

Example 5.9
.

An example where
n_{\natural}>1
in Definition
5.5
is provided by the 5-dimensional Lie group action

X=a\,x+b,\hskip 20.00003ptU=c\,u+d\,x+e,

where
a,c\neq 0
and
b,d,e\in\mathbb{R}
. Up to order two, the determining equations are

\displaystyle X_{u}=X_{xx}=X_{xu}=X_{uu}=0,

\displaystyle U_{xx}=U_{xu}=U_{uu}=0.

Prolonging, we deduce that, as expected,

\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=5\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm for all}\hskip 20.00003ptn\in\mathbb{N}.

On the other hand, assuming the regularity condition
u_{xx}\neq 0
, the reduced determining equations, up to order three, are

\overline{X}{}_{xx}=\overline{X}{}_{xxx}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{U}{}_{xxx}=\frac{u_{xxx}}{u_{xx}}\,\overline{U}{}_{xx},

and
\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(1)}=4
, while
\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}=5
for
n\geq 2
.
Thus,
\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}
for all
n\geq n_{\natural}=2
.

Example 5.10
.

Consider the Lie pseudo-group

X=x,\hskip 20.00003ptU=f(x,u).

In this case,

\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=\binom{n+2}{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{while}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}=n,

and hence the pseudo-group is not reducible, basically because it has a one-dimensional base but the transformations depend upon an arbitrary function of two variables.

The last example can be easily generalized, proving that a reducible pseudo-group cannot depend on functions of
\geq p+1
variables. We state this fact in terms of its Cartan characters.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
