be a reducible Lie pseudo-group whose determining equations become involutive at order
n_{\star}
. Then
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n_{\star}}^{(p+\alpha)}}=0
for
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
.

Proof.

For the purpose of contradiction, assume there is a Cartan character
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n_{\star}}^{(p+\alpha)}}\neq 0
for some
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
. The pseudo-group thus admits at least one arbitrary function depending on at least
p+1
variables, and hence

\mathsf{d}^{(n)}\geq\mathsf{a}_{n}=\binom{p+n+1}{n}=\frac{(p+2)\cdots(p+n+1)}{n!},

where
\mathsf{a}_{n}
is the number of jet components of order
0\leq|J|\leq n
associated with a function
f(z^{1},\ldots,z^{p+1})
of
p+1
variables.
On the other hand, according to (
5.12
),

\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}\leq(p+q)\,\binom{p+n}{n}=\frac{(p+q)\mskip 2.0mu(p+1)\mskip 2.0mu(p+2)\cdots(p+n)}{n!}=\frac{(p+1)(p+q)}{p+n+1}\,\mathsf{a}_{n}<\mathsf{a}_{n}\leq\mathsf{d}^{(n)},

whenever
n\geq\max\{n_{\star},n_{\natural},p^{2}+p\mskip 2.0muq+q\}
. Hence the reducibility condition (
5.11
) cannot hold when
n
is sufficiently large.

Q.E.D.

5.2
The Linearized Reduced Determining Equations.

Linearizing the reduced determining equations (
5.6
) at the reduced identity pseudo-group jet (
5.8
) yields the
linearized reduced determining equations

\mathcal{L}_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{(n)}=\big\{\overline{L}{}_{\nu}(z^{(n)},\overline{\zeta}{}^{(n)})=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nu=1,\ldots,\overline{l}{}_{n}\big\}.

(5.14)

Keeping only the highest order terms, we obtain the
reduced symbol

\Sigma_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{n}=\textbf{H}(\mathcal{L}_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{(n)}),

(5.15)

where, again,
H
is the highest order term map which only keeps the order
n
terms in the linearized reduced determining equations (
5.14
).
The coefficient matrix of the reduced symbol (
5.15
) yields the
n
-th order reduced symbol matrix
M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{n}
, from which we can compute the reduced indices
{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}
and reduced Cartan characters
{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}
for
i=1,\ldots,p
.

As in the previous section, we separate the reduced pseudo-group jet coordinates by order and let

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}{}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm J}{}^{n}\times\overline{D}{}^{(n)}={\rm J}{}^{n}\times\overline{D}{}^{0}\times\overline{D}{}^{1}\times\cdots\times\overline{D}{}^{n},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm J}{}^{n}\times\overline{G}{}^{(n)}={\rm J}{}^{n}\times\overline{G}{}^{0}\times\overline{G}{}^{1}\times\cdots\times\overline{G}{}^{n},

where

\overline{D}{}^{k}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(\ \ldots\ \overline{Z}{}^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(\ \ldots\ \overline{Z}{}^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(\ \ldots\ \overline{Z}{}^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\}}{\{(\ \ldots\ \overline{Z}{}^{a}_{B}\ \ldots\ )\hbox{}\,|\,|B|=k,\hskip 10.00002pta=1,\ldots,m\hbox{}\}}

denotes the space of
k
-th order derivatives of reduced local diffeomorphisms and similarly for
\overline{G}{}^{k}
, the latter subject to the reduced determining equations. The number of derivatives of order
k
is

\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k}=\dim\,\overline{D}{}^{k}=m\,\binom{p+k-1}{k}.

Of those, the number of derivatives of class
1\leq i\leq p
is

{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k}^{(i)}}=m\,\binom{p+k-i-1}{k-1},

so that

\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k}^{(i)}}=\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k},\hskip 20.00003pt\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>i\,{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k}^{(i)}}=\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k+1},\hskip 20.00003pt\sum_{k=0}^{n}\>\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k}=\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}^{(n)}=\dim\overline{D}{}^{(n)}.

For the reduced Lie pseudo-group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
, we let
\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{k}=\dim\overline{G}{}^{k}
denote the number of parametric reduced pseudo-group parameters of order
k
, so that

\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{0}+\ \cdots\ +\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{n}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n)}=\dim\overline{G}{}^{(n)},

which is the fiber dimension of the reduced determining equations of order
n
. The number of principal reduced pseudo-group parameters of order
k
is then given by

\overline{\mathsf{r}}{}_{k}=\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{k}-\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{k}.

Finally, the indices and Cartan characters of the reduced determining equations (
5.6
) satisfy

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}+{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}={\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{t}}{}_{n}^{(i)}},

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,p,

(5.16)

with

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}=\overline{\mathsf{r}}{}_{n}=\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{n},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{n}=\dim\Sigma_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{n}.

(5.17)

5.3
Involutivity of the Reduced Determining System.

The aim of this section is to prove that, under the assumption that the submanifold is reducible, the reduced determining system (
5.6
) is involutive. Moreover, the first
p
Cartan characters of the determining system and its reduction coincide.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a reducible Lie pseudo-group with order of reduciblity
n_{\natural}
and such that
\mathcal{G}^{(n_{\star})}
is involutive. Then there exists
\overline{n}{}_{\star}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq\overline{n}{}_{\star}\geq\max\{n_{\star},n_{\natural}\}
,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(i)}}={\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(i)}},

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,p,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(p+\alpha)}}=0,

\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q.

(5.18)

In particular, the involutivity of the
n
-th order determining equations
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
implies the involutivity of the
n
-th order reduced determining equations
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

First of all, the second set of equalities in (
5.18
)
follows from Lemma
5.11
with
n\geq n_{\star}
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is reducible, consider the reduced determining equations
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n_{\natural})}
, where
n_{\natural}
is the order of reducibility. By the Cartan–Kuranishi Theorem
[
66
, Theorem 7.4.1]
, after prolongation and projection, there exists
\overline{n}{}_{\star}\geq\text{max}\{n_{\star},n_{\natural}\}
such that the reduced determining equations
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(\overline{n}{}_{\star})}
are involutive.

Let
n\geq\overline{n}{}_{\star}
and
k\geq 1
. By the definition (
3.9
) of the Cartan characters

\mathsf{d}^{(n+k)}=\mathsf{d}^{(n+k-1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n+k}^{(i)}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n+k)}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n+k-1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n+k}^{(i)}},

where we used the fact that
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n+k}^{(p+\alpha)}}=0
,
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
.
Reducibility of the pseudo-group implies that
\mathsf{d}^{(n+k)}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n+k)}
and
\mathsf{d}^{(n+k-1)}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(n+k-1)}
, which requires

\sum_{i=1}^{p}\big({\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n+k}^{(i)}}-{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n+k}^{(i)}}\big)=0.

(5.19)

Using
[
66
, Eq. (8.8a)]
, the higher order Cartan characters are related via the equation

{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n+k}^{(i)}}=\sum_{j=i}^{p}\binom{k+j-i-1}{k-1}{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(j)}},\hskip 20.00003pti=1,\ldots,p.

(5.20)

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n+k}^{(i)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=i}^{p}\binom{k+j-i-1}{k-1}{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(j)}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{j}\binom{k+j-i-1}{k-1}{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(j)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\binom{k-1+j}{k}{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(j)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(1)}}+(k+1){\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(2)}}+\cdots+\frac{(k+1)\cdots(k+p-1)}{(p-1)!}{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(p)}}.

Substituting the last expression and its reduced version into (
5.19
), we obtain

\big({\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(1)}}-{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(1)}}\big)+(k+1)\big({\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(2)}}-{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(2)}}\big)+\cdots+\frac{(k+1)\cdots(k+p-1)}{(p-1)!}\big({\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(p)}}-{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(p)}}\big)=0.

Viewing this expression as a degree
p-1
polynomial in the variable
k
which vanishes for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we conclude that
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(i)}}-{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}=0
for
i=1,\ldots,p
.

Q.E.D.

Remark 5.13
.

Theorem
5.12
implies that if
\mathcal{G}
is reducible, then, at a sufficiently high order, the determining equations and the reduced determining equations of the pseudo-group contain the same number of parametric pseudo-group jet coordinates, and, furthermore, their first
p
Cartan characters are the same.

Example 5.14
.

Continuing Example
5.4
, we linearize the second order reduced determining equations (
5.7
) at the reduced identity jet and obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{y}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=\overline{\phi}{},

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{y}=\overline{\xi}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{xx}=\overline{\phi}{}_{y},

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{xy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{xx}=\overline{\phi}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{xy}=\overline{\phi}{}_{y},

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{yy}=\overline{\phi}{}_{yy}=0.

(5.21)

The order two reduced symbol is then given by the equations

\overline{\xi}{}_{xx}=\overline{\xi}{}_{xy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{yy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{xx}=\overline{\eta}{}_{xy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{yy}=\overline{\phi}{}_{yy}=0

so that the reduced indices and Cartan characters are

{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}=4,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=3,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}=2,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=0.

On the other hand, the order three reduced determining equations are

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xxx}=\bigl(\,{(\overline{U}{}_{y}-u_{y})^{2}+(\overline{U}{}_{xy}-u_{xy})}\,\bigr)\overline{X}{}_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}_{xxy}=\overline{X}{}_{xyy}=\overline{X}{}_{yyy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{xxx}=\bigl(\,{\overline{U}{}_{xx}-u_{xx}+(\overline{U}{}-u)\bigl(\,{\overline{U}{}_{xy}-u_{xy}+(\overline{U}{}_{y}-u_{u})^{2}}\,\bigr)+2\mskip 2.0mu(\overline{U}{}_{y}-u_{y})(\overline{U}{}_{x}-u_{x})}\,\bigr)\overline{X}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{xxy}=\bigl(\,{\overline{U}{}_{xy}-u_{xy}+(\overline{U}{}_{y}-u_{y})^{2}}\,\bigr)\overline{X}{}_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{Y}{}_{xyy}=\overline{Y}{}_{yyy}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{U}{}_{xyy}=u_{xyy},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{U}{}_{yyy}=u_{yyy},

from which we see that
\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{r}}{}_{3}=10,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{3}=2
, and
\pi^{3}_{2}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(3)})=\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(2)}
. Since

{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=\overline{\mathsf{r}}{}_{3}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or, equivalently,}\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{3},

the reduced determining equations (
5.7
) of order
\overline{n}{}_{\star}=2
are involutive.

Remark 5.15
.

In the previous example, the order at which the reduced determining equations became involutive, was the same as the order of the original determining equations (recall Example
4.1
) , i.e.
\overline{n}{}_{\star}=n_{\star}=2
. The next example shows that this does not always hold, and that, in general,
\overline{n}{}_{\star}\geq n_{\star}
.

Example 5.16
.

To illustrate the second half of the preceding remark, consider the Lie pseudo-group

X=x+a,\hskip 20.00003ptY=y+b,\hskip 20.00003ptU=f(x)\,u+g(x)\,y+h(x),

where
f,g,h
are analytic scalar functions with
f\neq 0
,
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. The determining equations, up to order two, are

\displaystyle X_{x}=Y_{y}=1,

\displaystyle X_{y}=X_{u}=Y_{x}=Y_{u}=0,

\displaystyle X_{xx}=X_{xy}=X_{yy}=X_{xu}=X_{yu}=X_{uu}=0,

\displaystyle Y_{xx}=Y_{xy}=Y_{yy}=Y_{xu}=Y_{yu}=Y_{uu}=0,

\displaystyle U_{yy}=U_{yu}=U_{uu}=0.

The corresponding indices and Cartan characters are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}=6,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(3)}}=3,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}=3,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(3)}}=0.

Computing the order three determining equations, we obtain

\displaystyle X_{xxx}=X_{xxy}=X_{xxu}=X_{xyy}=X_{xyu}=X_{xuu}=X_{yyy}=X_{yyu}=X_{yuu}=X_{uuu}=0,

\displaystyle Y_{xxx}=Y_{xxy}=Y_{xxu}=Y_{xyy}=Y_{xyu}=Y_{xuu}=Y_{yyy}=Y_{yyu}=Y_{yuu}=Y_{uuu}=0,

\displaystyle U_{xyy}=U_{yyy}=U_{yyu}=U_{xyu}=U_{yuu}=U_{xuu}=U_{uuu}=0.

Since

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(3)}}=27=\mathsf{r}_{3},\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(3)}}=3=\mathsf{d}_{3},

and there are no integrability conditions, this proves involutivity at order
n_{\star}=2
.
On the other hand, the reduced determining equations, up to order two, are

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{x}=\overline{Y}{}_{y}=1,

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{y}=\overline{Y}{}_{x}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xx}=\overline{X}{}_{xy}=\overline{X}{}_{yy}=\overline{Y}{}_{xx}=\overline{Y}{}_{xy}=\overline{Y}{}_{yy}=0.

(5.22)

The reduced indices are
\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}=4,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=2
, while the reduced Cartan characters are
\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}=2,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=1
. Furthermore, provided the regularity condition
u_{yy}\neq 0
holds, the order three reduced determining equations are

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xxx}=\overline{X}{}_{xxy}=\overline{X}{}_{xyy}=\overline{X}{}_{yyy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{xxx}=\overline{Y}{}_{xxy}=\overline{Y}{}_{xyy}=\overline{Y}{}_{yyy}=0,

\displaystyle\overline{U}{}_{yyy}=\frac{u_{yyy}}{u_{yy}}\,\overline{U}{}_{yy},

and the involutivity test
{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=8\neq\overline{\mathsf{r}}{}_{3}=9
fails, as does
{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(2)}}=4\neq\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{3}=3
.
On the other hand, omitting the computational details, the reduced determining equations become involutive at order
\overline{n}{}_{\star}=3
with

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{3}^{(1)}}=6,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{3}^{(2)}}=3,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{3}^{(1)}}=3,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{3}^{(2)}}=0,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{3}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{3}^{(2)}}=12=\overline{\mathsf{r}}{}_{4},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{3}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{3}^{(2)}}=3=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{4}.

Remark 5.17
.

According to Theorem
5.12
, the conditions (
5.18
) on the Cartan characters eventually hold whenever the Lie pseudo-group is reducible. We note that (
5.18
) may also hold for some non-reducible pseudo-groups, and that these equalities imply the involutivity of the associated determining equations. Indeed, assume (
5.18
) holds for all
n\geq n_{\diamond}\geq n_{\star}
, for some natural number
n_{\diamond}
. First, (
4.6
) and (
5.16
), together with (
5.18
), imply
\mathsf{d}_{n}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{n}
. Similarly, at order
n+1
we have
\mathsf{d}_{n+1}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{n+1}
. Combining the last equality with (
5.18
), we conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{p}\,i\,{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\,a\,{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{n}^{(a)}}=\mathsf{d}_{n+1}=\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}_{n+1}.

Thus, the reduced determining equations
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}
satisfy the algebraic involutivity test. Moreover, since
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
is involutive,
\pi^{n+1}_{n}(\mathcal{G}^{(n+1)})=\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
, which implies
\pi^{n+1}_{n}(\mathcal{H}^{(n+1)})=\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
. Then, using (
5.5
),

\displaystyle\overline{\pi}{}^{n+1}_{n}\big(\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n+1)}\big)

\displaystyle=\overline{\pi}{}^{n+1}_{n}\big(\mathfrak{r}^{n+1}(\mathcal{H}^{(n+1)})\big)=\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}\big(\pi^{n+1}_{n}(\mathcal{H}^{(n+1)})\big)=\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}(\mathcal{H}^{(n)})=\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)},

which thereby proves involutivity of the reduced determining equations
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}
.

We now illustrate the remark with an example.

Example 5.18
.

Consider the pseudo-group action

X=x+a,\hskip 20.00003ptU=\lambda\,u+f(x),

where
f
is an analytic scalar function, while
a,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\lambda\neq 0
. Up to order two, the determining equations are

X_{x}=1,\hskip 10.00002ptX_{u}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptX_{xx}=X_{xu}=X_{uu}=U_{xu}=U_{uu}=0.

These equations are involutive with indices and Cartan characters

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}=3,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}=2,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}=1,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}=0.

(5.23)

The number of parametric pseudo-group jet coordinates of order
\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
is
\mathsf{d}^{(k)}=k+3
.

On the other hand, assuming
u=u(x)
, the reduced determining equations of order
\leq 2
are

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{x}=1,

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xx}=0.

At order two, the reduced index and reduced Cartan character are

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}=1,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{2}^{(1)}}=1,

(5.24)

while the dimension of the reduced pseudo-group jet bundles are
\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(k)}=k+2
. Since
\overline{\mathsf{d}}{}^{(k)}<\mathsf{d}^{(k)}
, the pseudo-group is non-reducible. But (
5.23
) and (
5.24
) satisfy (
5.18
) when
n\geq 2
.

6
Reduced Moving Frames and Normal Forms.

In this section, we review the moving frame construction for infinite-dimensional Lie pseudo-groups, as originally introduced in
[
57
]
. Restricting ourselves to reducible Lie pseudo-groups, we will work with the reduced pseudo-group jets rather than the original jets, keeping in mind that when the pseudo-group is reducible, they are in one-to-one correspondence.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a reducible Lie pseudo-group acting on (local) sections
s=\{(x,u(x))\}
of the bundle
\pi\colon M\to\mathcal{X}
. For transformations near the identity
\mathds{1}_{M}
, the transformed submanifold
S=\varphi(s)
remains a section. The prolonged action on the
n
-th order submanifold jet space
{\rm J}{}^{n}
is obtained by applying the
implicit total derivative operators

\mathrm{D}_{X^{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}W^{j}_{i}\,\mathrm{D}_{x^{j}},\hskip 20.00003pti=1,\ldots,p,

(6.1)

where
(W^{j}_{i})=(\overline{X}{}^{i}_{j})^{-1}
denotes the entries of the inverse reduced total Jacobian matrix (which can be simplified using the determining equations), to the reduced target dependent variables
\widehat{U}^{\alpha}=\overline{U}{}^{\alpha}
:

\widehat{U}^{\alpha}_{J}=\mathrm{D}_{X}^{J}\widehat{U}^{\alpha}=\mathrm{D}_{X^{j_{1}}}\cdots\mathrm{D}_{X^{j_{k}}}\widehat{U}^{\alpha}.

(6.2)

If
\overline{g}{}^{(n)}
denotes the
parametric
reduced pseudo-group parameters of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}
, then, as a consequence of formula (
6.1
) for the implicit total derivative operators, the prolonged action (
6.2
) can be written in terms of the submanifold jet coordinates
(x,u^{(n)})
and the parametric reduced pseudo-group parameters
\overline{g}{}^{(n)}
:

(\overline{X}{},\widehat{U}^{(n)})=P^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)},\overline{g}{}^{(n)}).

(6.3)

Example 6.1
.

We compute the prolonged action for the Lie pseudo-group (
4.8
) acting on surfaces
u=u(x,y)
. We streamline the computations by taking the reduced determining equations (
5.7
) into account. In particular, we recall that the reduced parametric pseudo-group jet coordinates are given in (
5.13
).
Thus, the lifted total derivative operators (
6.1
) are

\mathrm{D}_{X}=\frac{1}{\overline{X}{}_{x}}\,\mathrm{D}_{x}-\frac{\overline{Y}{}_{x}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}}\,\mathrm{D}_{y}=\frac{1}{\overline{X}{}_{x}}\bigl[\,{\mathrm{D}_{x}+(u-\overline{U}{})\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{y}}\,\bigr],\hskip 20.00003pt\mathrm{D}_{Y}=\frac{1}{\overline{X}{}_{x}}\,\mathrm{D}_{y},

and the coordinate expressions for the prolonged action up to order two are found to be

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{X}

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{U}{}_{x}+(u-\overline{U}{})\,\overline{U}{}_{y}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}},\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{U}_{Y}=\frac{\overline{U}{}_{y}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}},

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{XX}

\displaystyle=\!\frac{\overline{U}{}_{xx}+(u_{y}-\overline{U}{}_{y})\,\overline{U}{}_{x}+(u_{x}-\overline{U}{}_{x})\,\overline{U}{}_{y}+(u-\overline{U}{})\bigl({2\overline{U}{}_{xy}+2(u-\overline{U}{})u_{yy}+(u_{y}-\overline{U}{}_{y})\,\overline{U}{}_{x}}\bigr)}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{XY}

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{U}{}_{xy}+(u_{y}-\overline{U}{}_{y})\,\overline{U}{}_{y}+(u-\overline{U}{})u_{yy}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}},\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{U}_{YY}=\frac{u_{yy}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}}.

(6.4)

We will also use the following third order expressions

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{XYY}

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{xyy}+2(u_{y}-\overline{U}{}_{y})u_{yy}+(u-\overline{U}{})u_{yyy}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{3}},\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{U}_{YYY}=\frac{u_{yyy}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{3}};

(6.5)

the other two, i.e.,
\widehat{U}_{XXX}
,
\widehat{U}_{XXY}
, are more complicated and not required. Observe that, as stated in (
6.3
), the resulting formulas only depend on the reduced parametric pseudo-group parameters and the submanifold jet coordinates.

We are now ready to introduce the notion of a reduced moving frame.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{\overline{H}{}}^{(n)}\to{\rm J}{}^{n}
denote the lifted subgroupoid obtained by pulling back
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}\to M
to
{\rm J}{}^{n}
.
A
reduced moving frame

\overline{\rho}{}^{(n)}
of order
n
is a
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}
equivariant local section
\overline{\rho}{}^{(n)}\colon{\rm J}{}^{n}\to\mathcal{\overline{H}{}}^{(n)}
.

Remark 6.3
.

The moving frame introduced in Definition
6.2
differs from the original definition given in
[
57
]
since it is based on the prolonged action of the reduced pseudo-group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
rather than the original pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
. For non-reducible Lie pseudo-group actions, the two notions differ, whereas, as we now explain, for reducible pseudo-groups they are equivalent. We will discuss the explicit construction of a reduced moving frame through the choice of a cross-section to the pseudo-group orbits in Section
6.2
below.

In the original implementation
[
57
]
, a moving frame exists at order
n
provided the prolonged action is regular and (locally) free, as specified in the following definition.

Definition 6.4
.

The pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
is said to act
freely
at a submanifold jets
z^{(n)}\in{\rm J}{}^{n}
if its isotropy group
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,g^{(n)}\in\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;g^{(n)}\cdot z^{(n)}=z^{(n)}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,g^{(n)}\in\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\hbox{}\,|\,g^{(n)}\cdot z^{(n)}=z^{(n)}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{g^{(n)}\in\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\hbox{}\,|\,g^{(n)}\cdot z^{(n)}=z^{(n)}\hbox{}\}}{\{g^{(n)}\in\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\hbox{}\,|\,g^{(n)}\cdot z^{(n)}=z^{(n)}\hbox{}\}}
is trivial, which means that
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;\pi^{n}_{0}(z^{(n)})=z\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}\hbox{}\,|\,\pi^{n}_{0}(z^{(n)})=z\hbox{}\,\}}{\{\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}\hbox{}\,|\,\pi^{n}_{0}(z^{(n)})=z\hbox{}\}}{\{\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}\hbox{}\,|\,\pi^{n}_{0}(z^{(n)})=z\hbox{}\}}
, i.e., the only pseudo-group jet fixing
z^{(n)}
is the identity. More generally, the pseudo-group acts
locally freely
at
z^{(n)}
if
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
is a discrete group.

Once the pseudo-group acts (locally) freely
5
5

5

In general, one expects a subvariety of singular jets in
{\rm J}{}^{n}
where the prolonged action is not locally free.
on an open subset
V^{(n)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}
for some
n
, persistence of freeness,
[
57
,
59
]
, implies that
\mathcal{G}
acts freely on the open subset
V^{(n+k)}=(\pi^{n+k})^{-1}V^{(n)}
. We now observe that freeness of the prolonged action implies reducibility of the Lie pseudo-group action.

Theorem 6.5
.

If
\mathcal{G}
acts freely on the open subset
V^{(n)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},M)
then it is order
n
reducible on any section whose jet lies in
V^{(n)}
.

Proof.

Note that the identity reduced jet
\displaystyle\overline{\mathds{1}}{}^{(n)}_{z}
fixes any jet
z^{(n)}\in{\rm J}{}^{n}
, where
z=\pi^{n}_{0}(z^{(n)})
. Thus, because the action of
\mathcal{G}
on
{\rm J}{}^{n}
factors through the reduced action, each element of

(\mathfrak{r}^{(n)})^{-1}\{\overline{\mathds{1}}{}^{(n)}_{z}\}\,\cap\,\mathcal{H}^{(n)}

(6.6)

fixes
z^{(n)}
. If the action is not reducible, the subset (
6.6
) will contain non-identity jets, and hence the isotropy subgroup of
z^{(n)}
will be non-trivial.

Q.E.D.

Theorem
6.5
implies that once the prolonged action becomes free, the reduced prolonged action is also free, that is, the isotropy group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
is trivial. For a reducible Lie pseudo-group, the converse is also true.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be reducible on
z^{(n)}
. If the prolonged action of the reduced pseudo-group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
is free at
z^{(n)}
, then
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}
acts freely at
z^{(n)}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{G}
is reducible and
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}=\{\overline{\mathds{1}}{}_{z}^{(n)}\}
, the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
must also contain a single jet. Since
\displaystyle\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}\in\mathcal{G}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
, it follows that
\mathcal{G}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}=\{\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{z}\}
.

Q.E.D.

Theorems
6.5
and
6.6
imply that for reducible Lie pseudo-groups we can go back and forth between the construction of a moving frame for the original pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
and for the reduced pseudo-group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
. This allows us to state the main existence theorem for a reduced moving frame.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
act freely and regularly on the open set
V^{(n)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}(\mathcal{X},M)
. Then for any
z^{(n)}\in V^{(n)}
there exists a reduced moving frame of order
n
in a neighborhood
N^{(n)}\subset V^{(n)}
containing
z^{(n)}
.

6.1
Isotropy.

According to the preceding discussion, there are two types of isotropy of a submanifold jet — those where the reduced action fixes the jet, and, more restrictively, those with trivial reduced action. Let us characterize them for better understanding of the underlying geometry. Note that the observations in this subsection are not used in the subsequent developments, and can thus be skipped without loss of continuity.

Given the submanifold jet
z^{(n)}\in{\rm J}{}^{n}
, let
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{(n)}
denote its isotropy subgroup of order
n
, i.e., the set of
n
-jets of local diffeomorphisms which fix
z^{(n)}
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
be those isotropy elements which have trivial reduction. We can thus identify
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}\simeq(\mathfrak{r}^{(n)})^{-1}\{\overline{\mathds{1}}{}^{(n)}_{z}\}
where we are now applying the reduction map
\mathfrak{r}^{(n)}
— see (
5.2
) — to an arbitrary diffeomorphism jet. Let
\mathcal{Q}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}/\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
denote the quotient space.

We now investigate
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
,
\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
, and
\mathcal{Q}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
. By applying a suitable diffeomorphism, we can, without loss of generality, assume that our section
s
is, locally, the trivial zero section,
u(x)\equiv 0
, with zero
n
jet, so
z^{(n)}=0^{(n)}
. In this setting, a diffeomorphism
1
-jet
Z^{(1)}=(X^{(1)},U^{(1)})
belongs to
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}_{0^{(1)}}
if and only if

X=U=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm and}\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{aligned} \delta^{i}_{j}&=\overline{X}{}^{i}_{j}=X^{i}_{x^{j}}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{q}u^{\beta}_{j}X^{i}_{u^{\beta}}=X^{i}_{x^{j}},\\
0&=u^{\alpha}_{j}=\overline{U}{}^{\alpha}_{j}=U^{\alpha}_{x^{j}}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{q}u^{\beta}_{j}U^{\alpha}_{u^{\beta}}=U^{\alpha}_{x^{j}},\end{aligned}\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{aligned} i,j&=1,\ldots,p,\\
\alpha&=1,\ldots,q,\end{aligned}

where
\delta^{i}_{j}
is the Kronecker delta. On the other hand,
Z^{(1)}\in\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{0^{(1)}}
if and only if
X=U=\widehat{U}^{\alpha}_{X^{j}}=\mathrm{D}_{X^{j}}(U^{\alpha})=0
, with
j=1,\ldots,p
, and
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
. Since the matrix
(W^{i}_{j})
in the definition of the total derivative operators (
6.1
) is invertible, the constraints for
Z^{(1)}
to be in
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{0^{(1)}}
are

X=U=0,\hskip 20.00003pt0=u^{\alpha}_{j}=\overline{U}{}^{\alpha}_{j}=U^{\alpha}_{x^{j}}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{q}u^{\beta}_{j}U^{\alpha}_{u^{\beta}}=U^{\alpha}_{x^{j}},\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{aligned} j&=1,\ldots,p,\\
\alpha&=1,\ldots,q.\end{aligned}

By similar computations,
Z^{(n)}\in\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}
if and only if

X=U=0,\hskip 10.00002ptX^{i}_{x^{j}}=\delta^{i}_{j},\hskip 20.00003ptX^{i}_{J}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt|J|\geq 2,\hskip 20.00003ptU^{\alpha}_{K}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt|K|\geq 1,

while
Z^{(n)}\in\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}
if and only if

X=U=0,\hskip 20.00003ptU^{\alpha}_{K}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt|K|\geq 1.

In other words, at
x=0
,
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}
consists of
n
-jets of diffeomorphisms of the form

\displaystyle X=f(x,u),

\displaystyle U=u\mskip 2.0mug(x,u),

\displaystyle f(0,0)=0,

\displaystyle\det(f^{i}_{x^{j}})|_{(0,0)}\neq 0,

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{q}g^{\alpha}(0,0)\neq 0,

while
\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}
consists of
n
jets of diffeomorphisms of the form

\displaystyle X=x+u\mskip 2.0muh(x,u),

\displaystyle U=u\mskip 2.0mug(x,u),

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{q}g^{\alpha}(0,0)\neq 0.

In particular, on the zero section, we have
X=x
and hence
\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}
consists of
n
-jets of diffeomorphisms which fix every single point of
s
, i.e., the jets of the global isotropy subgroup of
s
. On the other hand, the quotient space
\mathcal{Q}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}=\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}/\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{0^{(n)}}
can be identified with the space of local diffeomorphisms of the form

\displaystyle X=a(x),

\displaystyle U=u

with

\displaystyle a(0)=0,

\displaystyle\det(a^{i}_{j})(0)\neq 0.

These are just the reparametrizations of the zero section, which are extended to be diffeomorphisms with identical reparametrizations of the parallel sections, although the method of extension is unimportant and just selects a particular representative of the quotient space.

Thus, pseudo-groups whose reduced action is free differ from freely acting pseudo-groups only by the inclusion of some additional transformations that belong to the global isotropy subgroup of the section and/or perform reparametrizations. These all preserve the section, and thus do not affect the moving frame calculation nor the computations of differential invariants.

Example 6.8
.

Suppose
p=q=1
, and consider the Lie pseudo-group action

\displaystyle X=x+a,

\displaystyle U=f(x,u),

(6.7)

where
f_{u}\neq 0
. Since the reduced parametric pseudo-group jet coordinates are
\overline{X}{}
,
\overline{U}{}_{x^{n}}
,
n\geq 0
, and the prolonged action is
\widehat{U}_{X^{n}}=\overline{U}{}_{x^{n}}
, this pseudo-group admits a free reduced action. On the other hand, the pseudo-group (
6.7
) does not act freely anywhere on the jet space
{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
. When
\displaystyle p=1,

\displaystyle q=2
, the extended pseudo-group

\displaystyle X=x+a,

\displaystyle U=f(x,u),

\displaystyle V=v+b,

(6.8)

is of the same form, and furthermore is intransitive and so has nontrivial differential invariants, namely
v_{x^{n}}
for all
n\geq 1
, despite the fact that it does not act freely. On the other hand, when
\displaystyle p=2,

\displaystyle q=1
, the same pseudo-group

\displaystyle X=x+a,

\displaystyle Y=y+b,

\displaystyle U=f(x,u),

(6.9)

acts freely and transitively on the subset of jet space where
u_{y}\neq 0
at all orders
\geq 1
. We note that the pseudo-groups (
6.7
) and (
6.8
) are not reducible, while (
6.9
) is reducible by virtue of Theorem
6.5
.

6.2
The Reduced Moving Frame Construction.

Coming back to the construction of a reduced moving frame, this is accomplished by selecting a cross-section
\mathcal{K}^{(n)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}
that is transversal to the orbits of the prolonged group action (
6.2
). As in most applications, we will always assume that
\mathcal{K}^{(n)}
is a coordinate cross-section defined by fixing
\mathsf{d}^{(n)}
values of the individual jet coordinates
z^{(n)}=(x,u^{(n)})
to suitable constants. Let

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\subset\mathchoice{\left\{\,i,\ (\alpha;J)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 10pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr i=1,\ldots,p,&\quad$\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q,&\quad$\displaystyle|J|\leq n\cr}}}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,i,\ (\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 10pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr i=1,\ldots,p,&\quad$\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q,&\quad$\displaystyle|J|\leq n\cr}}}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{i,\ (\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 10pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr i=1,\ldots,p,&\quad$\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q,&\quad$\displaystyle|J|\leq n\cr}}}\hbox{}\}}{\{i,\ (\alpha;J)\hbox{}\,|\,{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 10pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr i=1,\ldots,p,&\quad$\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q,&\quad$\displaystyle|J|\leq n\cr}}}\hbox{}\}}

(6.10)

denote the set of indices of jet coordinates of order
\leq n
that determine the cross-section, which is thus prescribed by
\mathsf{d}^{(n)}=\#\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}
equations, of the form

\mathcal{K}^{(n)}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,x^{i}=c^{i},\;u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;i,\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,x^{i}=c^{i},\;u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{x^{i}=c^{i},\;u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\}}{\{x^{i}=c^{i},\;u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\}},

(6.11)

for suitable constants
c^{i},c^{\alpha}_{J}
.

Given a cross-section (
6.11
), the
reduced
(
right
)
moving frame
6
6

6

By an abuse of notation, we use the same symbol to denote the pseudo-group normalization function and the corresponding moving frame section in Definition
6.2
. Also, in (
6.12
) the moving frame only specifies the parametric reduced pseudo-group jets, the principal pseudo-group parameters being determined by the reduced determining equations (
5.6
).

\overline{g}{}^{(n)}=\overline{\rho}{}^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)})

(6.12)

gives the reduced pseudo-group element that maps a submanifold jet
(x,u^{(n)})
belonging to a suitable neighborhood of the cross-section to the cross-section jet
(\overline{X}{},\widehat{U}^{(n)})\in\mathcal{K}^{(n)}
that lies in the same pseudo-group orbit. The freeness assumption in Theorem
6.7
guarantees that the reduced pseudo-group element (
6.12
) is uniquely determined.

To explicitly determine the moving frame, we apply the cross-section normalizations, in the form
\displaystyle\overline{X}{}^{i}=c^{i},

\displaystyle\widehat{U}^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}
, to the corresponding components of the formulas (
6.3
) for the prolonged pseudo-group action, and solve the resulting algebraic equations

P^{i}(x,u^{(n)},\overline{g}{}^{(n)})=c^{i},\hskip 20.00003ptP^{\alpha}_{J}(x,u^{(n)},\overline{g}{}^{(n)})=c^{\alpha}_{J},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003pti,\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)},

(6.13)

for the reduced pseudo-group parameters. Transversality of the cross-section and freeness of the reduced action guarantee, via the Implicit Function Theorem, that the normalization equations (
6.13
) can be locally solved for
\overline{g}{}^{(n)}
near the cross-section, thereby producing the reduced moving frame (
6.12
).
Furthermore, substituting the moving frame expressions (
6.12
) into the formulas (
6.3
) for the prolonged action produces the
normalized differential invariants
. Those corresponding to the cross-section coordinates, namely

\displaystyle c^{i}=P^{i}(x,u^{(n)},\overline{\rho}{}^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)})),

\displaystyle c^{\alpha}_{J}=P^{\alpha}_{J}(x,u^{(n)},\overline{\rho}{}^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)})),

\displaystyle i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)},

reduce, by construction, to the normalization constants, and are known as the
phantom differential invariants
, whereas the remaining functions

{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle\hfil#$&$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 20pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr H^{j}(x,u^{(n)})\;&=P^{j}(x,u^{(n)},\overline{\rho}{}^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)})),\cr I^{\beta}_{K}(x,u^{(n)})\;&=P^{\beta}_{K}(x,u^{(n)},\overline{\rho}{}^{(n)}(x,u^{(n)})),\cr}}}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003ptj,\;(\beta;K)\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)},

(6.14)

form a complete system of functionally independent differential invariants of order
\leq n
, known as the
basic normalized differential invariants
, although in what follows “basic” will often be dropped.

Example 6.9
.

Returning to our running example, under the assumption that
u_{yy}>0
, a possible cross-section to the second order prolonged action (
6.4
) is given by

\mathcal{K}^{(2)}=\{\,x=0,\,y=0,\,u=c_{0},\,u_{x}=c_{1},\,u_{y}=d_{0},\,u_{xx}=c_{2},\,u_{xy}=d_{1},\,u_{yy}=1\,\},

(6.15)

where
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},d_{0},d_{1}
are arbitrary constants. More generally,

\mathcal{K}^{(\infty)}=\{\,x=0,\,y=0,\,u_{yy}=1,\,u_{x^{k}}=c_{k},\,u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptk\geq 0\,\}.

(6.16)

Following the original papers
[
57
,
58
]
, and to simplify the computations, we set the arbitrary constants to zero, i.e.,
c_{k}=d_{k}=0
, when computing the moving frame.

Referring to the formulas (
6.4
) for the prolonged action, the normalization equations, up to order two, are obtained by substituting the cross-section determining equations (
6.15
) into the prolonged action:

\displaystyle 0=\overline{X}{},

\displaystyle 0=\overline{Y}{},

\displaystyle 0=\widehat{U}=\overline{U}{},

\displaystyle 0=\widehat{U}_{X}=\frac{\overline{U}{}_{x}+(u-\overline{U}{})\overline{U}{}_{y}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}},

\displaystyle 0=\widehat{U}_{Y}=\frac{\overline{U}{}_{y}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}},

\displaystyle 0=\widehat{U}_{XX}=\frac{\displaystyle{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 97.5pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr\overline{U}{}_{xx}-(u_{y}-\overline{U}{}_{y})\,\overline{U}{}_{x}+(u_{x}-\overline{U}{}_{x})\,\overline{U}{}_{y}\cr\hskip 97.5pt{}+(u-\overline{U}{})(2\mskip 2.0mu\overline{U}{}_{xy}+2(u-\overline{U}{})u_{yy}+(u_{y}-\overline{U}{}_{y})\,\overline{U}{}_{x})\cr}}}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}},

\displaystyle 0=\widehat{U}_{XY}=\frac{\overline{U}{}_{xy}+(u_{y}-\overline{U}{}_{y})\,\overline{U}{}_{y}+(u-\overline{U}{})u_{yy}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}},\hskip 20.00003pt1=\widehat{U}_{YY}=\frac{u_{yy}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}}.

(6.17)

Solving these equations for the reduced pseudo-group parameters yields the reduced second order moving frame

\begin{gathered}\overline{X}{}=0,\qquad\overline{Y}{}=0,\qquad\overline{U}{}=0,\qquad\overline{X}{}_{x}=\sqrt{u_{yy}},\qquad\overline{U}{}_{x}=0,\qquad\overline{U}{}_{y}=0,\\
\overline{U}{}_{xx}=0,\qquad\overline{U}{}_{xy}=-\mskip 2.0muuu_{yy}.\end{gathered}

(6.18)

Substituting the pseudo-group normalizations (
6.18
) into the right hand side of the formulas (
6.5
) produces the third order normalized differential invariants

I_{1,2}=\frac{u_{xyy}+uu_{yyy}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{y}u_{yy}}{u_{yy}^{3/2}},\hskip 20.00003ptI_{0,3}=\frac{u_{yyy}}{u_{yy}^{3/2}}.

(6.19)

Remark 6.10
.

Since the prolonged pseudo-group transformations (
6.3
) only depend on the reduced pseudo-group jets, the moving frame method applies equally well to non-free actions whose reduced action is eventually free. However, we have, as yet, been unable to come up with any truly interesting examples, beyond the rather trivial ones that are based on Example
6.8
. Therefore, as in almost all other treatments of moving frames, we have restricted our attention to pseudo-groups which act freely on an open subset of jet space of suitably high order.

7
Normal Forms.

As shown in
[
57
, Section 8]
(see also
[
55
]
), the method of (reduced) moving frames can naturally be formulated in terms of power series. As explained in Section
2
, we can identify a submanifold jet
(x,u^{(\infty)})\in{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
with a formal power series

u^{\alpha}(y)=\sum_{J}\>\frac{u^{\alpha}_{J}}{J!}\,(y-x)^{J},\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha=1,\ldots,q,

(7.1)

centered at the point
x\in\mathcal{X}
. By definition, the power series converges to an analytic function in a neighborhood of
x
if and only if
(x,u^{(\infty)})\in A^{\infty}
.

Definition 7.1
.

Given an infinite order coordinate cross-section
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{(\infty)}\subset{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
, a power series (
7.1
) is said to be in
normal form
if the corresponding submanifold jet lies in the cross-section:
(x,u^{(\infty)})\in\mathcal{K}
.

Thus, the normal form will depend upon the choice of cross-section.
In particular, the normal form power series converges to an analytic function if and only if the corresponding jet
(x,u^{(\infty)})\in\mathcal{K}\,\cap\,A^{\infty}
lies in the analytic part of the infinite order cross-section. In general, the moving frame method does not make any guarantees that this occurs and so such normal forms are merely formal power series. The main result of this paper is to establish convergence of normal form power series under suitable assumptions, which include most examples that arise in applications.

In a little more detail, as in (
6.10
), let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
denote the set of indices
i
,
(\alpha;J)
of jet coordinates that prescribe the coordinate cross-section (
6.11
) at order
n=\infty
.
Thus, the coefficients
u^{\alpha}_{J}
with
(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
represent the normalization constants prescribed by the cross-section, i.e., the phantom invariants, which serve to fix the normal form power series. The remaining coefficients
u^{\beta}_{K}
with
(\beta;K)\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
will represent the corresponding complete set of basic normalized differential invariants, as described below. We further set

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\alpha}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,J\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,J\hbox{}\,|\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{J\hbox{}\,|\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\}}{\{J\hbox{}\,|\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha=1,\ldots,q.

(7.2)

We can then extract from (
7.1
) the
cross-section power series

C^{\alpha}(y)=\sum_{J\,\in\,\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\alpha}}\>\frac{c^{\alpha}_{J}}{J!}\,(y-x)^{J},\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha=1,\ldots,q,

(7.3)

whose indicated Taylor coefficients are the normalization constants, i.e., the phantom invariants. If
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\alpha}
is a finite set, then
C^{\alpha}(y)
is a polynomial, while if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\alpha}=\mathchar 0\relax
, our convention is that
C^{\alpha}(y)
does not exist.

Consider two sections
s,S\subset M
of the fibered manifold
\pi\colon M\to\mathcal{X}
. In local coordinates, the “source section” has the form
s=\{(x,u(x))\}
, while the “target section” is given by
S=\{(X,\widehat{U}(X))\}
.
We will assume that the source section represents the normal form, meaning that its jet
(x,u^{(\infty)})\in\mathcal{K}
lies in the cross-section. On the other hand, the target section will be a prescribed analytic section that we seek to normalize via a suitable pseudo-group diffeomorphism. In other words, we seek a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
such that, locally,
S=\varphi(s)
. In terms of the reduced pseudo-group, this requires

\overline{U}{}=\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or, more explicitly,}\hskip 20.00003ptU(x,u(x))=\widehat{U}(X(x,u(x))).

(7.4)

Warning
: This is the opposite point of view from that was used in Section
6.2
to construct the moving frame, where the target submanifold belonged to the cross-section. Thus, to be in alignment with our current point of view, we should switch the source and target coordinates in the constructions. While the calculations could clearly be implemented in this manner from the outset, using a suitable change in notation, in our view the resulting notations are confusing and are at odds with the traditional way moving frames are constructed for both finite-dimensional Lie group actions and infinite-dimensional pseudo-groups. We will explicitly note when the required switch is necessary.
In addition, it is preferable, due to other notational considerations, to make the normal form the source submanifold.

Thus, given a prescribed section with analytic power series

\widehat{U}^{\alpha}(Y)=\sum_{J}\>\frac{\widehat{U}^{\alpha}_{J}}{J!}\,(Y-X)^{J},\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha=1,\ldots,q,

(7.5)

centered at the point
X\in\mathcal{X}
,
the moving frame will map it to a normal form power series (
7.1
) whose phantom coefficients are constants and whose remaining coefficients are the basic differential invariants, expressed in terms of the jet coordinates
X^{i},\widehat{U}^{\alpha}_{J}
. In other words, if
I^{\alpha}_{J}(x,u^{(n)})
is a normalized differential invariant, then the corresponding coefficient in the normal form power series (
7.1
) is
u^{\alpha}_{J}=I^{\alpha}_{J}(X,\widehat{U}^{(n)})
. In view of (
7.3
), the normal form power series thus takes the form

u^{\alpha}(y)=C^{\alpha}(y)+\sum_{J\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\alpha}}\>\frac{I^{\alpha}_{J}}{J!}\,(y-x)^{J}=\sum_{J\,\in\,\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\alpha}}\>\frac{c^{\alpha}_{J}}{J!}\,(y-x)^{J}+\sum_{J\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{\alpha}}\>\frac{I^{\alpha}_{J}}{J!}\,(y-x)^{J},\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha=1,\ldots,q.

(7.6)

The key issue to be addressed in this paper is whether the resulting normal form power series (
7.6
) converges. In general this is not the case — for instance, it is trivially not convergent if the cross-section power series
C^{\alpha}(y)
do not converge — and an additional requirement must be imposed. Namely,
the coordinate cross-section must be “well-posed”, as formulated in Section
8.4
, and the corresponding cross-section power series (
7.3
) must converge. Fortunately, choosing a well-posed cross-section is not difficult, and leads to a practical algorithm for constructing convergent normal forms for reducible submanifolds.

Example 7.2
.

For our running example, the normal form corresponding to the cross-section (
6.16
) is the (formal) Taylor series at the origin corresponding to the function

u(x,y)=c(x)+y\mskip 2.0mud(x)+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mskip 2.0mu{y^{2}}\mskip 2.0muw(x,y),

(7.7)

where
c_{k},d_{k}
are the Taylor coefficients, at
x=0
, for the scalar functions
c(x),d(x)
, respectively, and where
w(0,0)=1
. In this example, the cross-section power series is

C(x,y)=c(x)+y\mskip 2.0mud(x)+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mskip 2.0mu{y^{2}}.

(7.8)

Except for the constant term, the Taylor coefficients of
w(x,y)
are the normalized differential invariants. In the calculations of Example
6.9
, we took
c(x)\equiv d(x)\equiv 0
, in which case, the third order invariants (
6.19
) are (up to multiple) the coefficients of the linear terms in
w(x,y)
. In this case, the first few terms of the normal form power series are

u(x,y)=\frac{y^{2}}{2}+\frac{I_{1,2}}{2}\,x\mskip 2.0muy^{2}+\frac{I_{0,3}}{6}\,y^{3}+\frac{I_{2,2}}{4}\,x^{2}\mskip 2.0muy^{2}+\frac{I_{1,3}}{6}\,x\mskip 2.0muy^{3}+\frac{I_{0,4}}{24}\,y^{4}+\ \cdots\ ,

where

I_{1,2}=\frac{\widehat{U}_{XYY}+\widehat{U}\widehat{U}_{YYY}+2\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{Y}\widehat{U}_{YY}}{\widehat{U}_{YY}^{3/2}},\hskip 20.00003ptI_{0,3}=\frac{\widehat{U}_{YYY}}{\widehat{U}_{YY}^{3/2}},

(7.9)

are the third order differential invariants (
6.19
) evaluated on the target section
U=\widehat{U}(X,Y)
, while
I_{2,2},I_{1,3},I_{0,4}
are the normalized fourth order differential invariants, again evaluated on the target section, whose explicit formulae can be deduced from
[
57
, Example 8.6]
. And similarly at higher order.

7.1
The Normal Form Determining Equations.

We now formulate a system of differential equations that a normal form must satisfy. These equations are obtained by suitably manipulating the reduced determining equations for the pseudo-group. As noted above, the normal form is denoted by source coordinates, i.e.,
u=u(x)
, while the prescribed submanifold is written in target coordinates as in (
7.4
).

Consider the reduced determining equations

\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}=\big\{\,\overline{\Delta}{}^{(n)}\big(x,u^{(n)},\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{U}{}^{(n)}\big)=0\,\big\}

(7.10)

for the reduced pseudo-group diffeomorphism
\overline{\varphi}{}(x)=\bigl(\,{\overline{X}{}(x,u(x)),\overline{U}{}(x,u(x))}\,\bigr)
evaluated on a section
u(x)
. Recall that
u^{(n)},\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{U}{}^{(n)}
denote derivatives with respect to the source variables
x
up to order
n
. Applying the chain rule to differentiate the first equation in (
7.4
) yields formulae for the
x
derivatives of
\overline{U}{}
in terms of the
x
derivatives of
\overline{X}{}
and the
X
derivatives of
\widehat{U}
:

\overline{U}{}^{(n)}=\overline{\mathfrak{U}}{}^{(n)}(\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\widehat{U}^{(n)}),

(7.11)

where
\widehat{U}^{(n)}
denotes the derivatives of
\widehat{U}
with respect to the target independent variables
X
up to order
n
. These formulae can be explicitly computed by successively applying the
chain rule total derivative operators

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{x^{i}}=\sum_{j\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\overline{X}{}^{j}_{x^{i}}\,\mathrm{D}_{X^{j}},

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,p,

(7.12)

to each
\widehat{U}^{\alpha}
.
For example, when
p=q=1
, we have
\mathrm{D}_{x}=\overline{X}{}_{x}\,\mathrm{D}_{X}
, and hence, up to order two,

\overline{U}{}_{x}=\widehat{U}_{X}\overline{X}{}_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{U}{}_{xx}=\widehat{U}_{XX}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}+\widehat{U}_{X}\overline{X}{}_{xx}.

Substituting the expressions (
7.11
) into the reduced determining equations
(
7.10
) produces the
normal form determining equations

\mathcal{N}^{(n)}=\big\{\widetilde{\Delta}^{(n)}\big(x,u^{(n)},\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\widehat{U}^{(n)}\big)=0\big\}.

(7.13)

Given a prescribed function
\widehat{U}=\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{})
defining a submanifold (section), whose derivatives
\widehat{U}^{(n)}
are known, we view (
7.13
) as an
n
-th order system of differential equations for the unknown functions
\overline{X}{}(x),u(x)
, the latter, when subject to the appropriate initial conditions, prescribing the normal form of the given submanifold, and our goal is to establish their involutivity.

Example 7.3
.

To illustrate the construction, let us compute the normal form determining equations for the Lie pseudo-group (
4.8
). We begin by applying the chain rule total differential operators

\displaystyle\text{D}_{x}=\overline{X}{}_{x}\text{D}_{X}+\overline{Y}{}_{x}\text{D}_{Y},

\displaystyle\text{D}_{y}=\overline{X}{}_{y}\text{D}_{X}+\overline{Y}{}_{y}\text{D}_{Y},

(7.14)

once and twice to the equation
\overline{U}{}=\widehat{U}
to produce the first and second order chain rule formulas

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{U}{}_{x}\;

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{X}\overline{X}{}_{x}+\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{Y}{}_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{U}{}_{y}=\widehat{U}_{X}\overline{X}{}_{y}+\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{Y}{}_{y},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{U}{}_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{XX}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}+2\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{XY}\overline{X}{}_{x}\overline{Y}{}_{x}+\widehat{U}_{YY}\overline{Y}{}_{x}^{2}+\widehat{U}_{X}\overline{X}{}_{xx}+\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{Y}{}_{xx},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{U}{}_{xy}\;

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{XX}\overline{X}{}_{x}\overline{X}{}_{y}+\widehat{U}_{XY}(\overline{X}{}_{x}\overline{Y}{}_{y}+\overline{X}{}_{y}\overline{Y}{}_{x})+\widehat{U}_{YY}\overline{Y}{}_{x}\overline{Y}{}_{y}+\widehat{U}_{X}\overline{X}{}_{xy}+\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{Y}{}_{xy},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{U}{}_{yy}\;

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{XX}\overline{X}{}_{y}^{2}+2\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{XY}\overline{X}{}_{y}\overline{Y}{}_{y}+\widehat{U}_{YY}\overline{Y}{}_{y}^{2}+\widehat{U}_{X}\overline{X}{}_{yy}+\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{Y}{}_{yy}.

(7.15)

We substitute these into the reduced determining equations (
5.7
). The resulting equations, once simplified, are the normal form determining equations

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{X}{}_{y}\;

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{Y}{}_{x}\;

\displaystyle=(\widehat{U}-u)\overline{X}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{Y}{}_{y}\;

\displaystyle=\overline{X}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{X}{}_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}-u_{y}\overline{X}{}_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}_{xy}=\overline{X}{}_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{Y}{}_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=\bigl({\widehat{U}_{X}+2\mskip 2.0mu(\widehat{U}-u)\widehat{U}_{Y}}\bigr)\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}-\bigl({u_{x}+(\widehat{U}-u)u_{y}}\bigr)\overline{X}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{Y}{}_{xy}\;

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}-u_{y}\overline{X}{}_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{Y}{}_{yy}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptu_{yy}=\widehat{U}_{YY}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}.

(7.16)

Observe that the parametric derivatives are
\overline{X}{},\overline{Y}{},u,\overline{X}{}_{x},u_{x},u_{y},u_{xx},u_{xy}
, while the left hand sides are the principal derivatives.

For later use in Example
8.5
, we further compute the normal form determining equations of order three. The most direct way to perform this computation is to apply the chain rule operators (
7.14
) to directly differentiate the second order equations (
7.16
). The result is

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{X}{}_{xxx}\;

\displaystyle=(u_{y}^{2}-u_{xy})\mskip 2.0mu\overline{X}{}_{x}-3\mskip 2.0muu_{y}\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}+(\widehat{U}_{XY}+2\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{Y}^{2}+(\widehat{U}-u)\widehat{U}_{YY})\mskip 2.0mu\overline{X}{}_{x}^{3},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{X}{}_{xxy}\;

\displaystyle=\overline{X}{}_{xyy}=\overline{X}{}_{yyy}=\overline{Y}{}_{xyy}=\overline{Y}{}_{yyy}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{Y}{}_{xxx}\;

\displaystyle=(2u_{x}u_{y}-u_{xx}+(\widehat{U}-u)(u_{y}^{2}-u_{xy}))\overline{X}{}_{x}-3\mskip 2.0mu(u_{x}\widehat{U}_{Y}+u_{y}\widehat{U}_{X}+2\mskip 2.0mu(\widehat{U}-u)u_{y}\widehat{U}_{Y})\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}\hskip-72.26999pt{}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt{}+(\widehat{U}_{XX}+4\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{X}\widehat{U}_{Y}+3\mskip 2.0mu(\widehat{U}-u)(\widehat{U}_{XY}+2\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{Y}^{2})+2(\widehat{U}-u)^{2}\widehat{U}_{YY})\overline{X}{}_{x}^{3},

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{Y}{}_{xxy}\;

\displaystyle=(u_{y}^{2}-u_{xy})\overline{X}{}_{x}-3\mskip 2.0muu_{y}\widehat{U}_{Y}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}+(\widehat{U}_{XY}+2\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{Y}^{2}+(\widehat{U}-u)\widehat{U}_{YY})\overline{X}{}_{x}^{3},

\displaystyle\hfil u_{xyy}\;

\displaystyle=-2\mskip 2.0muu_{y}\widehat{U}_{YY}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}+(\widehat{U}_{XYY}+2\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{Y}\widehat{U}_{YY}-u\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{YYY}+\widehat{U}\widehat{U}_{YYY})\overline{X}{}_{x}^{3},

\displaystyle\hfil u_{yyy}\;

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{YYY}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{3},

(7.17)

with parametric derivatives
\overline{X}{},\overline{Y}{},u,\overline{X}{}_{x},u_{x},u_{y},u_{xx},u_{xy},u_{xxx},u_{xxy}
.

To investigate involutivity of the normal form determining equations, we linearize at the identity jet, keeping in mind that
\overline{X}{}^{(n)}
and
u^{(n)}
vary, while
\widehat{U}^{(n)}
is fixed. The vector field used for linearization is

\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{J}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\>\psi^{\alpha}_{J}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}\bigg).

We begin by linearizing the chain rule formula (
7.11
), writing out its individual components.

Lemma 7.4
.

For any
\alpha=1,\ldots,q
and multi-index
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
, the linearization of the chain rule equation

\overline{U}{}^{\alpha}_{J}=\overline{\mathfrak{U}}{}^{\alpha}_{J}(\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\widehat{U}^{(n)})

(7.18)

at the identity jet is

\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}_{J}=\mathrm{D}_{x}^{J}\left(\,{\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>u^{\alpha}_{i}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}^{i}}\,\right)-\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>u^{\alpha}_{J,i}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}^{i}.

(7.19)

Proof
: Linearization at the identity amounts to computing the infinitesimal generator of a one parameter group. In the case of (
7.4
), the group can be identified with the induced action of the inverse of the change of independent variables prescribed by
X=\overline{X}{}(x)=X(x,u(x))
on the dependent variables
u
; for details, see the discussion on pages 105–106 of
[
50
]
. Because we are dealing with the inverse, the infinitesimal generator is

-\,\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\overline{\xi}{}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},

which only acts on the independent variables.
Linearizing the induced action on the derivatives (
7.18
) is the same as computing the prolongation of this vector field, which, according to
[
50
, Theorem 2.36]
is exactly given by the prolongation formula (
7.19
), the quantity in parentheses being its characteristic.

Q.E.D.

Example 7.5
.

Linearizing the particular chain rule formulas (
7.15
) at the identity, where (
5.9
) holds, produces

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}{}_{x}=u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=\mathrm{D}_{x}(u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{})-(u_{xx}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{xy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}),

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}{}_{y}=u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{y}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{y}=\mathrm{D}_{y}(u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{})-(u_{xy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{yy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}),

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}{}_{xx}=u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{xx}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{xx}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{xx}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{xy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}(u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{})-(u_{xxx}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{xxy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}),

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}{}_{xy}=u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{xy}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{xy}+u_{xx}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}+u_{xy}(\overline{\xi}{}_{y}+\overline{\eta}{}_{x})+u_{yy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=\mathrm{D}_{x}\mathrm{D}_{y}(u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{})\!-\!(u_{xxy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{xyy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}),\hskip-72.26999pt

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}{}_{yy}=u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{yy}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{yy}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{xy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{y}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{yy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}_{y}=\mathrm{D}_{y}^{2}(u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{})-(u_{xyy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}+u_{yyy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\eta}{}),

(7.20)

which are in accordance with the general formula (
7.19
).

Theorem 7.6
.

The linearization of the normal form determining equations (
7.13
) at the identity, where
(\overline{X}{},u^{(n)})=(x,\widehat{U}^{(n)})
, coincides with the linearization of the reduced determining equations (
7.10
) at the identity
(\overline{X}{},\overline{U}{}^{(n)})=(x,u^{(n)})
after the substitutions

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}_{J}\ \longmapsto\ \mathrm{D}_{x}^{J}\left(\,{\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>u^{\alpha}_{i}\overline{\xi}{}^{i}}\,\right)-\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>u^{\alpha}_{J,i}\overline{\xi}{}^{i}-\psi^{\alpha}_{J},

\displaystyle\alpha=1,\ldots,q,

\displaystyle J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}).

(7.21)

The proof of Theorem
7.6
appears after the following illustrative example.

Remark 7.7
.

The linearization of the normal form determining equations (
7.13
) in Theorem
7.6
occurs at the point
(x,\widehat{U}^{(n)})
. But since
u^{(n)}=\widehat{U}^{(n)}
at the identity, we may substitute
u^{(n)}
for
\widehat{U}^{(n)}
in the linearization, which is implicitly done in Theorem
7.6
.

Example 7.8
.

Returning to Example
7.3
, let us linearize the normal form determining equations (
7.16
) and (
7.17
) at the identity transformation. To do so, we apply the vector field

\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\>\bigg(\overline{\xi}{}_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{X}{}_{ij}}+\overline{\eta}{}_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{Y}{}_{ij}}+\psi_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{ij}}\bigg)

to the equations and then set
\displaystyle\overline{X}{}=x,

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}=y,

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{X^{i}Y^{j}}=u_{x^{i}y^{j}}
for all
i,j\geq 0
. At order
2
, this yields the linear system

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{\xi}{}_{y}\;

\displaystyle=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=-\psi,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\eta}{}_{y}=\overline{\xi}{}_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\xi}{}_{xx}=u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-\psi_{y},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\xi}{}_{xy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil\overline{\eta}{}_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\psi-\psi_{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\eta}{}_{xy}=u_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-\psi_{y},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\eta}{}_{yy}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\psi_{yy}=2\mskip 2.0muu_{yy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x},

(7.22)

while at order
3
we append the equations

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{xxx}

\displaystyle=(2\mskip 2.0muu_{xy}+u_{y}^{2})\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-\psi_{xy}-u_{y}\psi_{y}-u_{yy}\psi,

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{xxy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{xyy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{yyy}=0,

(7.23)

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{xxx}

\displaystyle=(2\mskip 2.0muu_{xy}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{x}u_{y})\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-\psi_{xx}-u_{y}\psi_{x}-u_{x}\psi_{y}-(2\mskip 2.0muu_{xy}+u_{y}^{2})\mskip 2.0mu\psi,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{xxy}

\displaystyle=(2\mskip 2.0muu_{xy}+u_{y}^{2})\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-\psi_{xy}-u_{y}\psi_{y}-u_{yy}\psi,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}{}_{xyy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{yyy}=0,

\displaystyle\psi_{xyy}

\displaystyle=(3\mskip 2.0muu_{xyy}+2\mskip 2.0muu_{y}u_{yy})\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-2\mskip 2.0muu_{yy}\psi_{y}-u_{yyy}\psi,

\displaystyle\psi_{yyy}=3\mskip 2.0muu_{yyy}\overline{\xi}{}_{x}.

Proof of Theorem
7.6

:
In view of (
5.10
), the linearized reduced determining equations have the form

\overline{L}{}_{\nu}=\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\>A_{\nu,\mathds{1}}^{i,J}\,\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{J}+\sum_{\alpha\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{q}\>\sum_{0\leq|K|\leq n}\>B_{\nu,\mathds{1}}^{\alpha,K}\,\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}_{K},

(7.24)

where the additional
\mathds{1}
subscript means that we evaluate the indicated coefficients at the identity. On the other hand, substituting (
7.18
) into (
5.10
), we deduce that the normal form determining equations take the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\nu}=\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\left[\,{\widetilde{A}_{\nu}^{i}(\overline{X}{}^{i}-x^{i})+\widetilde{A}_{\nu}^{i,i}(\overline{X}{}^{i}_{i}-1)+\sum_{\scriptstyle J\neq i\atop\scriptstyle 1\leq|J|\leq n}\>\widetilde{A}_{\nu}^{i,J}\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0pt{}+\sum_{\alpha\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{q}\>\sum_{0\leq|K|\leq n}\>\widetilde{B}_{\nu}^{\alpha,K}\bigl[\,{\overline{\mathfrak{U}}{}^{\alpha}_{K}(\overline{X}{}^{(k)},\widehat{U}^{(k)})-u^{\alpha}_{K}}\,\bigr],

whose coefficients are obtained from those of (
5.10
) by using the chain rule substitution (
7.11
).
Linearizing the latter expressions at the identity, using (
7.19
), and noting that at the identity (
7.11
) reduces to
\overline{U}{}^{(n)}=\widehat{U}^{(n)}=u^{(n)}
, produces

\widetilde{L}_{\nu}=\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\>A_{\nu,\mathds{1}}^{i,J}\,\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{J}+\sum_{\alpha\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{q}\>\sum_{0\leq|K|\leq n}\>B_{\nu,\mathds{1}}^{\alpha,K}\,\left[\,{\mathrm{D}_{x}^{K}\left(\,{\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>u^{\alpha}_{i}\overline{\xi}{}^{i}}\,\right)-\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>u^{\alpha}_{K,i}\overline{\xi}{}^{i}-\psi^{\alpha}_{K}}\,\right].

(7.25)

Comparing (
7.24
) and (
7.25
) completes the proof.
Q.E.D.

Remark 7.9
.

Inverting the substitutions (
7.21
) for
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
, we recover the usual formula for the prolongation of the vector field

-\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}=-\left(\,{\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\overline{\xi}{}^{i}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{\alpha\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{q}\>\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}}}\,\right)

(7.26)

to jet space. More explicitly, recall from
[
50
]
that the
n
-th order prolongation of
\overline{\mathbf{v}}
is the vector field

\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}=\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\overline{\xi}{}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\widehat{\phi}^{\alpha}_{J}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}},

(7.27)

where the prolonged vector field coefficients are given by the formula

\widehat{\phi}^{\alpha}_{J}=\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}_{J}-\mathrm{D}_{x}^{J}\left(\,{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\overline{\xi}{}^{i}u^{\alpha}_{i}}\,\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\overline{\xi}{}^{i}\mskip 2.0muu^{\alpha}_{J,i}.

(7.28)

Then, under the substitution (
7.21
), the prolonged vector field
-\,\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}
given in (
7.27
) is mapped to the vector field

\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}=-\,\sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>\overline{\xi}{}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\psi^{\alpha}_{J}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}.

(7.29)

As an immediate corollary, we are able to characterize the involutivity of the normal form determining equations.

Theorem 7.10
.

For any order
n
, if the reduced determining equations are involutive, then so are the normal form determining equations.

Proof
: Keeping only the highest order terms in the substitution (
7.21
) we have, at the level of the symbol, that

\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}_{J}\ \longmapsto\ \sum_{i\mskip 2.0mu=\mskip 2.0mu1}^{p}\>u_{i}\,\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{J}-\psi^{\alpha}_{J}.

(7.30)

Using the freedom that still remains within a given class, we order the columns of the symbol matrix
M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{n}
of the reduced determining equations so that the columns associated to
\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}_{J}
are to the left of the columns corresponding to
\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{K}
when
\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J=\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits K
. Now consider the row-echelon form
M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{},\text{REF}}^{n}
of the symbol matrix.
If
\overline{\phi}{}^{\alpha}_{J}
is a pivot of
M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{},\text{REF}}^{n}
, then, under the substitution (
7.30
),
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
is a pivot of the row-echelon symbol matrix
M_{\mathcal{N},\text{REF}}^{n}
of the normal form determining equations. If
\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{J}
is a pivot of
M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{},\text{REF}}^{n}
, then all the matrix components to the left of
\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{J}
in that row are zero. Based on our ordering of the columns of
M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{n}
, the substitution (
7.30
) does not alter the fact that
\overline{\xi}{}^{i}_{J}
is a pivot of
M_{\mathcal{N},\text{REF}}^{n}
. Therefore, the symbol matrices for the reduced determining equations and the normal form determining equations have the same indices and ranks and involutivity of
M_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{n}
implies involutivity of
M_{\mathcal{N}}^{n}
.

Next, the normal form determining equations are integrable as any integrability condition would map back to an integrability condition of the reduced determining equations. Therefore, the normal form determining equations are involutive.
Q.E.D.

8
Involutivity and Reduced Moving Frames.

We now have reached the heart of the paper where we complete the proof of our general convergence result for normal forms of submanifolds. The key remaining step is to establish compatibility of the cross-section normalizations producing the moving frame with the involutivity of the normal form determining system. The main complication is that they are not necessarily compatible at low order. However, as we will demonstrate, once we are beyond the order of freeness of the prolonged pseudo-group action and the order of involutivity of the normal form determining equations, this identification can be made. Indeed, this is to be suspected, since the order of freeness is also where the algebraic moving frame constructions used in
[
58
]
apply. As noted in
[
58
]
, the finite number of normalizations imposed at or below the order of freeness are not, in general, compatible with the algebraic framework used to establish generating sets of differential invariants and syzygies, and so must be appended to the former to obtain a complete system of differential invariants. Here we will see a similar behavior within the involutivity framework. Before establishing this connection, we illustrate the incompatibility at low order by revisiting our running example.

The involutivity of the normal form determining equations (
7.13
) relies on the class-based ordering of multi-indices, which imposes some restrictions on which jet coordinates are parametric and principal. For example, in the normal form determining equations (
7.16
), the equation

u_{yy}=\widehat{U}_{YY}\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}

is solved for
u_{yy}
since it is a principal derivative according to the involutivity framework. On the other hand, recalling the moving frame computations in Example
6.9
and the fact that we switch the source and target so that the source jet coordinates are in the cross-section, the normalization equation is written as

\widehat{U}_{YY}=\frac{u_{yy}}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}}=\frac{1}{\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}}\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm and is to be solved for}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}_{x}=\sqrt{\frac{u_{yy}}{\widehat{U}_{YY}}}.

Thus, the same equation may be solved for different jet coordinates depending on whether we implement the involutivity formalism or the moving frame construction.
At the level of the linearized equations, the equation in question is

2\mskip 2.0muu_{yy}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{\xi}{}_{x}-\psi_{yy}=0.

Since the symbol of the equation is
\psi_{yy}=0
, involutivity involves solving for
\psi_{yy}
, while the moving frame construction requires solving for
\overline{\xi}{}_{x}
.

The aim of this section is to show that, while they may differ at low order, if the normal form determining equations are prolonged beyond the order of freeness of the prolonged pseudo-group action, then the determination of the parametric derivatives via the symbol of the normal form determining equations is compatible with the moving frame construction provided we use an appropriately well-posed cross-section.

8.1
Beyond the Order of Freeness.

Let
n_{f}\geq 1
be the order of freeness of the prolonged Lie pseudo-group action. In this section we describe the structure of the order
n_{f}+1
normal form determining equations.

To simplify the exposition, we assume that the pseudo-group acts transitively on the space of independent variables
\mathcal{X}
. This implies that the order zero reduced pseudo-group jet coordinates
\overline{X}{}
are parametric parameters in the normal form determining equations. Freeness implies that at order
n_{f}
all the reduced horizontal pseudo-group jet coordinates
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
of orders
1\leq|J|\leq n_{f}
can be normalized by a suitable choice of cross-section. On the other hand, this implies that the same jet coordinates can be solved for in the normal form determining system
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f})}
:

\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}=\Xi^{i}_{J}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f})},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots),\hskip 20.00003pti=1,\ldots,p,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq|J|\leq n_{f},

(8.1)

where
u^{\beta}_{K}
are parametric normal form jet coordinates of order
|K|\leq n_{f}
. In particular, no derivatives
\overline{X}{}^{j}_{K}
of order
|K|\geq 1
appear on the right hand side of these equations. The remaining equations in
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f})}
will specify the principal normal form jet coordinates

u^{\alpha}_{J}=\Delta^{\alpha}_{J}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f})},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt1\leq\alpha\leq q,\hskip 10.00002pt|J|\leq n_{f},

(8.2)

and
u^{\beta}_{K}
are again parametric derivatives of order
|K|\leq n_{f}
. Since equations (
8.1
), (
8.2
) are obtained by implementing the reduced moving construction, these equations are not necessarily class-respecting at order
n_{f}
. This means that the class of the parametric normal form jet coordinates of order
n_{f}
on the right hand side of an equation may be greater than the class of the order
n_{f}
derivative occurring on the left hand side of the same equation.

To obtain class-respecting equations for the reduced horizontal pseudo-group jet coordinates, we differentiate the equations in (
8.1
) for the reduced pseudo-group parameters
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
of order
|J|=n_{f}
with respect to the multiplicative variables
j\leq\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(J)
, thereby obtaining the following subset of normal form determining equations:

\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J,j}=\Xi^{i}_{J,j}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f}+1)},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K,j},\ldots),\hskip 20.00003pti=1,\ldots,p,\hskip 10.00002pt|J|=n_{f},

(8.3)

of order
n_{f}+1
.
Note that when we differentiate, the resulting expressions include the first order derivatives
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{j}
, but these can be replaced by their expressions in (
8.1
) and hence the right hand sides of the resulting equations continue to be independent of the derivatives of the
\overline{X}{}^{i}
. We also note that all reduced horizontal pseudo-group jet coordinates of order
n_{f}+1
appear on the left hand sides of (
8.3
). In other words, all reduced horizontal pseudo-group parameters
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
of order
|J|=n_{f}+1
are principal. Also, the class of
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J,j}
in (
8.3
) is now

\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(J,j)=j
,
and the normal form jet coordinates
u^{\beta}_{K,j}
on the right hand side of (
8.3
) satisfy the class requirement

\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(K,j)=\min\{\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(K),j\}\leq j.

Therefore, the equations (
8.3
) are class-respecting. The remaining order
n_{f}+1
normal form determining equations are equations specifying the order
n_{f}+1
principal normal form jet coordinates

u^{\alpha}_{J}=\Delta^{\alpha}_{J}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f})},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots),\hskip 20.00003pt1\leq\alpha\leq q,\hskip 10.00002pt|J|=n_{f}+1,

(8.4)

where we used (
8.1
), (
8.3
) to remove the reduced horizontal pseudo-group parameters
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
of orders
1\leq|J|\leq n_{f}+1
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that the equations (
8.4
) are class-respecting. As we will see in the next section, this can be achieved by considering the vertical symbol of the normal form determining equations, which we now introduce.

8.2
Vertical and Prolonged Annihilator Symbols.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}
denote the linearization of the
n
-th order normal form determining equations (
7.13
) at the identity, and let

\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{n}=\textbf{H}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)})

be its symbol. Also, let
M_{\mathcal{N}}^{n}
be the corresponding symbol matrix. We first fix some of the freedom that exists when ordering the columns of
M_{\mathcal{N}}^{n}
within a fixed class. To be compatible with the moving frame construction, we require the columns associated to the reduced pseudo-group parameters
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
to appear to the left of the columns corresponding to the normal form jet coordinates
u^{\beta}_{K}
when
\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits K=\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J
. This ordering stems from the fact that, in the moving frame method, we prioritize solving for the reduced horizontal pseudo-group parameters
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
over the normal form jet coordinates
u^{\beta}_{K}
within a fixed class. We note that this convention is the opposite of that used in the proof of Theorem
7.10
. But as we show in Section
8.3
, this discrepancy becomes immaterial once we pass beyond the order of freeness of the prolonged pseudo-group action.

Since all the reduced horizontal pseudo-group parameters
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
of order
|J|=n_{f}+1
are principal variables in the order
n_{f}+1
normal form equations (
8.3
), (
8.4
), the involutivity of
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f}+1)}
is solely dependent on the equations (
8.4
), which relate the normal form jets. This observation leads us to introduce the
n
-th order vertical symbol

\Psi^{n}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{n}\,\cap\,{\rm span}\;\{\psi^{(n)}\},

(8.5)

consisting of all the equations in the
n
-th order symbol that only depend on the coefficients
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|=n
. Combining these spaces, we define the
vertical symbol

\Psi=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\>\Psi^{n}.

(8.6)

Remark 8.1
.

Coming back to the system (
8.4
), the principal normal form jet coordinates are indexed by the pivots of the row reduced order
n_{f}+1
vertical symbol
\Psi^{n_{f}+1}_{{}_{\text{REF}}}
.

Example 8.2
.

In our running example, keeping only the highest order terms in the linearized equations (
7.22
), we obtain the symbol equations

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{y}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\eta}{}_{y}=\overline{\xi}{}_{x},

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{xx}=\overline{\xi}{}_{xy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{yy}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\eta}{}_{xx}=\overline{\eta}{}_{xy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{yy}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\psi_{yy}=0.

Therefore, the vertical symbols of order
\leq 2
are

\Psi^{0}=\Psi^{1}=\mathchar 0\relax,\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm and}\hskip 20.00003pt\Psi^{2}=\{\psi_{yy}=0\}.

Similarly, from the order three linearized equations (
7.23
), we find that the order three vertical symbol is
\Psi^{3}=\{\psi_{xyy}=\psi_{yyy}=0\}
