, and more generally,

\Psi^{n}=\{\,\psi_{x^{j}y^{n-j}}=0\;|\;0\leq j\leq n-2\,\}\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm for}\hskip 20.00003ptn\geq 2.

Upon row reducing the vertical symbol
\Psi
, the pivots of
\Psi_{\text{REF}}
identify principal normal form jet coordinates in accordance with the theory of involutivity.
Now the question is whether this identification of principal normal form jet coordinates is compatible with the moving frame construction. To answer this question, we introduce the
n
-th order
prolonged annihilator subbundle

\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}\,\cap\,{\rm span}\;\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{},\psi^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}

(8.7)

containing the linearized normal form equations that only depend on
\overline{\xi}{}
and
\psi^{(n)}
. The name for (
8.7
) originates from the observation that
\{\overline{\xi}{},\psi^{(n)}\}
are the coordinates of the prolonged vector field (
7.29
), and that equations in
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
are linear combinations of
\{\overline{\xi}{},\psi^{(n)}\}
that vanish. To better understand the origin of (
8.7
), we recall that a function
I(x,u^{(n)})
is a differential invariant of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
if and only if it is annihilated by all prolonged infinitesimal generators (
7.27
) of the reduced pseudo-group action. In view of the alternative form (
7.29
), this is equivalent to the infinitesimal constraint

\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(I)=-\sum_{i=1}^{p}\>\overline{\xi}{}^{i}\frac{\partial I}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{q}\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq n}\psi^{\alpha}_{J}\frac{\partial I}{\partial u^{\alpha}_{J}}=0,

(8.8)

from which we deduce the following result.

Theorem 8.3
.

If
I(x,u^{(n)})
is a differential invariant, then the infinitesimal invariance equation
\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(I)=0
belongs to the
n
-th order prolonged annihilator subbundle
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
contains all the linear combinations of
\overline{\xi}{}
and
\psi^{(n)}
that vanish. Since the infinitesimal invariance criterion (
8.8
) is of this form, it must belong to
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
.

Q.E.D.

Applying Theorem
8.3
to the basic normalized differential invariants (
6.14
), evaluated at the source variables
(x,u^{(n)})
rather than the target variables
(X,\widehat{U}^{(n)})
, we conclude that the infinitesimal invariance conditions

\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(H^{j})=\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(I^{\beta}_{K})=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptj,\;(\beta;K)\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)},

(8.9)

are equations in
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
. Since the basic normalized differential invariants form a complete set of functionally independent differential invariants of order
\leq n
, it follows that, at each regular jet,

\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}|_{z^{(n)}}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(H^{j})|_{z^{(n)}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(I^{\beta}_{K})|_{z^{(n)}}=0\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;j,\;(\beta;K)\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(H^{j})|_{z^{(n)}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(I^{\beta}_{K})|_{z^{(n)}}=0\hbox{}\,|\,j,\;(\beta;K)\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(H^{j})|_{z^{(n)}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(I^{\beta}_{K})|_{z^{(n)}}=0\hbox{}\,|\,j,\;(\beta;K)\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\}}{\{\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(H^{j})|_{z^{(n)}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}(I^{\beta}_{K})|_{z^{(n)}}=0\hbox{}\,|\,j,\;(\beta;K)\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}\hbox{}\}}.

(8.10)

Remark 8.4
.

One needs to be a little careful here. Not every equation defining
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
is necessarily of the form (
8.9
) as its coefficients need not be partial derivatives of some function. On the other hand, (
8.10
) says that, at a fixed regular jet, the linear subvariety defined by the differential invariant conditions (
8.9
) coincides with the
n
-th order prolonged annihilator subbundle
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
.

Keeping only the highest order terms in (
8.7
), we introduce the
n
-th order prolonged annihilator symbol

\Upsilon^{n}=\textbf{H}(\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}).

Since
\overline{\xi}{}
has order zero, it follows that for
n\geq 1
, the
n
-th order prolonged annihilator symbol
\Upsilon^{n}
only involves linear equations in
\psi^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|=n
.

Example 8.5
.

Recalling the linearized normal form determining equations (
7.22
), (
7.23
), we conclude that when
u_{yy}\neq 0
,

\mathcal{Z}^{(3)}=\bigg\{\psi_{yyy}=\frac{3\mskip 2.0muu_{yyy}}{2\mskip 2.0muu_{yy}}\psi_{yy},\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{xyy}=\bigg(\frac{3}{2}\frac{u_{xyy}}{u_{yy}}+u_{y}\bigg)\psi_{yy}-2\mskip 2.0muu_{yy}\psi_{y}-u_{yyy}\psi\bigg\}.

(8.11)

We observe that, in accordance with the preceding remarks, the equations in (
8.11
) can also found by imposing the infinitesimal invariance conditions (
8.9
) for the normalized invariants (
6.19
), re-expressed in terms of the source variables
(x,u^{(n)})
.
Keeping only the highest order terms,

\Upsilon^{0}=\Upsilon^{1}=\Upsilon^{2}=\mathchar 0\relax,\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm while}\hskip 20.00003pt\Upsilon^{3}=\{\psi_{xyy}=\psi_{yyy}=0\}=\Psi^{3}.

(8.12)

More generally,
\Upsilon^{n}=\Psi^{n}
for all
n\geq 3
.

On the other hand, when
u_{yy}=0
, we have

\mathcal{Z}^{(2)}=\{\psi_{yy}=0\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{Z}^{(3)}=\{\psi_{yy}=\psi_{xyy}=\psi_{yyy}=0\},

so that

\Upsilon^{2}=\{\psi_{yy}=0\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\Upsilon^{3}=\{\psi_{xyy}=\psi_{yyy}=0\}.

In this case, the equality
\Upsilon^{n}=\Psi^{n}
holds for all
n\geq 2
.

Remark 8.6
.

It is worth reiterating that all the symbol computations are done at a fixed jet, whose dependence has been omitted throughout the paper. The last example reminds us that we need to pay attention to the base jet when performing computations, which can vary from point to point. This is important when, for example, analyzing singular normal forms,
[
60
]
.

Example 8.7
.

As a second example, consider the Lie pseudo-group

X=f(x),\hskip 20.00003ptY=\lambda\,y,\hskip 20.00003ptU=u+b,\hskip 20.00003ptV=v+c,

(8.13)

where
f\in\mathcal{D}({\mathbb{R}})
,
\lambda>0
, and
b,c\in\mathbb{R}
. Here we assume that
p=q=2
with
u=u(x,y)
,
v=v(x,y)
. Working under the assumption that
y\neq 0
, the normal form determining equations
\mathcal{N}^{(2)}
of order two are

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{x}=\frac{u_{x}}{\widehat{U}_{X}},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{X}{}_{y}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{Y}{}_{x}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{Y}{}_{y}=\frac{\overline{Y}{}}{y},\hskip 10.00002ptu_{y}=\frac{\overline{Y}{}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{Y}}{y},\hskip 10.00002ptv_{x}=\frac{u_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{V}_{X}}{\widehat{U}_{X}},\hskip 10.00002ptv_{y}=\frac{\overline{Y}{}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{V}_{Y}}{y},

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xx}=\frac{u_{xx}}{\widehat{U}_{X}}-\frac{u_{x}^{2}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{XX}}{\widehat{U}_{X}^{3}},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{X}{}_{xy}=\overline{X}{}_{yy}=\overline{Y}{}_{xx}=\overline{Y}{}_{xy}=\overline{Y}{}_{yy}=0,\hskip 10.00002ptu_{xy}=\frac{u_{x}\overline{Y}{}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{XY}}{y\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{X}},

\displaystyle u_{yy}=\frac{\overline{Y}{}^{2}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{YY}}{y^{2}},

\displaystyle v_{xx}=\frac{u_{xx}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{V}_{X}}{\widehat{U}_{X}}+u_{x}^{2}\bigg(\frac{\widehat{V}_{XX}\widehat{U}_{X}-\widehat{U}_{XX}\widehat{V}_{X}}{\widehat{U}_{X}^{3}}\bigg),

\displaystyle v_{xy}=\frac{u_{x}\overline{Y}{}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{V}_{XY}}{y\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{U}_{X}},

\displaystyle v_{yy}=\frac{\overline{Y}{}^{2}\mskip 2.0mu\widehat{V}_{YY}}{y^{2}}.

We remark that the equations for
u_{y}
,
v_{x}
,
v_{y}
,
\ldots
, can be obtained by successively applying the chain rule operators

\displaystyle\text{D}_{x}=\overline{X}{}_{x}\text{D}_{X}=\frac{u_{x}}{\widehat{U}_{X}}\,\text{D}_{X},

\displaystyle\text{D}_{y}=\overline{Y}{}_{y}\text{D}_{Y}=\frac{\overline{Y}{}}{y}\,\text{D}_{Y},

to the last two transformations in (
8.13
). Linearization at the identity yields the system of linear equations
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(2)}
given by

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{x}=\frac{\psi_{x}}{u_{x}},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\xi}{}_{y}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\eta}{}_{y}=\frac{\overline{\eta}{}}{y},\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{y}=\frac{u_{y}}{y},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{x}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{y}=\frac{v_{y}}{y}\,\overline{\eta}{},

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}{}_{xx}=\frac{\psi_{xx}}{u_{x}}-2\frac{u_{xx}\psi_{x}}{u_{x}^{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\xi}{}_{xy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{yy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{xx}=\overline{\eta}{}_{xy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{yy}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{xy}=u_{xy}\bigg(\frac{\psi_{x}}{u_{x}}+\frac{\overline{\eta}{}}{y}\bigg),

\displaystyle\psi_{yy}=2\frac{u_{yy}}{y}\,\overline{\eta}{},

\displaystyle\gamma_{xx}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{xx}+2\bigg(\frac{v_{xx}u_{x}-u_{xx}v_{x}}{u_{x}^{2}}\bigg)\psi_{x},

\displaystyle\gamma_{xy}=v_{xy}\bigg(\frac{\psi_{x}}{u_{x}}+\frac{\overline{\eta}{}}{y}\bigg),

\displaystyle\gamma_{yy}=2\frac{v_{yy}}{y}\,\overline{\eta}{},

where
\overline{\xi}{}
,
\overline{\eta}{}
,
\psi
,
\gamma
denote the linearizations of
\overline{X}{}
,
\overline{Y}{}
,
u
,
v
, respectively. Up to order two, the symbols are

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{0}

\displaystyle=\mathchar 0\relax,\hskip 20.00003pt\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{1}=\bigg\{\overline{\xi}{}_{x}=\frac{\psi_{x}}{u_{x}},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\xi}{}_{y}=\overline{\eta}{}_{x}=\overline{\eta}{}_{y}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{y}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{x}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{y}=0\bigg\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\overline{\xi}{}_{xx}=\frac{\psi_{xx}}{u_{x}},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\xi}{}_{xy}=\overline{\xi}{}_{yy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{xx}=\overline{\eta}{}_{xy}=\overline{\eta}{}_{yy}=\psi_{xy}=\psi_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle\hskip 263.18799pt\gamma_{xx}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{xx},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{xy}=\gamma_{yy}=0\bigg\}.

In this example,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{(2)}=\bigg\{\psi_{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{y}}{y}\,\overline{\eta}{},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{x}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{y}=\frac{v_{y}}{y}\,\overline{\eta}{},\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{xy}=u_{xy}\bigg(\frac{\psi_{x}}{u_{x}}+\frac{\overline{\eta}{}}{y}\bigg),\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{yy}=2\frac{u_{yy}}{y}\,\overline{\eta}{},

\displaystyle\gamma_{xx}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{xx}+2\bigg(\frac{v_{xx}u_{x}-u_{xx}v_{x}}{u_{x}^{2}}\bigg)\psi_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{xy}=v_{xy}\bigg(\frac{\psi_{x}}{u_{x}}+\frac{\overline{\eta}{}}{y}\bigg),\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{yy}=2\frac{v_{yy}}{y}\,\overline{\eta}{}\bigg\},

which can also be found by applying the vector field

\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(\infty)}=-\overline{\xi}{}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-\overline{\eta}{}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+\sum_{J}\bigg(\psi_{J}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{J}}+\gamma_{J}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{J}}\bigg)

to the differential invariants

\displaystyle I_{0,1}=y\mskip 2.0muu_{y},\hskip 20.00003ptI_{1,1}=\frac{y\mskip 2.0muu_{xy}}{u_{x}},\hskip 20.00003ptI_{0,2}=y^{2}u_{yy},

\displaystyle J_{1,0}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}},\hskip 20.00003ptJ_{0,1}=y\,v_{y},\hskip 20.00003ptJ_{2,0}=\frac{v_{xx}u_{x}-v_{x}u_{xx}}{u_{x}^{3}},\hskip 20.00003ptJ_{1,2}=\frac{y\mskip 2.0muv_{xy}}{u_{x}},\hskip 20.00003ptJ_{0,2}=y^{2}v_{yy},

and setting the result to zero. Finally, we note that

\displaystyle\hfil\Upsilon^{0}\;

\displaystyle=\Psi^{0}=\mathchar 0\relax,\hskip 20.00003pt\Upsilon^{1}=\Psi^{1}=\bigg\{\psi_{y}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{x}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{x},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{y}=0\bigg\},

\displaystyle\hfil\Upsilon^{2}\;

\displaystyle=\Psi^{2}=\bigg\{\psi_{xy}=\psi_{yy}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{xx}=\frac{v_{x}}{u_{x}}\,\psi_{xx},\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{xy}=\gamma_{yy}=0\bigg\}.

Remark 8.8
.

The linear spaces defined above are closely related to the algebraic constructions introduced in
[
58
]
. First, the vertical symbol (
8.6
) is related to the prolonged symbol submodule defined in
[
58
, Definition 4.2]
. On the other hand, the prolonged annihilator subbundle
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}
is equivalent to the bundle introduced in
[
58
, eq. (4.26)]
. As in
[
58
]
, we note that the vertical symbol
(
8.6
) forms a submodule, while, in general, this is not the case for the prolonged annihilator symbol
\Upsilon=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\Upsilon^{n}
. That said, as we will show in the next section, beyond the order of freeness
n_{f}
of the prolonged pseudo-group action, the equality
\Upsilon^{>n_{f}}=\Psi^{>n_{f}}
holds, which implies that the truncated prolonged annihilator symbol acquires the algebraic structure of the vertical symbol. Again, this is completely analogous to the constructions in
[
58
]
, where the algebraic structure of the differential invariants produced by the moving frame normalizations only appears after the order of freeness.

8.3
Compatibility.

As we observed in Section
8.1
, beyond the order of freeness
n_{f}
, the reduced horizontal pseudo-group jet coordinates
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
of order
|J|>n_{f}
can all be chosen to be principal derivatives. Therefore, the only parametric derivatives of order
>n_{f}
are found among the normal form jet coordinates
u^{\alpha}_{J}
, where the splitting of the normal form jets into principal and parametric variables is determined by the vertical symbol
\Psi^{>n_{f}}
. Since we wish to prioritize choosing the
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
as principal derivatives, in the following, we assign the index
(\alpha;J)
for
1\leq\alpha\leq q
to the normal form jet
u^{\alpha}_{J}
and
(q+i;J)
for
1\leq i\leq p
to the reduced horizontal pseudo-group jet
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
. Then, beyond the order of freeness, the splitting of the multi-indices

\mathcal{I}^{>n_{f}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N},\text{prin}}^{>n_{f}}\;\biguplus\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N},\text{par}}^{>n_{f}}

of order
>n_{f}
induced by the normal form determining equations has
(q+i;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N},\text{prin}}^{>n_{f}}
for all
i=1,\ldots,p
and all
|J|>n_{f}
, while
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N},\text{par}}^{>n_{f}}
only contains tuples of the form
(\alpha;J)
with
1\leq\alpha\leq q
and
|J|>n_{f}
.

As outlined in Section
6.2
, the construction of a moving frame is based on the introduction of a (coordinate) cross-section (
6.11
), where the phantom invariants
u^{\alpha}_{J}
with
(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
determine the cross-section, and hence prescribe the constant Taylor coefficients in the normal form series (
7.6
), and thus play the role of “parametric” jet coordinates in the moving frame framework. For the moving frame construction to be compatible with the involutivity of the normal form determining equations we need, starting at a certain order
n
, for the parametric derivatives in the normal form determining equations to coincide with the phantom derivatives. In other words, at a certain order
n
, the equality

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N},\text{par}}^{>n}=\mathcal{I}^{>n}_{\mathcal{K}}

(8.14)

should hold. To show that (
8.14
) can be achieved, we show below that the equality

\Psi^{>n_{f}}=\Upsilon^{>n_{f}}

(8.15)

between the truncated vertical and prolonged annihilator symbols holds. Equality (
8.15
) will then allow us to define in Section
8.4
the notion of a
well-posed cross-section
for which (
8.14
) holds at order
n=n_{f}
.

According to Definition
6.4
, the reduced Lie pseudo-group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
acts freely at
z^{(n)}\in{\rm J}{}^{n}
if and only if the isotropy group is trivial, meaning

\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}=\bigl\{\,{(\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{U}{}^{(n)})\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}\;|\;P^{(n)}(z^{(n)},\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{U}{}^{(n)})=z^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}=\bigl\{\,{\overline{\mathds{1}}^{(n)}_{z}}\,\bigr\},

(8.16)

where
P^{(n)}(z^{(n)},\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{U}{}^{(n)})
is the function that prescribes the prolonged action (
6.2
) at order
n
. At the infinitesimal level, the Lie pseudo-group acts locally freely if and only if

\overline{\mathfrak{g}}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}=\bigl\{\,{(\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)},\overline{\phi}{}^{(n)})\in\mathcal{L}_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)},\overline{\phi}{}^{(n)})\;|\;\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}=0}\,\bigr\}=\{0\},

(8.17)

where the prolonged vector field
\overline{\mathbf{v}}{}^{(n)}
is defined in (
7.27
).

The next result shows that
persistence of freeness
, cf.
[
57
,
59
]
, also holds for reduced Lie pseudo-group actions.

Theorem 8.9
.

If the reduced pseudo-group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
acts
(
locally
)
freely at
z^{(n)}\in{\rm J}{}^{n}
, then for all
k>0
it acts
(
locally
)
freely at
z^{(n+k)}\in{\rm J}{}^{n+k}
where
\pi^{n+k}_{n}(z^{(n+k)})=z^{(n)}
.

Proof.

The linearized equations (
8.17
) imply that the symbol of the system of equations (
8.16
) is trivial. Therefore the system (
8.16
) is involutive with vanishing Cartan characters
{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(i)}}=0
for
i=1,\ldots,p
.
Since
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n+k)}_{z^{(n+k)}}
can be obtained by prolonging
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}_{z^{(n)}}
, and involutivity is preserved under prolongation, we conclude, recalling (
5.20
), that the Cartan characters of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n+k)}_{z^{(n+k)}}
also vanish,
which means that all jets of order
n+k
are uniquely determined. Since
\overline{\mathds{1}}{}^{(n+k)}_{z^{(n+k)}}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n+k)}_{z^{(n+k)}}
, this is the only solution and the reduced pseudo-group remains free at order
n+k
.

Q.E.D.

We now make the substitutions (
7.11
) in (
8.16
) to obtain
7
7

7

Keep in mind, that, in accordance with Remark
7.7
,
u^{(n)}=\widehat{U}^{(n)}
at the identity transformation.

\bigl\{\,{(\overline{X}{}^{(n)},u^{(n)})\in\mathcal{N}^{(n)}\;|\;P^{(n)}(z^{(n)},\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\overline{\mathfrak{U}}{}^{(n)}(\overline{X}{}^{(n)},\widehat{U}^{(n)}))=z^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}=\bigl\{\,{(\mathds{1}^{(n)}_{x},\widehat{U}^{(n)})}\,\bigr\},

(8.18)

which holds whenever the reduced pseudo-group acts freely. At the infinitesimal level, we use equality (
7.29
) to conclude that
0=\overline{\mathbf{v}}{}^{(n)}=-\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{(n)}
, the latter being equivalent to

\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{}=0,\ \ \psi^{(n)}=0}\,\bigr\}.

Thus, the linearization of (
8.18
), at the identity transformation, yields

\bigl\{\,{(\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)},\psi^{(n)})\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)},\psi^{(n)})\;|\;\overline{\xi}{}=0,\ \psi^{(n)}=0}\,\bigr\}=\{0\}.

(8.19)

Remark 8.10
.

The local freeness condition (
8.19
) implies that the system of equations
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}(z^{(n)},\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)},\psi^{(n)})\,\cap\,\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{}=0,\ \psi^{(n)}=0}\,\bigr\}
is equivalent to
\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)}=0,\ \psi^{(n)}=0}\,\bigr\}
. Therefore any linear combination
Y\in\text{span}\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)},\;\psi^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}
can be written in the form
Y=U+V
, with
U\in\text{span}\{\overline{\xi}{},\psi^{(n)}\}
and the equation
V=0
belonging to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}
.

We now establish the key moving frame/involutivity compatibility result.

Theorem 8.11
.

If
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
acts
(
locally
)
freely at
z^{(n)}\in{\rm J}{}^{n}
, then the equality
\Psi^{k}|_{z^{(k)}}=\Upsilon^{k}|_{z^{(k)}}
holds for all
k>n
and all
z^{(k)}\in(\pi^{k}_{n})^{-1}\{z^{(n)}\}
.

Proof.

By an inductive argument that relies on the persistence of freeness, it suffices to prove the equality for
k=n+1
. Since
\Psi^{n}\subseteq\Upsilon^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it suffices to show the reverse inclusion. In other words, if
Q=0
is in
\Psi^{n+1}
, by which we mean that
Q=0
is one of the defining equations of
\Psi^{n+1}
, we must show that there exists
U\in\text{span}\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{},\;\psi^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}
such that

Q+U=0\ \in\ \mathcal{Z}^{(n+1)}.

If this is the case, then
Q=\textbf{H}(Q+U)=0
is in
\Upsilon^{n+1}
.

Now, since
Q=0
is an equation in the symbol
\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{n+1}
, there exists
Y\in\text{span}\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{}^{(n)},\;\psi^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}
such that

Q+Y=0\ \in\ \mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n+1)}.

Using Remark
8.10
, we have

Y=U+V,

with
U\in\text{span}\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{},\;\psi^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}
and
V=0
in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}
. Thus, the equation

Q+U=(Q+Y)-V=0\ \in\ \mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(n+1)}.

Since
Q=0
is in
\Psi^{n+1}
and
U\in\text{span}\bigl\{\,{\overline{\xi}{},\;\psi^{(n)}}\,\bigr\}
, we conclude that
Q+U=0\in\mathcal{Z}^{(n+1)}
.

Q.E.D.

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
n_{f}
be the order at which the prolonged action of the Lie group
\mathcal{G}
becomes free. Then

\Psi^{>n_{f}}=\Upsilon^{>n_{f}}.

(8.20)

Proof.

By Theorem
6.5
, the pseudo-group is reducible and the prolonged action of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
also becomes free at order
n_{f}
. By Theorem
8.11
,
\Psi^{n}=\Upsilon^{n}
for all
n>n_{f}
, which yields (
8.20
).

Q.E.D.

Assuming the normal form determining equations become involutive at order
\overline{n}{}_{\star}
and that the prolonged pseudo-group action becomes free at order
n_{f}\geq\overline{n}{}_{\star}
, we say, in light of the equality (
8.20
) between the vertical and prolonged annihilator symbols of order
\geq n_{f}+1
, that the involutivity of the normal form determining equations becomes
compatible
with the moving frame construction at order
n_{f}+1
.

Remark 8.13
.

Example
8.5
shows that freeness is not necessary to obtain the equality
\Psi^{>n}=\Upsilon^{>n}
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Non-free actions will arise, in particular, in equivalence problems where there are non-trivial isotropy groups. By appropriately dealing with the isotropy group, a modified version of Corollary
8.12
should still hold. The details are, however, deferred to a future study.

Example 8.14
.

Continuing Example
7.3
, we saw that the order two normal form determining equations are not compatible with the moving frame construction. But since the prolonged action becomes free at order two, in view of Theorem
8.12
, those of order three, as given in (
7.17
), will be compatible. The normal form determining equations of order three remain involutive with Cartan characters

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{3}^{(1)}}=2,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{3}^{(2)}}=0
.

8.4
Well-Posed Cross-Sections.

We now explain how to define a cross-section to be used in the construction of a moving frame that is compatible with the algebraic constructions and results of the previous sections. As seen in Section
6.2
, a moving frame is obtained by selecting a coordinate cross-section (
6.11
) transversal to the prolonged pseudo-group orbits. To construct such a cross-section, at any order
n\geq 0
it suffices to consider the prolonged annihilator symbol
\Upsilon^{n}
with coefficient matrix
M^{n}_{\Upsilon}
. The jet coordinates
u^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
|J|=n
specifying the cross-section equations
u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}
in (
6.11
) are then given by the non-pivot columns of the reduced coefficient matrix
M^{n}_{\Upsilon,_{\text{REF}}}
.

In light of Corollary
8.12
, for all
n>n_{f}
, the jet coordinates that specify the coordinate cross-section can be chosen so as to coincide with the parametric derivatives in the normal form determining equations, provided the columns of
M^{n}_{\Upsilon}
are sorted using the same class-respecting ordering imposed on the
n
-th order vertical symbol
\Psi^{n}
. Therefore, the cross-section equations

\mathcal{K}^{>n_{f}}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>n_{f}}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>n_{f}}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>n_{f}}\hbox{}\}}{\{u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>n_{f}}\hbox{}\}}

can be chosen so as to specify the parametric derivatives
u^{\alpha}_{J}
of order
>n_{f}
in the normal form determining equations, or, equivalently, the constant Taylor coefficients in the normal form power series (
7.6
). On the other hand, the system of equations obtained by combining the cross-section equations

\mathcal{K}^{(n_{f})}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,x^{i}=c^{i},\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n_{f})}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,x^{i}=c^{i},\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n_{f})}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{x^{i}=c^{i},\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n_{f})}\hbox{}\}}{\{x^{i}=c^{i},\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n_{f})}\hbox{}\}}

with the normal form equations (
8.1
), (
8.2
) of order
\leq n_{f}
determines all the parametric normal form derivatives therein. The equations
x^{i}=c^{i}
in

\mathcal{K}^{(n_{f})}
are used to define the order zero reduced pseudo-group parameters

\overline{X}{}^{i}(c^{1},\ldots,c^{p})=X^{i}_{0},\hskip 20.00003pti=1,\ldots,p,

(8.21)

where
X^{i}_{0}
are arbitrary constants. When combined, the entire cross-section
\mathcal{K}
, together with (
8.21
), determines all the parametric derivatives in the normal form determining equations. We call such a cross-section a
well-posed cross-section
.

This terminology stems from the fact that, as we explain in more detail in Section
8.5
, it specifies formally well-posed initial conditions for the normal form determining equations. A well-posed cross-section is a refinement of the notion of algebraic cross-section introduced in
[
58
]
, which is prescribed by a Gröbner basis of the submodule
\Psi
. On the other hand, implicit in our implementation of the theory of involutivity is the fact that the determination of a well-posed cross-section is prescribed by a Pommaret basis,
[
66
]
. The main difference between Gröbner and Pommaret bases occurs in the definition of the multiplicative variables of a multi-index
J
,
[
66
]
. As seen in Section
3
, for the Pommaret division the assignment of multiplicative variables depends on the class of
J
. On the other hand, there is no constraint on the multiplicative variables for a Gröbner basis. Thus, in general, Pommaret and Gröbner bases are not necessarily the same
8
8

8

Only when the ideal is stable, which we do not require, can one guarantee that its reduced Pommaret basis equals its reduced Gröbner basis,
[
43
]
.
. Finally, with a Pommaret basis, an ideal can be decomposed into a finite union of disjoint involutive Pommaret cones, as in (
3.18
), while the cones associated with a Gröbner basis may have non-trival intersections, and hence the connection with involutivity is not evident.

Remark 8.15
.

A well-posed cross-section is the Lie pseudo-group analogue of a minimal order cross-section introduced in
[
53
]
for finite-dimensional Lie group actions. In both cases, the cross-section has the property that pseudo-group jet coordinates are normalized as soon as possible. More precisely, a cross-section
\mathcal{K}\subset{\rm J}{}^{\infty}
is of
minimal order
if for all
n\geq 0
its projection
\mathcal{K}^{(n)}=\pi^{\infty}_{n}(\mathcal{K})\subset{\rm J}{}^{n}
forms a cross-section to the orbits of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(n)}
on
{\rm J}{}^{n}
.

Remark 8.16
.

In
[
39
]
, Kossovskiy and Zaitsev realized the importance of working with well-posed cross-sections in order to construct convergent normal forms. As mentioned in the first paragraph of section two of their work, they resolve the problem of divergence of Kolář’s normal form for degenerate hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
[
37
]
, by selecting a well-posed/minimal order cross-section.

We now introduce a simple criterion that guarantees that a prescribed cross-section is well-posed without having to compute the normal form determining equations. Assume that the determining equations of the reduced pseudo-group
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}
become involutive at order
\overline{n}{}_{\star}
and that the prolonged pseudo-group action becomes free at order
n_{f}\geq\overline{n}{}_{\star}
. By Theorem
7.10
, the normal form determining equations
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f}+1)}
are involutive. Proposition
3.11
implies that the set of parametric indices
\mathcal{I}^{>n_{f}}_{\mathcal{N},\text{par}}
admits a Rees decomposition. Since a well-posed cross-section is constructed such that (
8.14
) holds with
n=n_{f}
, it follows that its set of defining indices admits the same Rees decomposition, so

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>n_{f}}=\biguplus_{(\alpha;J)\mskip 2.0mu\in\mskip 2.0mu\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{n_{f}+1}}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(J).

(8.22)

Conversely, Proposition
3.11
states that the Rees decomposition (
8.22
) is sufficient to guarantee the existence of a Pommaret basis for the ideal
\Upsilon^{>n_{f}}=\Psi^{>n_{f}}
. This implies the following well-posedness criterion.

Theorem 8.17
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie pseudo-group whose prolonged action becomes free at order
n_{f}
. A cross-section
\mathcal{K}
is well-posed if it is of minimal order and its set of defining indices
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>n_{f}}
admits a Rees decomposition (
8.22
).

Proof.

We need to show that there exists a system of normal form determining equations that is involutive at order
n_{f}+1
with
\mathcal{K}
providing well-posed initial conditions.

Since the prolonged action becomes free, by Theorem
6.5
the pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
is reducible with reduced determining equations (
7.10
). The normal form determining equations are then obtained by substituting the chain rule formulas (
7.11
) into (
7.10
) to obtain (
7.13
). These equations are subsequently solved for the principal pseudo-group jets
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
, and the principal normal form jets
u^{\alpha}_{J}
with
(\alpha;J)\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}
. In order for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{(n)}
to be as large as possible, we must require that as many reduced pseudo-group parameters
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
be principal as possible. This, in other words, is equivalent to requiring that the cross-section
\mathcal{K}
be of minimal order. Recall from Section
8.1
that once
n=n_{f}
all reduced pseudo-group derivatives
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
are principal.

The order
n_{f}+1
normal form determining equations are given by equations of the form (
8.3
), (
8.4
). The equations (
8.3
) for the reduced pseudo-group jets do not provide any obstruction to involutivity, and therefore it suffices to consider the equations for the normal form jets (
8.4
). By assumption, since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>n_{f}}
admits a Rees decomposition (
8.22
), Proposition
3.11
guarantees that the symbols
\Upsilon^{>n_{f}}=\Psi^{>n_{f}}
admit a Pommaret basis. The existence of the Pommaret basis implies that it is possible to express the differential equations for the normal form jets of order
n_{f}+1
in such a way that (
8.3
), (
8.4
) is involutive with the parametric normal form jets
u^{\beta}_{K}
of order
|K|=n_{f}+1
specified by the cross-section.

Q.E.D.

Example 8.18
.

For our running example — the Lie pseudo-group (
4.8
) — a well-posed cross-section is given by (
6.16
), which we can verify satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
8.17
. First, for all
n\geq 0
,
\mathcal{K}^{(n)}
is transversal to the prolonged pseudo-group action and thus is of minimal order.
Next, since the prolonged action becomes free at order
n_{f}=2
, we must consider the cross-section determining equations of order
>2
, namely

\mathcal{K}^{>2}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k+1},\,u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k+1},\,u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\}}{\{u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k+1},\,u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}{\{u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k+1},\,u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}.

The corresponding set of determining indices has the Rees decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>2}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(k+1,0),\,(k,1)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;n\geq 2\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(k+1,0),\,(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,n\geq 2\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(k+1,0),\,(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,n\geq 2\hbox{}\}}{\{(k+1,0),\,(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,n\geq 2\hbox{}\}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(k+1,0)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(k+1,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(k+1,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}{\{(k+1,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}\;\biguplus\;\mathchoice{\left\{\,(k,1)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}{\{(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}=\mathcal{C}(3,0)\;\biguplus\;\mathcal{C}(2,1).

8.5
Convergence of the Normal Form Power Series.

We are now ready to state and prove the main result of the paper. As in the previous section, let
n_{f}\geq\overline{n}{}_{\star}
denote the order of freeness, taken to be at least as large as the order of involutivity. We follow the discussion on page
3.12
to rewrite the order
n_{f}+1
involutive normal form determining equations
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f}+1)}
as an equivalent system of first order differential equations

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{(1)}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \ &\widetilde{\Delta}^{(1)}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f}+1)},(\overline{X}{}^{(n_{f})})^{(1)},(u^{(n_{f})})^{(1)})=0,\\
&\begin{aligned} &\partial_{i}\overline{X}{}^{j}_{J}=X^{j}_{J,i},&&\partial_{i}u^{\alpha}_{J}=u^{\alpha}_{J,i},&\hskip 28.45274pt&|J|\leq n_{f},\;1\leq i\leq p,\\
&\partial_{i}\overline{X}{}^{j}_{J}=\partial_{k}\overline{X}{}^{j}_{J,i\setminus k},&&\partial_{i}u^{\alpha}_{J}=\partial_{k}u^{\alpha}_{J,i\setminus k},&&|J|=n_{f},\;k=\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits J<i\leq p.\ \end{aligned}\end{aligned}\right\}.

(8.23)

According to Proposition
3.9
, this first order system remains involutive with the same Cartan characters as the original normal form determining system
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f}+1)}
. Furthermore, we write (
8.23
) in reduced Cartan normal form. Since the second and third lines of (
8.23
) are already in Cartan normal form, we focus on the equations
\widetilde{\Delta}^{(1)}=0
. When expressing the order
n_{f}+1
jets as first order derivatives, we use
the substitutions (
3.13
) and make the blanket assumption that when writing
\partial_{k}u^{\gamma}_{K\setminus k}
, the multi-index
K
is of order
n_{f}+1
and class
k
. Doing so, we obtain the first order system of differential equations

\displaystyle\partial_{j}u^{\alpha}_{J\setminus j}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\alpha}_{J}\big(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f}+1)},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots,\partial_{i}u^{\gamma}_{I\setminus i},\ldots\big),

(8.24)

\displaystyle\partial_{\ell}\overline{X}{}^{i}_{L}

\displaystyle=\Xi^{i}_{L,\ell}\big(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f}+1)},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots,\partial_{n}u^{\kappa}_{N\setminus n},\ldots\big),

where all the normal form jets
u^{\beta}_{K}
,
\partial_{i}u^{\gamma}_{I\setminus i}
,
\partial_{n}u^{\kappa}_{N\setminus n}
appearing on the right hand side of the equations are parametric with
|K|\leq n_{f}
,
|L|=n_{f}
,
|I|=|J|=|N|=n_{f}+1
, while
i\leq j
and
n\leq\ell\leq\mathop{\rm cls}\nolimits(L)
. We note that the equations (
8.24
) are just the equations (
8.3
), (
8.4
) written as first order differential equations. The equations (
8.24
) are supplemented with the algebraic equations

u^{\alpha}_{J}=\Delta^{\alpha}_{J}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f})},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots),\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}=\Xi^{i}_{J}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f})},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots),

(8.25)

given by (
8.1
), (
8.2
), where
u^{\alpha}_{J}
and
\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}
are principal derivatives and
u^{\beta}_{K}
are parametric derivatives of order
\leq n_{f}
. According to Theorem
3.12
, provided all the functions
\Delta^{\alpha}_{J}
,
\Xi^{i}_{J}
, and
\Xi^{i}_{L,\ell}
in (
8.24
), (
8.25
) are real-analytic at the origin, the formally well-posed initial conditions

\displaystyle u^{\beta}_{K}(0,\ldots,0)

\displaystyle=f^{\beta}_{K},

(8.26)

\displaystyle u^{\gamma}_{I\setminus 1}(x^{1},0,\ldots,0)

\displaystyle=f^{\gamma}_{I\setminus 1}(x^{1}),

\displaystyle\vdots

\displaystyle u^{\gamma}_{I\setminus p-1}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p-1},0)

\displaystyle=f^{\gamma}_{I\setminus p-1}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p-1}),

\displaystyle u^{\gamma}_{I\setminus p}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p})

\displaystyle=f^{\gamma}_{I\setminus p}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{p}),

specifying the parametric derivatives occurring on the right hand side of the equations (
8.24
), (
8.25
) are analytic at the origin, and the algebraic equations

\displaystyle u^{\alpha}_{J}(0,\ldots,0)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\alpha}_{J}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f})},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots)|_{(0,\ldots,0)},

(8.27)

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}^{i}_{J}(0,\ldots,0)

\displaystyle=\Xi^{i}_{J}(x,\widehat{U}^{(n_{f})},\overline{X}{},\ldots,u^{\beta}_{K},\ldots)|_{(0,\ldots,0)},

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}^{i}(0,\ldots,0)

\displaystyle=X^{i}_{0},

are satisfied, then the normal form determining system admits one and only one solution that is analytic at the origin. In particular, the normal form
u(x)
, which forms part of the solution is analytic. In (
8.27
), the right hand side of the third equation are the components of the point
X_{0}=(X_{0}^{1},\ldots,X_{0}^{p})\in\mathcal{X}
at which the submanifold is being considered.

Remark 8.19
.

The initial conditions (
8.26
), (
8.27
) are stated under the assumption that the pseudo-group
\mathcal{G}
can map the
origin
0\in\mathcal{X}
to the point
X_{0}
. In applications, the origin can be replaced by any convenient point
\bm{p}\in\mathcal{X}
. For example, the points where
y=0
are singular for the pseudo-group (
8.13
), and here the origin can be replaced by the point
\bm{p}=(0,1)
, so that any point
(X_{0},Y_{0})
with
Y_{0}>0
lies on its group orbit. In general, given
\bm{p}\in\mathcal{X}
, the initial conditions (
8.26
) can be modified by considering hyperplanes passing through
\bm{p}
. Of course, it is also possible to make a local change of coordinates preserving
\delta
-regularity so that
\bm{p}
is mapped to
0
and the initial conditions are given by (
8.26
), (
8.27
).

Example 8.20
.

For our running example, based on the cross-section (
6.16
), the standard moving frame implementation yields the general normal form (
7.7
).
Since the prolonged action becomes free at order
n_{f}=2
, we must consider the order three normal form determining equations given in (
7.17
) to show that
formal power series (
7.7
) converges. We note that the last two equations of (
7.17
) are solved for the principal normal form jets
u_{xyy}
and
u_{yyy}
, in accordance with the order three vertical symbol (
8.12
). As first order partial differential equations, these determine
\partial_{x}(u_{yy})
and
\partial_{y}(u_{yy})
. On the other hand, the order three normal form jets
u_{xxy}=\partial_{x}(u_{xy})
and
u_{xxx}=\partial_{x}(u_{xx})
are parametric of class one.

In accordance with (
8.26
),
u_{xx}
and
u_{xy}
are fixed by imposing initial conditions along the
x
-axis. Differentiating (
7.7
), those are given by

u_{xx}(x,0)=c_{xx}(x),\hskip 20.00003ptu_{xy}(x,0)=d_{x}(x),

(8.28)

These are supplemented with the algebraic initial conditions

\displaystyle X(0,0)=X_{0},

\displaystyle Y(0,0)=Y_{0},

(8.29)

\displaystyle u(0,0)=c_{0},

\displaystyle u_{x}(0,0)=c_{1},

\displaystyle u_{y}(0,0)=d_{0},

\displaystyle u_{yy}(0,0)=1,

that come from the low order normalizations. We note that the initial conditions (
8.28
), (
8.29
) can be simplified to

\displaystyle X(0,0)=X_{0},

\displaystyle Y(0,0)=Y_{0},

\displaystyle u(x,0)=c(x),

\displaystyle u_{y}(x,0)=d(x),

\displaystyle u_{yy}(0,0)=1.

Assuming the functions
c(x)
and
d(x)
are both analytic, the solution to the involutive normal form determining equations (
7.17
)
includes the normal form function (
7.7
), thereby establishing its analyticity and hence convergence of the corresponding power series.

Keeping this example in mind, we are now able to state our general convergence result. Further illustrative examples will appear in Section
10
.

Theorem 8.21
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an analytic Lie pseudo-group acting transitively on
\mathcal{X}
with its prolonged action acting eventually freely on an analytic submanifold
\widehat{U}(X)
.
If the cross-section

\mathcal{K}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,x^{i}=0,\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,x^{i}=0,\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\,\}}{\{x^{i}=0,\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\}}{\{x^{i}=0,\ u^{\alpha}_{J}=c^{\alpha}_{J}\hbox{}\,|\,i,\;(\alpha;J)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}\hbox{}\}}

(8.30)

is well-posed, and the cross-section power series (
7.3
) determined by the normalization constants
c^{\alpha}_{J}
are convergent and so define analytic functions, then the corresponding normal form power series
(
7.6
)
converges and defines an analytic function in the neighborhood of the origin.

Proof.

Since the prolonged action of
\mathcal{G}
becomes eventually free, the pseudo-group is reducible by Theorem
6.5
, and by Theorem
5.12
there exists
\overline{n}{}_{\star}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that reduced determining equations
\overline{\mathcal{G}}{}^{(\overline{n}{}_{\star})}
are involutive. By Theorem
7.10
the normal form determining equations
\mathcal{N}^{(\overline{n}{}_{\star})}
are involutive. Let
n_{f}\geq\overline{n}{}_{\star}
be an order at which the prolonged action is free. Since involutivity is preserved under prolongation, the normal form determining equations
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f}+1)}
are involutive. Since
\mathcal{G}
and
\widehat{U}(X)
are analytic, the normal form determining equations
\mathcal{N}^{(n_{f}+1)}
are also analytic, and, when written as a system of first order differential equations, are given by (
8.24
), (
8.25
). With
\mathcal{K}
being a well-posed cross-section, the analytic cross-section power series (
7.3
) provides the analytic well-posed initial conditions (
8.26
), (
8.27
). That is,

\displaystyle C^{\gamma}_{I\setminus i}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{i},0,\ldots,0)=f^{\gamma}_{I\setminus i}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{i}),

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,p,

\displaystyle C^{\beta}_{K}(0,\ldots,0)=f^{\beta}_{K}.

Theorem
3.12
implies that the solution to the initial value problem is unique and analytic including the part of the solution corresponding to the normal form power series (
7.6
).

Q.E.D.

Remark 8.22
.

Implicit in the statement of Theorem
8.21
is the fact that the coordinates used to express the well-posed cross-section (
8.30
) are
\delta
-regular. Indeed, by definition
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie pseudo-group if its elements are the solutions to an involutive system of differential equations, and involutivity, within our framework, requires
\delta
-regularity of the underlying coordinate system.

9
Chains.

In their paper
[
13
]
, Chern and Moser introduced the concept of a chain as a tool for proving the convergence of their normal form power series for CR hypersurfaces
S\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
. A regular curve
\bm{\mathcal{C}}\subset S
in the hypersurface
S
is said to be a
chain
if its projection
\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}})\subset\mathcal{X}
onto the space of independent variables can be rectified by a biholomorphic transformation that also normalizes the Taylor coefficients of the hypersurface
S
appearing in the Chern–Moser normal form. They employ a finite sequence of transformations that successively rectify the chains and thereby place the Taylor expansion of the transformed surface in normal form. Each transformation is analytic since it either satisfies an algebraic constraint or is the solution to an analytic system of ordinary differential equations. Therefore, the final transformed hypersurface is analytic, and its Taylor series, which is in normal form, converges.

To make the discussion more precise, let us first review the convergence argument in
[
13
]
when
m=2
so that
S\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
is a three-dimensional hypersurface. We introduce complex (source) coordinates
\displaystyle z=x+\,{\rm i}\,y,

\displaystyle w=u+\,{\rm i}\,v
, so that
\displaystyle Z=X+\,{\rm i}\,y,

\displaystyle W=U+\,{\rm i}\,V
are the corresponding target coordinates. As in
[
13
]
, we assume that the prescribed hypersurface is locally parametrized by

S=\{(Z,\overline{Z},U,\widehat{V}(Z,\overline{Z},U))\},

so that
(Z,\overline{Z},U)\in\mathcal{X}
are viewed as independent variables.
After translation, we can work at the origin and consider the Taylor expansion

\widehat{V}(Z,\overline{Z},U)=\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}Z^{j}\overline{Z}{}^{k}F_{j,k}(U),

(9.1)

where the Taylor coefficients and powers of
U
are contained in the functions
F_{j,k}
. By assumption
F_{0,0}(0)=0
, since the hypersurface has been translated to the origin. One then seeks a chain, meaning a curve

\bm{\mathcal{C}}=\{(\psi(u),\varphi(u))\}\subset S\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003pt\varphi_{u}(0)\neq 0,

(9.2)

whose projection
\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}})
is holomorphically rectified onto the line
\ell=\{(0,0,u)\}
and sends the hypersurface Taylor series (
9.1
) to the Chern–Moser normal form, which is given in (
9.6
) below. This is accomplished by the following sequence of analytic transformations, each of which serves to normalize some of the Taylor coefficients in the expansion (
9.1
).

Step 1:

The holomorphic transformation

Z=z+\psi(w),\hskip 20.00003ptW=\varphi(w)

takes
\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}})
into
\ell
and sends (
9.1
) to
9
9

9

During the course of the procedure, the expressions for the Taylor coefficient functions
F_{jk}
will change. We avoid introducing new notation for each version.

v=\sum_{j+k\geq 1}z^{j}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}{}^{k}F_{j,k}(u).

We observe that such a transformation does not impose any constraint on the chain.

Step 2:

Cancel the harmonic terms
z^{j}F_{j,0}(u)
and
\overline{z}{}^{k}F_{0,k}(u)
using a transformation of the form

z^{*}=z,\hskip 20.00003ptw^{*}=w+g(z,w)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptg(0,w)=0,

(9.3)

so that the new power series is

v=\sum_{j\geq 1\text{ or }k\geq 1}z^{j}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}{}^{k}F_{j,k}(u).

The function
g(z,w)
is derived in the proof of
[
13
, Lemma 3.2]
and is found by solving an algebraic equation. We note that (
9.3
) does not affect the line
\ell=\{(0,0,u)\}
, which is also the case for all upcoming transformations.

Step 3:

Under the assumption that the hypersurface is Levi nondegenerate, which means that
\widehat{V}_{Z\overline{Z}}\neq 0
, normalize
z\overline{z}{}^{k}F_{1,k}(u)=0
and
z^{j}\overline{z}F_{j,1}(u)=0
using

z^{*}=z+f(z,w),\hskip 20.00003ptw^{*}=w,

with
f(0,w)=0
,
f_{z}(0,w)=0
, so that

v=z\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}\mskip 2.0muF_{1,1}(u)+\sum_{j,k\geq 2}z^{j}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}{}^{k}F_{j,k}(u),

where
F_{1,1}(0)\neq 0
. The function
f(z,w)
satisfies an algebraic equation given in the proof of
[
13
, Lemma 3.3]
.

Step 4:

Normalize
F_{1,1}(u)=1
using a transformation of the form

z^{*}=C(w)\mskip 2.0muz,\hskip 20.00003ptw^{*}=w

(9.4)

so that the transformed power series is

v=z\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}+\sum_{j,k\geq 2}z^{j}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}{}^{k}F_{j,k}(u).

To do so, it suffices to take

C(u)=\sqrt{F_{1,1}(u)}

(9.5)

and then replace
u
by
w
to obtain the transformation (
9.4
).

Step 5:

Normalize

\displaystyle F_{2,2}(u)=0,

\displaystyle F_{3,2}(u)=\overline{F_{3,2}(u)}=0,

\displaystyle F_{3,3}(u)=0,

so that the
Chern–Moser normal form
is

v=z\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}+z^{4}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}{}^{2}F_{4,2}(u)+z^{2}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}{}^{4}F_{2,4}(u)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+k\geq 7\\
j,k\geq 2\end{subarray}}z^{j}\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}{}^{k}F_{j,k}(u).

(9.6)

The normalization
F_{3,2}(u)=0
imposes a differential constraint on the first component of the chain (
9.2
) given by a second order ordinary differential equation for
\overline{\psi}{}(u)
:

\psi_{uu}=Q(u,\psi,\overline{\psi}{},\psi_{u},\overline{\psi}{}_{u}).

The explicit formula for
Q
is not provided in
[
13
]
. For three-dimensional hypersurfaces, a Lie theoretic description of this equation is given in
[
45
]
.

The normalization
F_{22}(u)=0
is achieved using the transformation

z^{*}=\lambda(w)\mskip 2.0muz,\hskip 20.00003ptw^{*}=w,

(9.7)

such that when
w=u
is real, the restricted transformation satisfies

\lambda(u)\,\overline{\lambda(u)}=1
,
\lambda(0)=1
, and is a solution to the first order ordinary differential equation

\lambda_{u}=-\frac{\,{\rm i}\,}{2}F_{2,2}(u)\mskip 2.0mu\lambda.

The transformations (
9.4
) and (
9.7
) are slightly different. In light of (
9.5
), the function
C(w)
in (
9.4
) is real-valued or purely imaginary depending on whether
F_{1,1}(u)>0
or
F_{1,1}(u)<0
, while the function
\lambda(w)
in (
9.7
) is complex-valued.

Finally, the normalization
F_{3,3}(u)=0
is achieved via the transformation

z^{*}=z\sqrt{\varphi_{w}(w)},\hskip 20.00003ptw^{*}=\varphi(w),

with
\varphi(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi_{w}(0)>0
, and satisfies the third order ordinary differential equation

\varphi_{uuu}=\frac{3\mskip 2.0mu\varphi^{2}_{uu}}{2\mskip 2.0mu\varphi_{u}}-3\mskip 2.0muF_{3,3}(u)\mskip 2.0mu\varphi_{u}.

This provides constraints on the second component of the chain (
9.2
).

We now explain how this particular Chern–Moser construction can be formulated within our general framework. To make the connection evident, let us assume for the time being that the class one Cartan character of the involutive normal form determining equations is the only nonzero character, so

{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(1)}}\neq 0,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(2)}}=\cdots={\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(p)}}=0.

(9.8)

In this particular setting, the general solution depends only on functions of one variable, and the initial conditions (
8.26
) reduce to

u^{\beta}_{K}(0,\ldots,0)=f^{\beta}_{K},\hskip 20.00003ptu^{\gamma}_{I\setminus 1}(x^{1},0,\ldots,0)=f^{\gamma}_{I\setminus 1}(x^{1}).

(9.9)

Since the Taylor coefficients of the initial conditions (
9.9
) determine the cross-section
\mathcal{K}
, the left hand side of the equations (
9.9
) can be replaced by the cross-section functions (
7.3
) so that

C^{\beta}_{K}(0,\ldots,0)=f^{\beta}_{K}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptC^{\gamma}_{I\setminus 1}(x^{1},0\ldots,0)=f^{\gamma}_{I\setminus 1}(x^{1}).

(9.10)

We observe that the equations (
9.10
) are defined on the line
\ell=\{(x^{1},0,\ldots,0)\}\subset\mathcal{X}
. Then, a one-dimensional chain
\bm{\mathcal{C}}
is a regular curve in the section
S
with the property that there exists a pseudo-group transformation
\varphi^{-1}\in\mathcal{G}
mapping
\bm{\mathcal{C}}
to the curve
\varphi^{-1}(\bm{\mathcal{C}})=\bm{\ell}=(\ell,C(\ell))
contained in the normal form
s
, where
C(y)
is the cross-section function (
7.3
). In particular, we note that the projection of the chain onto the space of independent variables
\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}})\subset\mathcal{X}
is rectified to the line
\varphi^{-1}|_{\mathcal{X}}(\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}}))=\ell
. In other words,
\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}})=\varphi|_{\mathcal{X}}(\ell)
.

Thus, to find the chain
\bm{\mathcal{C}}=\varphi(\ell,C(\ell))
passing through
(X_{0},\widehat{U}(X_{0}))
, it suffices to find
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
such that

(\varphi|_{\mathcal{X}}(\ell),\widehat{U}(\varphi|_{\mathcal{X}}(\ell)))=\varphi(\ell,C(\ell)).

(9.11)

Setting
(X(x,u),U(x,u))=\varphi(x,u)
and
(\overline{X}{}|_{\bm{\ell}},\overline{U}{}|_{\bm{\ell}})=\varphi(\ell,C(\ell))=(X(\ell,C(\ell)),U(\ell,C(\ell))
, equation (
9.11
) reduces to solving

\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}|_{\bm{\ell}})=\overline{U}{}|_{\bm{\ell}}.

(9.12)

We note that (
9.12
) is the same equation as (
7.4
) but restricted to the curve
\bm{\ell}=(\ell,C(\ell))
. More explicitly, (
9.12
) is obtained by replacing
(x,u(x))
in the second equation of (
7.4
) by
(\ell,C(\ell))
to obtain

\widehat{U}(X(\ell,C(\ell))=U(\ell,C(\ell)).

(9.13)

With
\bm{\ell}
being one-dimensional, the equations (
9.13
) form a system of ordinary differential equations for the parametric reduced pseudo-group jets with initial value
(\overline{X}{}|_{\bm{\ell}}(0),\overline{U}{}|_{\bm{\ell}}(0))=(X_{0},\widehat{U}(X_{0}))
. We now show how this works with two examples.

Example 9.1
.

Consider the Lie pseudo-group

X=f(x),\hskip 20.00003ptY=y+b,\hskip 20.00003ptU=\frac{u}{f_{x}(x)},

(9.14)

acting on surfaces
u(x,y)
, where
f\in\mathcal{D}({\mathbb{R}})
and
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We assume
u\neq 0
in what follows, and similarly for
\widehat{U}
. Furthermore, we choose
\overline{Y}{}
,

\overline{X}{}_{x^{k}}
, with
k\geq 0
, as parametric reduced pseudo-group jets in the computations below.

The normal form determining equations of order one are

\overline{X}{}_{y}=\overline{Y}{}_{x}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{Y}{}_{y}=1,\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}_{x}=\frac{u}{\widehat{U}},\hskip 20.00003ptu_{y}=\frac{\widehat{U}_{Y}}{\widehat{U}}\,u.

(9.15)

These equations are involutive with indices and Cartan characters

{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{1}^{(1)}}=2,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{b}}{}_{1}^{(2)}}=3,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{1}^{(1)}}=1,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{1}^{(2)}}=0.

A moving frame for the pseudo-group (
9.14
) was constructed in
[
57
]
using the cross-section

\mathcal{K}=\{x=y=0,\;u=1,\;u_{x^{k}}=0,\;k\geq 1\},

which induces the initial conditions

X(0,0)=X_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptY(0,0)=Y_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptu(x,0)=1

for the system of partial differential equations (
9.15
) and corresponds to the normal form

u(x,y)=1+y\mskip 2.0muw(x,y),

(9.16)

where the Taylor coefficients of
w(x,y)
give the basic differential invariants expressed in terms of the jet coordinates of
\widehat{U}
. The cross-section function is
u(x,0)=C(x)=1
, and defines the line

\bm{\ell}=\{(x,0,1)\}\subset s

(9.17)

contained in the graph of the normal form (
9.16
).

A chain is a regular curve
\bm{\mathcal{C}}=\bigl\{\,{(\overline{X}{}(x),Y_{0},\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}(x),Y_{0}))}\,\bigr\}\subset S
contained in the submanifold that is rectified to the line (
9.17
) by a pseudo-group transformation (
9.14
). First, for the
y
-coordinate of
\bm{\ell}
to be sent to
Y_{0}
in the chain, a translation with
b=Y_{0}
is performed. On the other hand, the function
\overline{X}{}(x)
satisfies the chain determining equation (
9.13
), which yields the differential equation

\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}(x),Y_{0})=\overline{U}{}=\frac{1}{\overline{X}{}_{x}(x)}.

In other words,

\overline{X}{}_{x}(x)=\frac{1}{\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}(x),Y_{0})}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with the initial condition}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}(0)=X_{0}.

This is an ordinary differential equation for
\overline{X}{}(x)
, whose right hand side is analytic when the surface
\widehat{U}(X,Y)
is analytic, and hence defines an analytic normalizing transformation.

Example 9.2
.

Let us return to our running example, which consists of the Lie pseudo-group (
4.8
) with normal form (
7.7
) and cross-section power series (
7.8
).
Let us determine the chain corresponding to the two initial conditions

\displaystyle u(x,0)=c(x),

\displaystyle u_{y}(x,0)=d(x).

These provide the pair of equations

{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 30pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}(x),\overline{Y}{}(x,0))=c(x)+\frac{\overline{Y}{}_{x}(x,0)}{\overline{X}{}_{x}(0)},&.0pt$\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{Y}(\overline{X}{}(x),\overline{Y}{}(x,0))\overline{X}{}_{x}(x)=d(x)+\frac{\overline{X}{}_{xx}(x)}{\overline{X}{}_{x}(x)}\cr}}}.

Thus, the chain
\bm{\mathcal{C}}=\bigl\{\,{(\overline{X}{}(x),\overline{Y}{}(x,0),\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}(x),\overline{Y}{}(x,0))}\,\bigr\}
is obtained by solving a pair of ordinary differential equations

\displaystyle\overline{X}{}_{xx}(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{U}_{Y}(\overline{X}{}(x),\overline{Y}{}(x,0))\overline{X}{}_{x}^{2}(x)-d(x)\overline{X}{}_{x}(x),

(9.18)

\displaystyle\overline{Y}{}_{x}(x,0)

\displaystyle=\bigl[\,{\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}(x),\overline{Y}{}(x,0))-c(x)}\,\bigr]\overline{X}{}_{x}(x),

subject to the initial conditions

\overline{Y}{}(0,0)=Y_{0},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}(0,0)=X_{0},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{X}{}_{x}(0)=\overline{X}{}_{x}^{0}.

(9.19)

Again, analyticity of the surface
\widehat{U}(X,Y)
and of the cross-section function (
7.8
) implies analyticity of the right hand sides of the differential equations (
9.18
), and thus analyticity of the normalizing transformation. To obtain the quadratic term in
y
in the normal form series (
7.8
), we need to impose the algebraic constraint
\overline{X}{}_{x}^{0}=\sqrt{\widehat{U}_{YY}(X_{0},Y_{0})}
on the initial conditions. We note that the ordinary differential equations (
9.18
) for the inverse pseudo-group transformation had originally been derived in
[
57
, Example 32]
with
c(x)=d(x)=0
.

The preceding discussion focused on one-dimensional chains (curves), where the constraint on the Cartan characters (
9.8
) holds. In the more general situation, when there are one or more nonzero higher order Cartan characters, the appropriate analog of chains will include submanifolds of dimension
\geq 2
. For example, if the largest nonzero Cartan character is
{\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{c}}{}_{n}^{(k)}}
, then a
k
-dimensional chain
\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
is a submanifold in
S
that can be mapped to

\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{k}=(\mathcal{P}_{k},C(\mathcal{P}_{k})),

where
C(y)
is the cross-section function (
7.3
), and such that the projection
\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{k})\subset\mathcal{X}
is rectified to the particular
k
-dimensional coordinate subspace
\mathcal{P}_{k}=\{(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k},0,\ldots,0)\}
. The pseudo-group transformation rectifying the chain will satisfy a system of partial differential equations for the parametric reduced pseudo-group jets that are given by

\widehat{U}(\overline{X}{}|_{\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{k}})=\overline{U}{}|_{\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}.

Inside the
k
-dimensional chain
\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
there may be a sequence of lower dimensional chains
\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\subset\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\subset\cdots\subset\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{k-1}\subset\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
, with each projection
\pi(\bm{\mathcal{C}}_{j})
mapped to the
j
-dimensional coordinate subspace
\mathcal{P}_{j}=\{(x^{1},\ldots,x^{j},0,\ldots,0)\}
under a suitable pseudo-group transformation. The existence of these subchains will depend on the form of the initial values (
8.26
), which is ultimately determined by the Cartan characters,
[
66
, Proposition 8.2.10]
. We remark that such higher dimensional chains can be found in
[
17
]
, which introduces two-dimensional chains when studying normal forms for elliptic CR submanifolds in
\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

10
Additional Examples.

In this section we provide four more relatively simple examples illustrating the results of the paper. We conclude by showing how the convergence theorem of Chern and Moser,
[
13
]
, can be
deduced from our general theorem; this requires finding suitable coordinates that assure involutivity of the determining equations. In these examples, we will omit the bar notation over
X
and
Y
and the hat notation on
U
and its derivatives, which will unclutter the equations while hopefully not leading to any confusion now that the procedures and meanings are clear.

Example 10.1
.

In our running example, the pseudo-group considered only involved functions depending on one independent variable, namely
x
. In this example we
consider the pseudo-group

X=f(x),\hskip 20.00003ptY=g(y),\hskip 20.00003ptU=u+c,

where
f,g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
,
c\in\mathbb{R}
, and
x
,
y
are assumed to be independent variables so that we consider the action on surfaces
u=u(x,y)
. Thus, the first order reduced determining equations are

X_{y}=Y_{x}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptU_{x}=u_{x},\hskip 20.00003ptU_{y}=u_{y},

(10.1)

while the order two equations are

X_{xy}=X_{yy}=Y_{xx}=Y_{xy}=0,\hskip 10.00002ptU_{xx}=u_{xx},\hskip 10.00002ptU_{xy}=u_{xy},\hskip 10.00002ptU_{yy}=u_{yy}.

(10.2)

Using the ordering
x\prec y
, the indices for the order one equations (
10.1
) are
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}=2
and
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}=2
so that

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}=6\neq 7=\mathsf{r}_{2}.

Alternatively, the Cartan characters are
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(1)}}=1
,
{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(2)}}=1
and

{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(2)}}=3\neq 2=\mathsf{d}_{2}.

It follows that the equations (
10.1
) are not involutive. In fact, the reduced determining equations are not involutive at any order
n
. To see this, we observe that the order
n
determining equations for
Y
are

Y_{x^{n}}=Y_{x^{n-1}y}=\cdots=Y_{xy^{n-1}}=0,

which are all of class one with respect to our chosen ordering. From those equations it is not possible to obtain the equation
Y_{xy^{n}}=0
at order
n+1
since
y
is not a multiplicative variable. Changing the ordering to
y\prec x
would not resolve the issue because the same problem would now appear among the determining equations for
X
.
The conclusion is that the current coordinates are not
\delta
-regular.

As emphasized in Section
3
, we must therefore introduce new coordinates that are
\delta
-regular. This can be done, for example, by setting

x=t+s\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pty=t-s.

The pseudo-group then becomes

T+S=f(t+s),\hskip 20.00003ptT-S=g(t-s),\hskip 20.00003ptU=u+c

or

T=\frac{f(t+s)+g(t-s)}{2},\hskip 20.00003ptS=\frac{f(t+s)-g(t-s)}{2},\hskip 20.00003ptU=u+c.

Relabeling the variables and functions, we now consider the Lie pseudo-group

X=f(x+y)+g(x-y),\hskip 20.00003ptY=f(x+y)-g(x-y),\hskip 20.00003ptU=u+c.

(10.3)

The normal form determining equations can be obtained by recursively applying the total derivative operators

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{x}=X_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{X}+Y_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{Y},

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{y}=X_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{X}+Y_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{Y},

(10.4)

to the pseudo-group transformations (
10.3
) and eliminating the derivatives of the functions
f,g
from the resulting equations. At first order, this results in

\displaystyle X_{x}=f_{t}+g_{t},

\displaystyle X_{y}=f_{t}-g_{t},

\displaystyle Y_{x}=f_{t}-g_{t},

\displaystyle Y_{y}=f_{t}+g_{t},

\displaystyle X_{x}U_{X}+Y_{x}U_{Y}=u_{x},

\displaystyle X_{y}U_{X}+Y_{y}U_{Y}=u_{y},

(10.5)

where
f_{t},g_{t}
represent the first order derivatives of
f,g
. Provided
U_{X}^{2}-U_{Y}^{2}\neq 0
, we can eliminate
f_{t},g_{t}
to produce the first order normal form determining equations:

X_{x}=Y_{y}=\frac{u_{x}U_{X}-u_{y}U_{Y}}{U_{X}^{2}-U_{Y}^{2}},\hskip 20.00003ptX_{y}=Y_{x}=\frac{u_{y}U_{X}-u_{x}U_{Y}}{U_{X}^{2}-U_{Y}^{2}},

(10.6)

where we take
u_{x},u_{y}
to be the parametric derivatives. This is consistent with the moving frame construction, but not with the theory of involutivity, which would require solving for
u_{y}
, assuming the ordering
x\prec y
. In accordance with the discussion in Section
8
, this is a second example illustrating the discrepancy between the two theories at low order.

The normal form determining equations of order two can be obtained by differentiating (
10.6
) using the total differential operators (
10.4
) — or, alternatively applying (
10.4
) to (
10.5
) and eliminating the first and second derivatives of
f,g
— which produces

X_{xy}=Y_{xx},\hskip 20.00003ptX_{yy}=X_{xx},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{xy}=X_{xx},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{yy}=Y_{xx},

(10.7)

along with

\displaystyle\hfil X_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 97.5pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr(u_{xx}-U_{XX}X_{x}^{2}-2\mskip 2.0muU_{XY}X_{x}Y_{x}-U_{YY}Y_{x}^{2})U_{X}\cr\hskip 97.5pt{}-(u_{xy}-U_{XX}X_{x}Y_{x}-U_{XY}(X_{x}^{2}+Y_{x}^{2})-U_{YY}X_{x}Y_{x})U_{Y}\cr}}}}{U_{X}^{2}-U_{Y}^{2}},

\displaystyle\hfil Y_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle{\vbox{\halign{$\displaystyle#\hfil$&&\hskip 97.5pt$\displaystyle#\hfil$\cr(u_{xy}-U_{XX}X_{x}Y_{x}-U_{XY}(X_{x}^{2}+Y_{x}^{2})-U_{YY}X_{x}Y_{x})U_{X}\cr\hskip 97.5pt{}-(u_{xx}-U_{XX}X_{x}^{2}-2\mskip 2.0muU_{XY}X_{x}Y_{x}-U_{YY}Y_{x}^{2})U_{Y}\cr}}}}{U_{X}^{2}-U_{Y}^{2}},

(10.8)

and

u_{yy}=u_{xx}-\frac{(u_{x}^{2}-u_{y}^{2})\mskip 2.0mu(U_{XX}-U_{YY})}{U_{X}^{2}-U_{Y}^{2}}.

(10.9)

Note that to place (
10.8
) in the proper reduced Cartan normal form, we should replace
X_{x},X_{y},Y_{x},Y_{y}
by their formulas from (
10.6
), although the resulting expressions are a bit unwieldy. We also note that the second order parametric derivatives are
u_{xx},u_{xy}
.

We can easily verify that the order two normal form determining equations are involutive. Indeed, the indices and Cartan characters
10
10

10

As above, we only need to compute one of these sets to verify involutivity.
are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}=4,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}=3,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}=2,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}=0,

and they satisfy the algebraic involutivity tests

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}=10=\mathsf{r}_{3}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}=2=\mathsf{d}_{3}.

Since there are no integrability conditions, the equations are involutive.

On the space

V^{(\infty)}=\{U_{X}^{2}\neq U_{Y}^{2}\}\subset{\rm J}{}^{\infty}

of regular jets, the prolonged action becomes free at order one
11
11

11

Every Lie pseudo-group is trivially free at order
n=0
. Freeness is only of interest when
n\geq 1
,
[
57
]
.
, and a cross-section is given by

\mathcal{K}=\{x=y=0,\ \ u_{x^{k}}=c_{k},\ \ u_{yx^{k}}=d_{k}\;|\;k\geq 0,\ \ c_{1}^{2}-d_{0}^{2}\neq 0\}.

(10.10)

The corresponding cross-section function is

C(x,y)=c(x)+y\,d(x)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptc_{x}^{2}(0)-d^{2}(0)\neq 0,

(10.11)

and the normal form is

u(x,y)=c(x)+y\,d(x)+y^{2}\mskip 2.0muw(x,y).

(10.12)

In the simplest case, we can take
c(x)=x
and
d(x)=0
. Observe that the initial conditions (
10.11
) depend on functions of the same variable
x
, which would not be the case if we were to write the system in the original
\delta
irregular coordinates.

According to the general theory, since the action becomes free at order one, the involutivity of the order two normal form determining equations (
10.7
), (
10.8
), (
10.9
) guarantees the convergence of the normal form (
10.12
) provided well-posed analytic initial conditions are provided and the target function
U(X,Y)
is analytic. On the other hand, the equations (
10.6
) provide algebraic constraints among the order one jets at the origin. The desired initial conditions are given by

\displaystyle X(0,0)=X_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptY(0,0)=Y_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptu(0,0)=C(0,0)=c_{0},

\displaystyle u_{x}(x,0)=C(x,0)=c_{x}(x),\hskip 20.00003ptu_{y}(x,0)=C_{y}(x,0)=d(x),

\displaystyle c_{x}^{2}(0)-d^{2}(0)\neq 0,

where
c(x)
,
d(x)
are analytic functions. This shows that (
10.10
) is a well-posed cross-section satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
8.17
. Indeed the cross-section is of minimal order with the set of defining indices of order
>1
admitting the Rees decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>1}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(k+1,0),\;(k,1)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(k+1,0),\;(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(k+1,0),\;(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}{\{(k+1,0),\;(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(k+1,0)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(k+1,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(k+1,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}{\{(k+1,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}\;\biguplus\;\mathchoice{\left\{\,(k,1)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}{\{(k,1)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}=\mathcal{C}(2,0)\;\biguplus\;\mathcal{C}(1,1).

Example 10.2
.

In the examples considered thus far, the Lie pseudo-group actions were all, in the chosen system of coordinates, quasi-horizontal as defined in
[
3
]
. This property is not necessary for the results of this paper to be valid, and we illustrate this fact by considering the Lie pseudo-group

X=x+a,\hskip 20.00003ptY=y+b,\hskip 20.00003ptU=f(u),

(10.13)

where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
. Of course, the pseudo-group (
10.13
) can be transformed into a quasi-horizontal action via the hodograph transformation
(x,y,u)\to(u,y,x)
, but we will not make this transformation here.

Provided
U_{X}\neq 0
, the normal form determining equations of order
1
are

X_{x}=Y_{y}=1,\hskip 20.00003ptX_{y}=Y_{x}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptu_{y}=\frac{u_{x}U_{Y}}{U_{X}},

(10.14)

while at order
2
we have

\displaystyle X_{xx}=X_{xy}=X_{yy}=Y_{xx}=Y_{xy}=Y_{yy}=0,

\displaystyle u_{xy}=\frac{u_{x}U_{XY}+u_{xx}U_{Y}}{U_{X}}-\frac{u_{x}U_{Y}U_{XX}}{U_{X}^{2}},\hskip 20.00003ptu_{yy}=\frac{u_{x}U_{YY}}{U_{X}}+\frac{u_{xx}U_{Y}^{2}}{U_{X}^{2}}-\frac{u_{x}U_{Y}^{2}U_{XX}}{U_{X}^{3}}.

(10.15)

The indices and Cartan characters for the order one determining equations (
10.14
) are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}=2,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}=3,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(1)}}=1,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(2)}}=0

so that the involutivity condition

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}=8=\mathsf{r}_{2},\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox{\rm or, equivalently,}\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(2)}}=1=\mathsf{d}_{2}

is satisfied. Since there are no integrability conditions, the order one determining equations (
10.14
) are involutive.

The pseudo-group action becomes free at order one. A well-posed cross-section is given by

\mathcal{K}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k}}=c_{k}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 0\text{ and }c_{1}\neq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k}}=c_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 0\text{ and }c_{1}\neq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k}}=c_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 0\text{ and }c_{1}\neq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k}}=c_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 0\text{ and }c_{1}\neq 0\hbox{}\}},

with the set of defining indices of order
>1
admitting the Rees decomposition

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>1}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(k,0)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(k,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(k,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}{\{(k,0)\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 2\hbox{}\}}=\mathcal{C}(2,0).

The corresponding cross-section function is

C(x)=c(x)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptc_{x}(0)\neq 0,

and the normal form is given by

u(x,y)=c(x)+y\,w(x,y).

(10.16)

In the simplest case, we could have taken
c(x)=x
.

The action being free at order one, the general theory dictates that, assuming analyticity of the function
U(X,Y)
, analyticity of the normal form (
10.16
) will follow from the involutivity of the order two normal form determining equations (
10.15
) along with the equations (
10.14
) providing algebraic constraints among the first order jets at the origin. Formally well-posed initial conditions are given by

X(0,0)=X_{0},\hskip 10.00002ptY(0,0)=Y_{0},\hskip 10.00002ptu(0,0)=c(0),\hskip 20.00003ptu_{x}(x,0)=c_{x}(x),

with
c_{x}(0)\neq 0
.

Example 10.3
.

In the previous examples, the Lie pseudo-group actions considered only involved local diffeomorphisms of the real line. We now examine the pseudo-group

X=x+a,\hskip 20.00003ptY=g(x,y),\hskip 20.00003ptZ=z+b,\hskip 20.00003ptU=u,

(10.17)

where
g(x,y)
is an analytic function that depends on two variables with
g_{y}(x,y)\neq 0
, while
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. In this example, we assume that
u=u(x,y,z)
is a function of three variables, i.e., we consider the action on three-dimensional submanifolds.

We can obtain the normal form determining equations by applying the total derivative operators

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{x}=\mathrm{D}_{X}+Y_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{Y},

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{y}=Y_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{Y},

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{z}=\mathrm{D}_{Z},

(10.18)

to (
10.17
). Assuming
U_{Y}\neq 0
, we can rewrite these equations in the form

\displaystyle X_{x}=1,

\displaystyle X_{y}=X_{z}=0,

\displaystyle Y_{x}=\frac{u_{x}-U_{X}}{U_{Y}},

\displaystyle Y_{y}=\frac{u_{y}}{U_{Y}},

\displaystyle Y_{z}=Z_{x}=Z_{y}=0,

\displaystyle Z_{z}=1,

\displaystyle u_{z}=U_{Z},

(10.19)

where the parametric derivatives are
u_{x},u_{y}
. We note that this is compatible with both the theory of moving frames and involutivity. The second order normal form determining equations can be obtained by applying the differential operators (
10.18
) to the first order equations (
10.19
) giving

\displaystyle\hfil X_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=X_{xy}=X_{yy}=X_{xz}=X_{yz}=X_{zz}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptY_{xz}=Y_{yz}=Y_{zz}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil Z_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=Z_{xy}=Z_{yy}=Z_{xz}=Z_{yz}=Z_{zz}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil Y_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{xx}-U_{XX}}{U_{Y}}-\frac{2\mskip 2.0muU_{XY}(u_{x}-U_{X})}{U_{Y}^{2}}-\frac{U_{YY}(u_{x}-U_{X})^{2}}{U_{Y}^{3}},

\displaystyle\hfil Y_{xy}\;

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{xy}}{U_{Y}}-\frac{U_{XY}u_{y}}{U_{Y}^{2}}-\frac{U_{YY}u_{y}(u_{x}-U_{X})}{U_{Y}^{3}},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{yy}=\frac{u_{yy}}{U_{Y}}-\frac{U_{YY}u_{y}^{2}}{U_{Y}^{3}},

\displaystyle\hfil u_{xz}\;

\displaystyle=U_{XZ}+\frac{U_{YZ}(u_{x}-U_{X})}{U_{Y}},\hskip 20.00003ptu_{yz}=\frac{U_{YZ}u_{y}}{U_{Y}},\hskip 20.00003ptu_{zz}=U_{ZZ},

(10.20)

with parametric derivatives are
u_{xx},u_{xy},u_{yy}
, and similarly for the higher order versions.
The indices and Cartan characters for the order one normal form determining equations (
10.19
) are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}=3,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}=3,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(3)}}=4,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(1)}}=1,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(2)}}=1,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(3)}}=0,

which satisfy the involutivity condition

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(3)}}=21=\mathsf{r}_{2}\mskip 2.0mu\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or, equivalently,}\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(3)}}=3=\mathsf{d}_{2}.

Since there are no integrability constraints, the order one normal form determining equations are involutive.

The pseudo-group action becomes free at order one and a well-posed cross-section is given by

\mathcal{K}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k},\;u_{x^{j}y^{k+1}}=d_{j,k}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;j,k\geq 0\text{ and }d_{0,0}\neq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k},\;u_{x^{j}y^{k+1}}=d_{j,k}\hbox{}\,|\,j,k\geq 0\text{ and }d_{0,0}\neq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k},\;u_{x^{j}y^{k+1}}=d_{j,k}\hbox{}\,|\,j,k\geq 0\text{ and }d_{0,0}\neq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{x=y=0,\;u_{x^{k+1}}=c_{k},\;u_{x^{j}y^{k+1}}=d_{j,k}\hbox{}\,|\,j,k\geq 0\text{ and }d_{0,0}\neq 0\hbox{}\}}

with the defining indices of order
>1
admitting the Rees decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>1}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(i+2,0),\;(j,k+1)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;i\geq 0,\;j+k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(i+2,0),\;(j,k+1)\hbox{}\,|\,i\geq 0,\;j+k\geq 1\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(i+2,0),\;(j,k+1)\hbox{}\,|\,i\geq 0,\;j+k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}{\{(i+2,0),\;(j,k+1)\hbox{}\,|\,i\geq 0,\;j+k\geq 1\hbox{}\}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{\,(i+2,0)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;i\geq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(i+2,0)\hbox{}\,|\,i\geq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(i+2,0)\hbox{}\,|\,i\geq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{(i+2,0)\hbox{}\,|\,i\geq 0\hbox{}\}}\;\biguplus\;\mathchoice{\left\{\,(j+1,1)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;j\geq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(j+1,1)\hbox{}\,|\,j\geq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(j+1,1)\hbox{}\,|\,j\geq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{(j+1,1)\hbox{}\,|\,j\geq 0\hbox{}\}}\;\biguplus\;\mathchoice{\left\{\,(j,k+2)\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;j,k\geq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,(j,k+2)\hbox{}\,|\,j,k\geq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{(j,k+2)\hbox{}\,|\,j,k\geq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{(j,k+2)\hbox{}\,|\,j,k\geq 0\hbox{}\}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}(2,0)\;\biguplus\;\mathcal{C}(1,1)\;\biguplus\;\mathcal{C}(0,2).

The corresponding cross-section function
C(x,y)
satisfies the constraints

C(0,0)=0,\hskip 20.00003ptC_{x}(x,0)=c(x),\hskip 20.00003ptC_{y}(x,y)=d(x,y)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002ptC_{y}(0,0)=d(0,0)\neq 0.

In the simplest case, we could let
C(x,y)=y
.
In general, the normal form is given by

u(x,y,z)=U_{0}+C(x,y)+z\,w(x,y,z),

(10.21)

where
U_{0}=U(X_{0},Y_{0},Z_{0})
is a constant, fixed by the prescribed submanifold.

Since the prolonged action becomes free at order one, the convergence of the normal form (
10.21
) follows from the involutivity of the order two normal form determining equations (
10.20
), with the equations (
10.19
) providing algebraic constraints on the order one jets at the origin. Since the pseudo-group action (
10.17
) is intransitive, we also have the order zero normal form determining equation
u=U
, which needs to be evaluated at the origin. Well-posed initial conditions are given by

\displaystyle X(0,0,0)=X_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptY(0,0,0)=Y_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptZ(0,0,0)=Z_{0},

\displaystyle u(0,0,0)=U_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptu_{x}(x,0,0)=C_{x}(x,0)=c(x),\hskip 20.00003ptu_{y}(x,y,0)=C_{y}(x,y)=d(x,y).

Example 10.4
.

As our next example, we consider the Lie pseudo-group

X=x+a,\hskip 20.00003ptY=y+b,\hskip 20.00003ptZ=z+f(x,y),\hskip 20.00003ptU=u+g(x,y),

(10.22)

where
f,g
satisfy the Cauchy–Riemann equations

f_{x}=g_{y},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{y}=-\mskip 2.0mug_{x}.

(10.23)

As in Example
10.3
, we obtain the normal form determining equations by recursively applying the total differential operators

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{x}=\mathrm{D}_{X}+Z_{x}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{Z},

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{y}=\mathrm{D}_{Y}+Z_{y}\mskip 2.0mu\mathrm{D}_{Z},

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{z}=\mathrm{D}_{Z},

(10.24)

to the pseudo-group transformations (
10.22
). At first order, we have

\displaystyle X_{x}=1,

\displaystyle X_{y}=X_{z}=0,

\displaystyle Y_{y}=1,

\displaystyle Y_{x}=Y_{z}=0,

\displaystyle Z_{z}=1,

(10.25)

along with

\displaystyle Z_{x}=f_{x},

\displaystyle Z_{y}=f_{y},

\displaystyle U_{X}+Z_{x}U_{Z}=u_{x}+g_{x},

\displaystyle U_{Y}+Z_{y}U_{Z}=u_{y}+g_{y},

\displaystyle U_{Z}=u_{z}.

(10.26)

Eliminating the derivatives of
f,g
from the latter equations using (
10.23
) produces

\displaystyle Z_{x}=\frac{U_{Z}(u_{x}-U_{X})-(u_{y}-U_{Y})}{1+U_{Z}^{2}},

\displaystyle Z_{y}=\frac{U_{Z}(u_{y}-U_{Y})+u_{x}-U_{X}}{1+U_{Z}^{2}},

\displaystyle u_{z}=U_{Z},

(10.27)

where the parametric derivatives are
u_{x},u_{y}
. As in Example
10.1
, this is compatible with the moving frame construction but not with involutivity, which would require solving for
u_{y}
in the first equation of (
10.27
), assuming the ordering
x\prec y\prec z
. The second order normal form determining equations can be obtained by using (
10.24
) to differentiate (
10.25
), (
10.27
). We find

\displaystyle\hfil X_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=X_{xy}=X_{yy}=X_{xz}=X_{yz}=X_{zz}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil Y_{xx}\;
