\displaystyle=Y_{xy}=Y_{yy}=Y_{xz}=Y_{yz}=Y_{zz}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptZ_{xz}=Z_{yz}=Z_{zz}=0,

\displaystyle\hfil Z_{xx}\;

\displaystyle=-\mskip 2.0muZ_{yy}=[\mskip 2.0mu-u_{xy}+U_{XY}+(u_{xx}-U_{XX})\mskip 2.0muU_{Z}+(U_{YZ}-2U_{XZ}U_{Z})Z_{x}+U_{XZ}Z_{y}{}

\displaystyle\hskip 210.0pt{}-U_{Z}U_{ZZ}Z_{x}^{2}+U_{ZZ}Z_{x}Z_{y}\mskip 2.0mu]/({1+U_{Z}^{2}}),

\displaystyle\hfil Z_{xy}\;

\displaystyle=[\mskip 2.0muu_{xx}-U_{XX}+(u_{xy}-U_{XY})\mskip 2.0muU_{Z}-(2\mskip 2.0muU_{XZ}+U_{YZ}U_{Z})Z_{x}-U_{XZ}U_{Z}Z_{y}{}

\displaystyle\hskip 210.0pt{}-U_{ZZ}Z_{x}^{2}-U_{Z}U_{ZZ}Z_{x}Z_{y}\mskip 2.0mu]/({1+U_{Z}^{2}}),

\displaystyle\hfil u_{yy}\;

\displaystyle=-\mskip 2.0muu_{xx}+U_{XX}+U_{YY}+U_{ZZ}(Z_{x}^{2}+Z_{y}^{2})+2\mskip 2.0muU_{XZ}Z_{x}+2\mskip 2.0muU_{YZ}Z_{y},

\displaystyle\hfil u_{xz}\;

\displaystyle=U_{XZ}+U_{ZZ}Z_{x},\hskip 20.00003ptu_{yz}=U_{YZ}+U_{ZZ}Z_{y},\hskip 20.00003ptu_{zz}=U_{ZZ}.

(10.28)

To write the equations in reduced Cartan normal form, one should replace
Z_{x},Z_{y}
by their expressions in (
10.27
) to express the right hand sides in terms of only the parametric derivatives
u_{x},u_{y},u_{xx},u_{xy}
; however, the resulting formulas are too unwieldy to display.

The indices and Cartan characters for the order two normal form determining equations (
10.28
) are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}=10,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}=8,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(3)}}=4,\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}=2,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(3)}}=0.

Omitting the computational details, the only third order parametric derivatives are
u_{xxx}
and
u_{xxy}
, and hence

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{2}^{(3)}}=38=\mathsf{r}_{3}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or, equivalently,}\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(1)}}+2\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(2)}}+3\mskip 2.0mu{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}^{(3)}}=2=\mathsf{d}_{3}.

Since there are no integrability conditions, the order two normal form equations are involutive.

The prolonged pseudo-group action becomes free at order one and a well-posed cross-section is given by

\mathcal{K}=\mathchoice{\left\{\,x=y=z=0,\ \ u_{x^{k}}=c_{k},\ \ u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\;\right|\left.\;k\geq 0\hbox{}\,\right\}}{\{\,x=y=z=0,\ \ u_{x^{k}}=c_{k},\ \ u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 0\hbox{}\,\}}{\{x=y=z=0,\ \ u_{x^{k}}=c_{k},\ \ u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 0\hbox{}\}}{\{x=y=z=0,\ \ u_{x^{k}}=c_{k},\ \ u_{x^{k}y}=d_{k}\hbox{}\,|\,k\geq 0\hbox{}\}}

so that the set of defining indices of order
>1
admits the Rees decomposition

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}^{>1}=\mathcal{C}(2,0,0)\;\biguplus\;\mathcal{C}(1,1,0).

The cross-section function is given by

C(x,y)=c(x)+y\mskip 2.0mud(x)

and the normal form is

u(x,y,z)=c(x)+y\mskip 2.0mud(x)+y^{2}\,v(x)+z\,w(x,y,z).

The convergence of the normal form follows from the involutivity of the order two normal form determining equations (
10.28
), combined with the algebraic constraints obtained by evaluating the order one equations (
10.25
), (
10.27
) at the origin. Formally well-posed initial conditions are given by

\displaystyle X(0)=X_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptY(0)=Y_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptZ(0)=Z_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptu(0,0,0)=C(0,0)=c_{0},

\displaystyle u_{x}(x,0,0)=C_{x}(x,0)=c_{x}(x),\hskip 20.00003ptu_{y}(x,0,0)=C_{y}(x,0)=d(x).

Example 10.5
.

In
[
60
]
we revisited the Chern–Moser normal form problem,
[
13
]
, for nondegenerate real hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
under the action of the pseudo-group of holomorphic transformations, obtaining five inequivalent classes of normal forms termed locally umbilic, non-umbilic, generic, circular, and semi-circular. The convergence of these normal forms relied on results from
[
13
]
. We now use Theorems
8.17
and
8.21
to give an alternative argument.

Let
z=x+\,{\rm i}\,y
,
w=u+\,{\rm i}\,v
be local coordinates on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Accordingly, the pseudo-group of holomorphic transformations
(z,w)\mapsto(Z(z,w),W(z,w))
of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, with
W=U+\,{\rm i}\,V
, is determined by the differential equations

Z_{\overline{z}}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptZ_{v}=\,{\rm i}\,Z_{u},\hskip 20.00003ptV_{\overline{z}}=\,{\rm i}\,U_{\overline{z}},\hskip 20.00003ptV_{u}=-U_{v},\hskip 20.00003ptV_{v}=U_{u}.

(10.29)

We consider real hypersurfaces
S\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
that are locally parametrized as the graph of a real-valued function

v=v(z,\overline{z},u).

(10.30)

A partial cross-section to the prolonged action was found in
[
60
, eq. (3.14)]
and is given by

\{v_{z\overline{z}}=1,\,z=\overline{z}=u=v=v_{z^{k}u^{\ell}}=v_{\overline{z}^{k}u^{\ell}}=v_{z\overline{z}u^{\ell+1}}=v_{z^{k+2}\overline{z}u^{\ell}}\\
=v_{z\overline{z}^{k+2}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{2}\overline{z}^{2}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{3}\overline{z}^{2}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{2}\overline{z}^{3}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{3}\overline{z}^{3}u^{\ell}}=0\,|\,k,\ell\geq 0\}.

(10.31)

Depending on the class of the normal form, only a finite number of normalizations must be added to (
10.31
) to obtain a complete cross-section. These normalizations do not affect the convergence argument, and we therefore work with the partial cross-section (
10.31
).

The normal form for locally umbilic hypersurfaces is given by the Heisenberg sphere
v=z\mskip 2.0mu\overline{z}
, which is obviously analytic. We thus focus on the remaining four classes of normal forms. Since the equations

v_{\overline{z}^{k}u^{\ell}}=v_{z\overline{z}^{k+2}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{2}\overline{z}^{3}u^{\ell}}=0

can be obtained by conjugating
v_{z^{k}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{k+2}\overline{z}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{3}\overline{z}^{2}u^{\ell}}=0
, they can be omitted from (
10.31
). No information is lost as, for example, the pseudo-group normalization originating from the normalization
v_{\overline{z}^{k}u^{\ell}}=0
is recovered by taking the conjugate of the pseudo-group normalization obtained by solving
v_{z^{k}u^{\ell}}=0
. Said differently, the normalization of a Taylor coefficient of the real-valued function (
10.30
) induces a normalization of its conjugated Taylor coefficient. We thus focus on the reduced partial cross-section

\mathcal{K}=\big\{\,v_{z\overline{z}}=1,\;z=u=v_{z^{k}u^{\ell}}=v_{z\overline{z}u^{\ell+1}}=v_{z^{k+2}\overline{z}u^{\ell}}\\
=v_{z^{2}\overline{z}^{2}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{3}\overline{z}^{2}u^{\ell}}=v_{z^{3}\overline{z}^{3}u^{\ell}}=0\;|\;k,\ell\geq 0\,\big\}.

(10.32)

Since the given coordinates turn out to be
\delta
irregular, as in Example
10.1
, we need to make a change of variables in order for the pseudo-group determining equations (
10.29
) to become involutive. Reverting back to complex variables, let

u=\frac{w+\overline{w}}{2},\hskip 20.00003ptv=\frac{w-\overline{w}}{2\,{\rm i}\,}.

(10.33)

The determining equations of the pseudo-group then become

Z_{\overline{z}}=Z_{\overline{w}}=W_{\overline{z}}=W_{\overline{w}}=0.

(10.34)

Introducing the ordering
w\prec z\prec\overline{z}\prec\overline{w}
, the indices and Cartan characters of (
10.34
) are

{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(3)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(4)}}=2\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(1)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(2)}}=2,\hskip 10.00002pt{\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(3)}}={\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}^{(4)}}=0.

Since the second order determining equations are

\displaystyle Z_{z\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=Z_{w\overline{z}}=Z_{\overline{z}\overline{z}}=Z_{\overline{z}\overline{w}}=Z_{z\overline{w}}=Z_{w\overline{w}}=Z_{\overline{w}\overline{w}}=0,

\displaystyle W_{z\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=W_{w\overline{z}}=W_{\overline{z}\overline{z}}=W_{\overline{z}\overline{w}}=Z_{z\overline{w}}=W_{w\overline{w}}=W_{\overline{w}\overline{w}}=0,

the involutivity test
{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(1)}}+2{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(2)}}+3{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(3)}}+4{\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{1}^{(4)}}=14=\mathsf{r}_{2}
is satisfied, and there are clearly no integrability conditions. Therefore, the determining equations (
10.34
) are involutive.

Substituting the change of variables (
10.33
) into the hypersurface defining equation (
10.30
) and solving for
\overline{w}
using the Implicit Function Theorem, we obtain the complex defining equation
12
12

12

Kossovskiy and Zaitsev also used the complex defining equation (
10.35
) in their convergence argument; see the acknowledgments in their paper
[
39
]
.

\overline{w}=\overline{w}(z,\overline{z},w)

(10.35)

of the hypersurface
S
. Thus, in the new coordinates, the jet variables are
\overline{w}_{z^{j}\overline{z}{}^{k}w^{\ell}}
with
j,k,\ell\geq 0
. To find the cross-section in these new jet variables, we substitute the real and complex defining equations (
10.30
), (
10.35
) into the second equation of (
10.33
) to obtain the relationship

\overline{w}(z,\overline{z},w)=w-2\,{\rm i}\,v\bigg(z,\overline{z},\frac{w+\overline{w}(z,\overline{z},w)}{2}\bigg).

(10.36)

Implicitly differentiating (
10.36
) produces the expressions for the new jet coordinates
\overline{w}_{J}
in terms of the original ones
v_{K}
. For example, at order one, we have

\displaystyle\overline{w}_{z}=-2\,{\rm i}\,v_{z}-\,{\rm i}\,v_{u}\overline{w}_{z},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{w}_{\overline{z}}=-2\,{\rm i}\,v_{\overline{z}}-\,{\rm i}\,v_{u}\overline{w}_{\overline{z}},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{w}_{w}=1-\,{\rm i}\,v_{u}(1+\overline{w}_{w}).

These equations can be solved for
\overline{w}_{z},\overline{w}_{\overline{z}},\overline{w}_{w}
and the result will depend on whether
v_{u}
is zero or not. For orders
\geq 2
, one finds, using induction, that

\overline{w}_{z^{j}\overline{z}{}^{k}w^{\ell}}=-2\,{\rm i}\,v_{z^{j}\overline{z}{}^{k}u^{\ell}}+\mathcal{S}_{j,k,\ell}(\overline{w}_{J},v_{K}),

(10.37)

where
\mathcal{S}_{j,k,\ell}
is a polynomial involving
\overline{w}_{J}
, with
|J|\leq j+k+\ell
, and
v_{K}=v_{z^{\alpha}\overline{z}{}^{\beta}u^{\gamma}}
, with
\alpha\leq j
,
\beta\leq k
,
\gamma\geq 1
and
|K|\leq j+k+\ell
. Moreover,

\mathcal{S}_{j,k,\ell}(\overline{w}_{J},0)=0.

Using (
10.37
) and induction, the partial cross-section in the new complex jet coordinates is

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}=\big\{\,\overline{w}_{w}=1,\;\overline{w}_{z\overline{z}}=-2\,{\rm i}\,,\;z=w=\overline{w}_{z^{k}w^{\ell}}=\overline{w}_{z\overline{z}w^{\ell+1}}=\overline{w}_{z^{k+2}\overline{z}w^{\ell}}\\
=\overline{w}_{z^{2}\overline{z}^{2}w^{\ell}}=\overline{w}_{z^{3}\overline{z}^{2}w^{\ell}}=\overline{w}_{z^{3}\overline{z}^{3}w^{\ell}}=0\;|\;k,\ell\geq 0\,\big\}.

As shown in
[
60
, Section 4]
, the prolonged action of the holomorphic pseudo-group becomes free at some order
n_{0}\geq 7
for generic, non-umbilic, and semi-circular hypersurfaces. Circular hypersurfaces retain a one-dimensional isotropy group, but the convergence argument remains valid at some order
n_{0}\geq 8
. In this case, the isotropy pseudo-group parameter is added to the order zero jet
\overline{X}{}
on the right hand side of the normal form determining equations (
8.24
), (
8.25
), and its value at the origin is an extra initial condition included on the last line of (
8.27
). In all cases, it is possible to construct a minimal cross-section and at the appropriate order
n_{0}
, one observes that
\mathcal{I}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}^{>n_{0}}
admits the following Rees decomposition with respect to the ordering
w\prec z\prec\overline{z}

\biguplus_{j=0}^{n_{0}+1}\mathcal{C}(n_{0}+1-j,j,0)\biguplus_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\mathcal{C}(n_{0}-j,j,1)\biguplus\mathcal{C}(n_{0}-3,2,2)\biguplus\mathcal{C}(n_{0}-4,3,2)\biguplus\mathcal{C}(n_{0}-5,3,3).

By Theorem
8.17
, the cross-section is well-posed and thus Theorem
8.21
implies that the normal form of a nondegenerate analytic hypersurface converges, thus reproducing Chern and Moser’s celebrated convergence result.

11
Final Comments.

In this paper, we have proven a fundamental result establishing the convergence of normal form power series for suitably regular submanifolds under a large class of Lie pseudo-group actions, which includes, in particular, all those for which the equivariant moving frame methods developed in
[
57
,
58
]
can be applied. To do so, we introduced the normal form determining equations (
7.13
), whose solution includes the normal form, and proved their involutivity when the submanifold is reducible. In Section
8
, we showed that, beyond the order of freeness, the involutivity of the normal form determining equations is compatible with the moving frame construction, and that a well-posed cross-section provides suitable analytic initial conditions. The convergence of the normal form is then guaranteed by an application of the Cartan–Kähler Theorem.

The results of the paper have been obtained under the assumption that the prolonged pseudo-group action eventually acts freely, which is a necessary requirement for the construction of a moving frame. That said, there are many circumstances where the prolonged pseudo-group action never becomes free, in which case the geometric problem admits a non-trivial isotropy groups. In these situations one can construct a partial moving frame,
[
54
,
68
]
. As indicated in Example
10.5
, if the isotropy group is finite-dimensional, then the constructions and results of the paper can be adapted to encompass this setting. In this case, the isotropy pseudo-group jet coordinates are parameters that can be added to the right hand side of the normal form determining equations (
8.24
), (
8.25
). At a sufficiently high order, the isotropy pseudo-group jet coordinates do not influence the involutivity of the normal form determining equations. These parametric variables can take any value and those are appended to the initial conditions (
8.27
). Extending the result of the paper to Lie pseudo-groups that do not eventually act freely, and to singular submanifolds that admit infinite-dimensional isotropy pseudo-groups will be the subject of future research.

We anticipate that our general convergence result will find a wide range of applications in the construction of normal forms. This include, for example, the investigation of Bishop surfaces in CR geometry,
[
29
]
, the construction of Poincaré–Dulac normal forms,
[
22
,
35
]
, as well as normal forms in control theory, dynamical systems, partial differential equations, and so on.
